A Minimal Formulation of Session Types: The Sessions of Trios in Concert
Alen Arslanagić, Jorge A. Pérez, Dan Frumin

Abstract
Session types are a type-based approach to the verification of message-passing programs. They specify communication structures essential to enforcing program correctness; by relying on sequencing constructs, a session type can precisely describe the intended order of communication actions through a channel. In this paper we study a fragment of session types that makes a very limited use of sequencing; we call it minimal session types. In the context of a core process calculus with sessions and higher-order concurrency, we establish two technical results. First, we prove that every process P P typable with standard session types can be compiled down into a process 𝒟 ⁡ ( P ) {\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P})} typable with minimal session types. Second, we prove that P P and 𝒟 ⁡ ( P ) {\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P})} are behaviorally equivalent. These results show that having sequencing in both processes and session types is convenient, but that only sequencing in processes is truly indispensable, as it can correctly codify sequencing in types. Our developments draw inspiration from work by Parrow on behavior-preserving decompositions of untyped processes using trios , i.e., processes with exactly three nested prefixes. By casting Parrow’s approach in the realm of typed processes, our developments reveal a conceptually simple formulation of session types, supported by static and dynamic correctness results.



1
Introduction

Session types are a type-based approach to the verification of message-passing programs.
A session type specifies what messages should be exchanged through a channel and in what order.
This makes session types a useful tool to enforce safety and liveness properties related to communication correctness.
Originated in the realm of concurrency theory—and using the
\pi
-calculus as specification language—session types have had a significant impact on the foundations of programming languages
[
14
]
, but also on their practice
[
1
]
.
Our goal in this work is to understand to what extent session types can admit simpler formulations and to clarify the status of such formulations.

In session-typed languages,
sequencing
constructs in types and processes specify the intended structure of message-passing protocols.
Sequencing is what distinguishes session types from other type systems, such as simple types
[
22
]
and linear types
[
17
]
, in which a channel’s type specifies exactly one communication action, rather than a series of actions.
For example, in the session type
S=?(\mathsf{int}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}?(\mathsf{int}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}!\langle\mathsf{bool}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}
, sequencing (denoted ‘
;
’) allows us to specify a protocol for a channel that
first
receives (?)
two integers,
then
sends (!)
a boolean, and
finally
ends (
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}
).
As such,
S
could be the type of a service that checks for integer equality.

Sequencing in types goes hand-in-hand with sequencing in processes, which is specified using the prefix construct (denoted ‘
.
’).
The
\pi
-calculus process
P=s?(x_{1}).s?(x_{2}).s!\langle x_{1}=x_{2}\rangle.\mathbf{0}
is an implementation of the equality service: it
first
expects two values on name
s
,
then
outputs a boolean on
s
, and
finally
stops.
We can see that name
s
in
P
conforms to the session type
S
.
Session types can also specify protocols in which sequencing occurs within labeled choices and recursion; these typed constructs are also in close match with their respective process expressions.
This way, e.g., a recursive variant of

S
is
\mu\mathsf{t}.?(\mathsf{int}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}?(\mathsf{int}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}!\langle\mathsf{bool}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathsf{t}
, which specifies a
persistent
integer equality service.

Session types were originally developed as a typing discipline for the analysis of binary (two-party) protocols
[
12
]
.
Since then session types have been extended in many directions.
We find, for instance, multiparty session types
[
13
]
, and extensions with dependent types, assertions, and exceptions (cf.
[
8
,
14
]
for surveys).
Building upon sequencing constructs, all these extensions seek to address natural questions on the expressivity and applicability of session types theories.

In this paper, we study a different direction.
We identify a
fragment
of (binary) session types that makes a very limited use of sequencing constructs.
In our fragment,
the type
S
in
session types such as ‘
!\langle U\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}S
’ and ‘
?(U){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}S
’ can only correspond to
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}
.
Sequencing is confined to recursive session types, which can only be of the forms ‘
\mu\mathsf{t}.!\langle U\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathsf{t}
’ and ‘
\mu\mathsf{t}.?(U){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathsf{t}
’.
That is, we admit sequencing solely for the purpose of specifying recursive protocols with a single action.
Because of this restricted role of sequencing, we call the resulting fragment
minimal session types
.

We study minimal session types on top of
\mathsf{HO}
, the core process calculus for session-based concurrency studied by Kouzapas et al.
[
20
]
.

\mathsf{HO}
is a very small language, which only supports passing of abstractions (i.e., functions from names to processes) and lacks name-passing and recursion.
Still,
\mathsf{HO}
is very expressive, because both features can be encoded in
\mathsf{HO}
in a fully abstract way.
Moreover,
\mathsf{HO}
has a well-developed theory of behavioral equivalences
[
19
]
.
Being a strict fragment of the typed framework by Kouzapas et al., processes typed with our minimal session types for
\mathsf{HO}
inherit all the technical properties and behavioral theory from
[
20
,
19
]
.

Leveraging the economical formulation and expressivity of
\mathsf{HO}
, we investigate whether the limited use of sequencing in minimal session types
is a too drastic restriction.
It turns out that it is not:
we show that for every
\mathsf{HO}
process
P
typable under standard session types, there is a
decomposition
of
P
, denoted
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P})}
, a process that codifies the sequencing information given by the session types (protocols) of
P
using additional synchronizations.

Figure

1
illustrates the key idea using the process
P
and session type
S
motivated above.
Because
P
contains three actions in sequence (as specified by
S
), its decomposition
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P})}
consists of three processes in parallel—each implementing a single action of
P
—as well as of orchestration mechanisms between them: the synchronizations on names
c_{2},\ldots,c_{5}
ensure that the sequencing in
P
is preserved and that received names are properly propagated. These three parallel processes are typable with minimal session types, which are obtained by “slicing”
S
(in the figure, these types appear below each process).

P=s?(x_{1}).s?(x_{2}).s!\langle x_{1}=x_{2}\rangle.\mathbf{0}
s:{?({\mathsf{int}})};{?({\mathsf{int}})};{!\langle{\mathsf{bool}}\rangle};\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P})}
{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.5,0.3}c_{2}?().}s_{1}?(x_{1}).{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.5,0.3}\overline{c_{3}}!\langle x_{1}\rangle}
\parallel
{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.5,0.3}c_{3}?(y_{1}).}s_{2}?(x_{2}).{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.5,0.3}\overline{c_{4}}!\langle y_{1},x_{2}\rangle}
\parallel
{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.5,0.3}c_{4}?(x_{1},x_{2}).}s_{3}!\langle x_{1}=x_{2}\rangle.{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.5,0.3}\overline{c_{5}}!\langle\rangle}
s_{1}:{?({\mathsf{int}})};\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}
c_{2}:{?()};\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}
s_{2}:{?({\mathsf{int}})};\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}
c_{3}:{?({\mathsf{int}})};\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}
s_{3}:{!\langle{\mathsf{bool}}\rangle};\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}
c_{4}:{?({\mathsf{int},\mathsf{int}})};\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}

Figure 1
:
The process decomposition, illustrated. Arrows in
magenta
indicate synchronizations orchestrated by the decomposition
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P})}
into trios.

The definition of
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P})}
is interesting on its own, as it draws inspiration from a known result by Parrow, who showed that any
untyped

\pi
-calculus process can be decomposed as a collection of
trios
, i.e., processes with exactly three nested prefixes
[
21
]
.
As already mentioned,
\mathsf{HO}
is a higher-order language and so is different from the (untyped, first-order)
\pi
-calculus considered by Parrow.
Indeed, as we will see, several aspects of our decomposition (and of its properties) take advantage of the higher-order nature of
\mathsf{HO}
.

We establish two technical results for
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P})}
: first, it is well-typed using minimal session types (
static correctness
); second, it is behaviorally equivalent to
P
(
dynamic correctness
).
These properties ensure that having sequencing in both types and processes is convenient, but that only sequencing at the level of processes is truly indispensable, as it can correctly codify sequencing in types.

As explained above, we dub our fragment of session types
minimal
to stress the limited use of sequencing with respect to standard presentations of session types, in which sequencing occurs unconstrained. That is, we interpret ‘minimality’ in terms of the role of sequencing in types for channels. Interestingly, as we discuss later on, the constructs present in our minimal session types correspond to forms already studied in the literature. One consequence of our technical results is that minimal session types stand between linear types (in which there is no sequencing nor recursion in types) and usual session types.
Also, our study of the process decomposition into trios complement prior works that seek to formally relate session types with other type systems, such as, generic types
[
9
]
and linear types
[
7
,
5
,
6
]
. See
Section

7
for a discussion on related works.

Contributions & Outline.

In summary, in this paper we present the following contributions:

1.

We identify
minimal session types
(MSTs): a fragment of standard session types for
\mathsf{HO}
that makes a very limited use of sequencing (
Definition

3.1
).

2.

We show how to correctly decompose processes typable with standard session types into processes typable with minimal session types, and how to decompose standard session types into minimal session types. This is a result of static correctness (
3.1
).

3.

We show that the decomposition of a process is behaviorally equivalent to the original process. This is a result of
dynamic correctness
, formalized in terms of
MST bisimulations
, a typed behavioral equivalence that we introduce here (
Theorem

4.1
).

4.

We develop optimizations and extensions of our decomposition that bear witness to its robustness.

The rest of the paper is organized as follows.
In
Section

2
we recall the preliminaries on the session type system for
\mathsf{HO}
, the core process calculus for session-based concurrency on which we base our developments.
In
Section

3
we present minimal session types and the decomposition of well-typed
\mathsf{HO}
processes into minimal session types processes, accompanied by explanations and examples.
In
Section

4
we show the correctness of the decomposition, by establishing an
MST bisimulation
between an
\mathsf{HO}
process and its decomposition.
In
Section

5
we examine two optimizations of the decomposition that are enabled by the higher-order nature of our setting.
In
Section

6
we discuss extensions of our approach to consider constructs for branching and selection.
Finally, in
Section

7
we discuss related work and in
Section

8
we present some closing remarks.
The appendix contains omitted definitions and proofs.

Differences with the conference version.

An earlier version of this paper was presented at ECOOP 2019
[
3
]
.
The current paper revises the conference version, includes further examples, and incorporates a major addition:
Section

4
on dynamic correctness, including the notion of an MST bisimulation and the constructed bisimulation relation, is completely new to this presentation.

Colors.

Throughout the paper we use colors (such as
pink
and
green
) to improve readability.
However, the usage of colors is not indispensable to follow the paper (black and white suffice).

2
The Source Language

We start by recalling the syntax, semantics, and type system for
\mathsf{HO}
, the higher-order process calculus for session-based concurrency studied by Kouzapas et al.
[
20
,
19
]
. Our presentation of
\mathsf{HO}
follows the aforementioned papers, which concern definitions and results for
\mathsf{HO}\pi
, the super-calculus of
\mathsf{HO}
with name-passing, abstraction-passing, and recursion.

\mathsf{HO}
is arguably the simplest language for session types: it supports
passing of abstractions (functions from names to processes)
but does not support name-passing nor process recursion.
Still,
\mathsf{HO}
is very expressive: it can encode name-passing, recursion, and polyadic communication via type-preserving encodings that are fully-abstract with respect to contextual equivalence
[
20
]
.

2.1
Syntax and Semantics

Definition 2.1

(
\mathsf{HO}
processes)
.

The syntax of names, variables, values, and
\mathsf{HO}
processes is defined as follows:

\displaystyle n,m

\displaystyle\;::=\;a,b\mathrel{\big|}s,\overline{s}\qquad\quad u,w\;::=\;n\mathrel{\big|}x,y,z\qquad\quad V,W\;::=\;{x,y,z}\mathrel{\big|}{\lambda x.\,P}

\displaystyle P,Q

\displaystyle\;::=\;u!\langle V\rangle.{P}\mathrel{\big|}u?(x).{P}\mathrel{\big|}{V\,{u}}\mathrel{\big|}P\;|\;Q\mathrel{\big|}(\nu\,n)\,P\mathrel{\big|}\mathbf{0}

We use
a,b,c,\dots
to range over
shared names
, and
s,\overline{s},\dots
to range over
session names
. Shared names are used for unrestricted, non-deterministic interactions; session names are used for linear, deterministic interactions. We write
n,m
to denote session or shared names, and assume that the sets of session and shared names are disjoint. The
dual
of a name
n
is denoted
\overline{n}
; we define
\overline{\overline{s}}=s
and
\overline{a}=a
, i.e., duality is only relevant for session names. Variables are denoted with
x,y,z,\dots
. An abstraction
\lambda x.\,P
is a process
P
with parameter
x
.

Values

V,W,\ldots
include variables and abstractions, but not names.

Process

V\,{u}

is the application
which substitutes name
u
on abstraction
V
. Constructs for
inaction
\mathbf{0}
, parallel composition
P_{1}\;|\;P_{2}
, and
name restriction
(\nu\,n)\,P
are standard.

\mathsf{HO}
lacks name-passing and recursion, but they are expressible in the language (see
Example

2.1
below).

To enhance readability, we often omit trailing
\mathbf{0}
’s, so we write, e.g.,

u!\langle V\rangle{}
instead of
u!\langle V\rangle.{\mathbf{0}}
.
Also, we write

u!\langle\rangle.{P}
and
u?().{P}
whenever the exchanged value is not relevant (cf.
Remark

3.2
).

Restriction for shared names
(\nu\,a)\,P
is as usual; session name restriction
(\nu\,s)\,P
simultaneously binds session names
s
and
\overline{s}
in
P
.
Functions
\mathtt{fv}(P)
,
\mathtt{fn}(P)
, and
\mathtt{fs}(P)
denote, respectively, the sets of free
variables, names, and session names in
P
, and are defined as expected.
If
\mathtt{fv}(P)=\emptyset
, we call
P

closed
.
We write
P\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$u$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\}
(resp.,
P\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$V$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\}
) for the capture-avoiding substitution of name
u
(resp.,  value
V
) for
y
in process
P
.
We identify processes up to consistent renaming of bound names, writing
\equiv_{\alpha}
for this congruence.
We shall rely on Barendregt’s variable convention, under which free and bound names are different in every mathematical context.

The operational semantics of
\mathsf{HO}
is defined in terms of a
reduction relation
,
denoted
\longrightarrow
.
Reduction is closed under
structural congruence
,
denoted
\equiv
, which is defined as the smallest congruence on processes such that:

\displaystyle P\;|\;\mathbf{0}\equiv P\qquad P_{1}\;|\;P_{2}\equiv P_{2}\;|\;P_{1}\qquad P_{1}\;|\;(P_{2}\;|\;P_{3})\equiv(P_{1}\;|\;P_{2})\;|\;P_{3}

\displaystyle(\nu\,n)\,\mathbf{0}\equiv\mathbf{0}\qquad P\;|\;(\nu\,n)\,Q\equiv(\nu\,n)\,(P\;|\;Q)\ (n\notin\mathtt{fn}(P))\qquad P\equiv Q\textrm{ if }P\equiv_{\alpha}Q

We assume the expected extension of
\equiv
to values
V
.
The reduction relation expresses the behavior of processes; it is defined as follows:

\displaystyle(\lambda x.\,P)\,{u}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow P\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$u$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\}

\displaystyle{\scriptsize{[\textrm{\small App}]}}

\displaystyle n!\langle V\rangle.P\;|\;\overline{n}?(x).Q

\displaystyle\longrightarrow P\;|\;Q\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$V$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\}

\displaystyle{\scriptsize{[\textrm{\small Pass}]}}

\displaystyle P\longrightarrow P^{\prime}\Rightarrow(\nu\,n)\,P

\displaystyle\longrightarrow(\nu\,n)\,P^{\prime}

\displaystyle{\scriptsize{[\textrm{\small Res}]}}

\displaystyle P\longrightarrow P^{\prime}\Rightarrow P\;|\;Q

\displaystyle\longrightarrow P^{\prime}\;|\;Q

\displaystyle{\scriptsize{[\textrm{\small Par}]}}

\displaystyle P\equiv Q\longrightarrow Q^{\prime}\equiv P^{\prime}\Rightarrow P

\displaystyle\longrightarrow P^{\prime}

\displaystyle{\scriptsize{[\textrm{\small Cong}]}}

Rule
{\scriptsize{[\textrm{\small App}]}}
defines name application (
\beta
-reduction). Rule
{\scriptsize{[\textrm{\small Pass}]}}
defines a
shared or session interaction, depending on the nature of
n
.
Other rules are standard
\pi
-calculus rules.
We write

\longrightarrow^{k}
for a
k
-step reduction, and

\longrightarrow^{\ast}
for the reflexive, transitive closure of
\longrightarrow
.

We illustrate
\mathsf{HO}
processes and their semantics by means of an example.

Example 2.1

(Encoding Name-Passing)
.

The
\mathsf{HO}
calculus lacks the name-passing primitives of
\mathsf{HO}\pi
.
Hence, it cannot express reductions of the form

\displaystyle n!\langle m\rangle.P\;|\;\overline{n}?(x).Q\longrightarrow P\;|\;Q\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$m$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\}

(1)

Fortunately, name-passing can be
encoded in
\mathsf{HO}
in a fully-abstract way: as shown in
[
18
,
20
]
, one can use abstraction passing to “pack” a name.
To this end,
Figure

2
defines the required syntactic sugar, at the level of processes and types.
Then, the reduction (
1
) can be mimicked as

\displaystyle{n!\langle\ulcorner m\urcorner\rangle.P\;|\;\overline{n}?(\ulcorner x\urcorner).Q}=\penalty\ \penalty\

\displaystyle n!\big\langle\lambda z.\,\,z?(x).(x\,{m})\big\rangle.{P}\;|\;\overline{n}?(y).(\nu\,s)(y\,{s}\;|\;\overline{s}!\langle\lambda x.\,{Q}\rangle)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\

\displaystyle{P}\;|\;(\nu\,s)(\lambda z.\,\,z?(x).(x\,{m})\,{s}\;|\;\overline{s}!\langle\lambda x.\,{Q}\rangle)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\

\displaystyle{P}\;|\;(\nu\,s)(s?(x).(x\,{m})\;|\;\overline{s}!\langle\lambda x.\,{Q}\rangle)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\

\displaystyle{P}\;|\;(\lambda x.\,{Q})\,{m}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\

\displaystyle{P}\;|\;{Q}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$m$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\}

\lhd

\displaystyle t!\langle\ulcorner\tilde{u}\urcorner\rangle.P

\displaystyle\triangleq t!\langle\lambda z.z?(x).(x\ {\tilde{u}})\rangle.P

\displaystyle t?(\ulcorner\tilde{x}\urcorner).Q

\displaystyle\triangleq t?(y).\big((\nu\,z)\,(y\ z\;|\;z!\langle\lambda{\tilde{x}}.Q\rangle)\big)

\displaystyle\vskip 5.69054pt{\ulcorner\widetilde{S}\urcorner}

\displaystyle\triangleq(?(\llceil\widetilde{S}\rrceil\!\multimap\!\diamond){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}})\!\multimap\!\diamond

\displaystyle{\ulcorner\langle\widetilde{S}\rangle\urcorner}

\displaystyle\triangleq(?(\langle\llceil\widetilde{S}\rrceil\rangle\!\multimap\!\diamond){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}})\!\multimap\!\diamond

\displaystyle\ulcorner\widetilde{C}\!\multimap\!\diamond\urcorner

\displaystyle\triangleq\llceil\widetilde{C}\rrceil\!\multimap\!\diamond

\displaystyle\ulcorner\widetilde{C}\!\rightarrow\!\diamond\urcorner

\displaystyle\triangleq\llceil\widetilde{C}\rrceil\!\rightarrow\!\diamond

\displaystyle\llceil!\langle{U}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}S\rrceil

\displaystyle\triangleq{!\langle\ulcorner U\urcorner\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\llceil S\rrceil}

\displaystyle\llceil?({U}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}S\rrceil

\displaystyle\triangleq{?(\ulcorner U\urcorner){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\llceil S\rrceil}

\displaystyle\llceil C_{1},\ldots,C_{n}\rrceil

\displaystyle\triangleq\llceil C_{1}\rrceil,\ldots,\llceil C_{n}\rrceil

Figure 2
:
Encoding name passing in
\mathsf{HO}

Remark 2.1

(Polyadic Communication)
.

\mathsf{HO}
as presented above allows only for
monadic communication
, i.e., the exchange of tuples of values with length 1.
We will find it convenient to use
\mathsf{HO}
with
polyadic communication
, i.e., the exchange of tuples of values
\widetilde{V}=(V_{1},\ldots,V_{k})
, with length
|\widetilde{V}|=k
.
We will use similar notation for tuples of names and variables, and we will use
\epsilon
to denote the empty tuple.

In
\mathsf{HO}
, polyadicity appears in session synchronizations and applications, but not in synchronizations on shared names. This entails having the following reduction rules:

\displaystyle(\lambda\widetilde{x}.\,P)\,{\widetilde{u}}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow P\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\widetilde{u}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\widetilde{x}$}\}

\displaystyle s!\langle\widetilde{V}\rangle.P\;|\;\overline{s}?(\widetilde{x}).Q

\displaystyle\longrightarrow P\;|\;Q\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\widetilde{V}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\widetilde{x}$}\}

where the simultaneous substitutions

P\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\widetilde{u}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\widetilde{x}$}\}

and
P\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\widetilde{V}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\widetilde{x}$}\}

are as expected.
This polyadic
\mathsf{HO}
can be readily encoded into (monadic)
\mathsf{HO}

[
19
]
; for this reason, by a slight
abuse of notation we will often write
\mathsf{HO}
when we actually mean “polyadic
\mathsf{HO}
”.

We discuss two simple examples that illustrate how
\mathsf{HO}
can implement mechanisms resembling servers and forms of partial instantiation; these mechanisms shall come in handy later, when defining the process decomposition in
Section

3
.

Example 2.2

(A Server of a Kind)
.

Let
S_{a}
denote the process
a?(x).(x\,{r})
, which receives an abstraction on the shared name
a
and then applies it to
r
. Consider the following process composition:

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle=(\nu\,r)\,(\nu\,a)\,\big(a!\langle V\rangle.\mathbf{0}\;|\;a!\langle W\rangle.\mathbf{0}\;|\;S_{a}\;|\;\overline{r}?(x_{1}).\overline{r}?(x_{2}).Q\big)

\displaystyle V

\displaystyle=\lambda y.\,(y!\langle V^{\prime}\rangle.S_{a}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$y$}\!/\mbox{\small$r$}\})

\displaystyle W

\displaystyle=\lambda z.\,(z!\langle W^{\prime}\rangle.S_{a}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$z$}\!/\mbox{\small$r$}\})

where
V^{\prime}
and
W^{\prime}
are some unspecified shared values.
In
P
, process
S_{a}
operates as a server that provides
r
upon an invocation on
a
.
Dually, the outputs on
a
are requests to this server.
One possible reduction sequence for
P
is the following:

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle\longrightarrow(\nu\,r)\,(\nu\,a)\,\big(a!\langle W\rangle.\mathbf{0}\;|\;V\,{r}\;|\;\overline{r}?(x_{1}).\overline{r}?(x_{2}).Q\big)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow(\nu\,r)\,(\nu\,a)\,\big(a!\langle W\rangle.\mathbf{0}\;|\;r!\langle V^{\prime}\rangle.S_{a}\;|\;\overline{r}?(x_{1}).\overline{r}?(x_{2}).Q\big)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow(\nu\,r)\,(\nu\,a)\,\big(a!\langle W\rangle.\mathbf{0}\;|\;S_{a}\;|\;\overline{r}?(x_{2}).Q\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$V^{\prime}$}\!/\mbox{\small$x_{1}$}\}\big)=P^{\prime}

In this reduction sequence, the value
V
in the first request is instantiated with the name
r
by consuming a copy of
S_{a}
available in
P
.
However, a copy of the server
S_{a}
is restored through the value
V
, after an communication on
r
.
This way, in
P^{\prime}
the exchange of
W^{\prime}
on
r
can take place:

P^{\prime}\longrightarrow^{\ast}(\nu\,r)\,(\nu\,a)\,\big(S_{a}\;|\;Q\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$V^{\prime}$}\!/\mbox{\small$x_{1}$}\}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$W^{\prime}$}\!/\mbox{\small$x_{2}$}\}\big)

Example 2.3

(Partial Instantiation)
.

Let
S_{a}
and
S_{b}
be servers as defined in the previous example:

\displaystyle S_{a}=a?(x).(x\,{r})\qquad\qquad S_{b}=b?(x).(x\,{v})

Further, let
R
be a process in which requests to
S_{a}
and
S_{b}
are nested within abstractions:

\displaystyle R

\displaystyle=a!\big\langle\lambda y.\,b!\langle\lambda z.\,V\,{(y,z)}\rangle\big\rangle

Notice how the polyadic application ‘
V\,{(y,z)}
’ is enclosed in the innermost abstraction.
Now consider the following composition:

\displaystyle P=(\nu\,a,b)\,R\;|\;S_{a}\;|\;S_{b}

The structure of
R
induces a form of partial instantiation for
y,z
, implemented by combining synchronizations and
\beta
-reductions. To see this, let us inspect one possible reduction chain for
P
:

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle\longrightarrow(\nu\,b)\,\left(\lambda y.\,b!\langle\lambda z.\,V\,{(y,z)}\rangle\,{r}\right)\;|\;S_{b}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow(\nu\,b)\,{b!\langle\lambda z.\,V\,{(r,z)}\rangle}\;|\;S_{b}=P^{\prime}

The first request of
R
, aimed to obtain name
r
, is
realized by the first reduction, i.e., the communication with
S_{a}
on name
a
:
the result is the application of the top-level abstraction to
r
.
Subsequently, the application step substitutes

y
with
r
.
Hence, in
P^{\prime}
,
names in the nested application are only
partially instantiated
: at this point, we have ‘
V\,{(r,z)}
’.

Process
P^{\prime}
can then execute the same steps to instantiate
z
with name
v

by interacting with
S_{b}
.
After two reductions, we obtain the fully instantiated application
V\,{(r,v)}
:

\displaystyle P^{\prime}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow{\lambda z.\,V\,{(r,z)}}\,{v}\longrightarrow V\,{(r,v)}

2.2
Session Types for
\mathsf{HO}

We give essential definitions and properties for the session type system for
\mathsf{HO}
, following
[
19
]
.

Definition 2.2

(Session Types for
\mathsf{HO}
)
.

Let us write
\diamond
to denote the process type.
The syntax of value types
U
, channel types
C
, and session types
S
for
\mathsf{HO}
is defined as follows:

\displaystyle U

\displaystyle\;::=\;C\!\rightarrow\!\diamond\mathrel{\big|}C\!\multimap\!\diamond\qquad\qquad C\;::=\;S\mathrel{\big|}{\langle U\rangle}

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle\;::=\;!\langle U\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}S\mathrel{\big|}\,?(U){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}S\mathrel{\big|}\,\mu\mathsf{t}.S\mathrel{\big|}\mathsf{t}\mathrel{\big|}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}

As we have seen,
\mathsf{HO}
only admits the exchange of abstractions; accordingly,
value types include
C\!\rightarrow\!\diamond
and
C\!\multimap\!\diamond
, which denote
shared
and

linear
higher-order types, respectively. Channel types include session
types and the shared types
\langle U\rangle
.

Session types follow the standard binary session type syntax
[
12
]
, in which sequencing specifies communication structures.
This way, the
output type

!\langle U\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}S
describes a session in which first a value of type
U
is sent, and then the session proceeds as
S
.
Dually, the
input type

?(U){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}S
describes a session in which first a value of type
U
is received, and then the session proceeds as
S
.
In examples, we often assume basic types (such as
\mathsf{int}
,
\mathsf{bool}
,
\mathsf{str}
) are exchanged in communications.
Session types also include
recursive types

\mu\mathsf{t}.S
, in which the variable
\mathsf{t}
is assumed to occur guarded in
S
, i.e., types such as
\mu\mathsf{t}.\mathsf{t}
are not allowed.
In most cases, recursive types will be
tail-recursive
, although instances of
non-tail-recursive
session types will also be relevant (cf.

Example

3.3
).
Finally, type
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}
is the type of the terminated protocol.

Notation 2.1
.

As mentioned in the introduction, we shall study session types in which the continuation
S
in
!\langle U\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}S
and
?(U){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}S
is always
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}
.
Given this, we may sometimes omit trailing
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}
’s and write

!\langle U\rangle
and
?(U)

rather than

!\langle U\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}
and
?(U){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}
, respectively.

In theories of session types
duality
is a key notion:
implementations derived from dual session types will respect their protocols at run-time, avoiding communication errors.
Intuitively, duality is obtained by
exchanging
!
by
?
(and vice versa), including the fixed point construction.
We write
S\ \mathsf{dual}\ T
if session types
S
and
T
are dual according to this intuition;
the formal definition is coinductive, and given in
[
19
]
(see also
[
10
]
).

We consider shared, linear, and session

environments
, denoted
\Gamma
,
\Lambda
, and
\Delta
, resp.:

\displaystyle\Gamma

\displaystyle\;::=\;\emptyset\mathrel{\big|}\Gamma,x:C\!\rightarrow\!\diamond\mathrel{\big|}\Gamma,u:\langle U\rangle

\displaystyle\Lambda

\displaystyle\;::=\;\emptyset\mathrel{\big|}\Lambda,x\!:\!C\!\multimap\!\diamond

\displaystyle\Delta

\displaystyle\;::=\;\emptyset\mathrel{\big|}\Delta,u\!:\!S

\Gamma
maps variables and shared names to value types;
\Lambda
maps variables to
linear
higher-order types.
\Delta
maps session names to session types. While

\Gamma
admits weakening, contraction, and exchange principles, both
\Lambda

and
\Delta

are only subject to exchange. The domains of
\Gamma,\Lambda
, and
\Delta
are
assumed pairwise distinct. We write
\Delta_{1}\cdot\Delta_{2}
to denote the
disjoint union of
\Delta_{1}
and
\Delta_{2}
.

We write
\Gamma\backslash x
to denote the environment obtained from
\Gamma
by removing the assignment
x:C\!\rightarrow\!\diamond
, for some
C
.
Notations
\Delta\backslash u
and
\Gamma\backslash\widetilde{x}
are defined similarly and have the expected readings.
With a slight abuse of notation, given a tuple of variables
\widetilde{x}
, we sometimes write
(\Gamma,\Delta)(\widetilde{x})
to denote the tuple of types assigned to the variables in
\widetilde{x}
by the environments
\Gamma
and
\Delta
.

The typing judgements for values
V
and processes
P
are denoted

\Gamma;\Lambda;\Delta\vdash V\triangleright U\qquad\text{and}\qquad\Gamma;\Lambda;\Delta\vdash P\triangleright\diamond

\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{}{\Gamma;\emptyset;\{u:S\}\vdash u\triangleright S}\quad\textsc{(Sess)}\qquad\frac{}{\Gamma,u:U;\emptyset;\emptyset\vdash u\triangleright U}\quad\textsc{(Sh)}\\
\\
\frac{}{\Gamma;\{x:C\!\multimap\!\diamond\};\emptyset\vdash x\triangleright C\!\multimap\!\diamond}\quad\textsc{(LVar)}\qquad\frac{}{\Gamma,X:\Delta;\emptyset;\Delta\vdash X\triangleright\diamond}\quad\textsc{(RVar)}\\
\\
\frac{\Gamma;\Lambda;\Delta_{1}\vdash P\triangleright\diamond\hskip 8.19447pt\Gamma;\emptyset;\Delta_{2}\vdash x\triangleright C}{\Gamma\backslash x;\Lambda;\Delta_{1}\backslash\Delta_{2}\vdash\lambda{x}.\,P\triangleright{C}\!\multimap\!\diamond}\quad\textsc{(Abs)}\quad\frac{{\Gamma;\Lambda;\Delta_{1}\vdash V\triangleright C\!\leadsto\!\diamond\penalty\ \penalty\ \leadsto\,\in\{\multimap,\rightarrow\}\hskip 8.19447pt\Gamma;\emptyset;\Delta_{2}\vdash u\triangleright C}}{\Gamma;\Lambda;\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}\vdash V\,{u}\triangleright\diamond}\quad\textsc{(App)}\par\\
\\
\frac{\Gamma;\emptyset;\emptyset\vdash V\triangleright C\!\multimap\!\diamond}{\Gamma;\emptyset;\emptyset\vdash V\triangleright C\!\rightarrow\!\diamond}\quad\textsc{(Prom)}\quad\frac{\Gamma;\Lambda,x:C\!\multimap\!\diamond;\Delta\vdash P\triangleright\diamond}{\Gamma,x:C\!\rightarrow\!\diamond;\Lambda;\Delta\vdash P\triangleright\diamond}\quad\textsc{(EProm)}\quad\frac{\Gamma;\Lambda;\Delta\vdash P\triangleright T\hskip 8.19447ptu\not\in\mathtt{dom}(\Gamma,\Lambda,\Delta)}{\Gamma;\Lambda;\Delta,u:\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}\vdash P\triangleright\diamond}\quad\textsc{(End)}\\
\\
\frac{\Gamma,X:\Delta;\emptyset;\Delta\vdash P\triangleright\diamond}{\Gamma;\emptyset;\Delta\vdash\mu X.P\triangleright\diamond}\quad\textsc{(Rec)}\qquad\frac{\Gamma;\Lambda_{i};\Delta_{i}\vdash P_{i}\triangleright\diamond\hskip 8.19447pti=1,2}{\Gamma;\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2};\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}\vdash P_{1}\;|\;P_{2}\triangleright\diamond}\quad\textsc{(Par)}\qquad\frac{}{\Gamma;\emptyset;\emptyset\vdash\mathbf{0}\triangleright\diamond}\quad\textsc{(Nil)}\\
\\
\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma;\Lambda;\Delta_{1}\vdash P\triangleright\diamond\quad\Gamma;\emptyset;\emptyset\vdash u\triangleright\langle U\rangle\quad\Gamma;\emptyset;\Delta_{2}\vdash V\triangleright U\end{array}}{\Gamma;\Lambda;\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}\vdash u!\langle V\rangle.P\triangleright\diamond}\quad\textsc{(Req)}\penalty\ \penalty\ \frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma;\Lambda_{1};\Delta_{1}\vdash P\triangleright\diamond\quad\Gamma;\emptyset;\emptyset\vdash u\triangleright\langle U\rangle\quad\Gamma;\Lambda_{2};\Delta_{2}\vdash x\triangleright U\end{array}}{\Gamma\backslash x;\Lambda_{1}\backslash\Lambda_{2};\Delta_{1}\backslash\Delta_{2}\vdash u?(x).P\triangleright\diamond}\quad\textsc{(Acc)}\\
\\
\frac{u:S\in\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}\hskip 8.19447pt\Gamma;\Lambda_{1};\Delta_{1}\vdash P\triangleright\diamond\hskip 8.19447pt\Gamma;\Lambda_{2};\Delta_{2}\vdash V\triangleright U}{\Gamma;\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2};((\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2})\setminus u:S),u:!\langle U\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}S\vdash u!\langle V\rangle.P\triangleright\diamond}\quad\textsc{(Send)}\\
\\
\frac{\Gamma;\Lambda_{1};\Delta_{1},u:S\vdash P\triangleright\diamond\hskip 8.19447pt\Gamma;\Lambda_{2};\Delta_{2}\vdash{x}\triangleright{U}}{\Gamma\backslash x;\Lambda_{1}\backslash\Lambda_{2};\Delta_{1}\backslash\Delta_{2},u:?(U){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}S\vdash u?({x}).P\triangleright\diamond}\quad\textsc{(Rcv)}\\
\\
\frac{\Gamma;\Lambda;\Delta,s:S_{1},\overline{s}:S_{2}\vdash P\triangleright\diamond\hskip 8.19447ptS_{1}\ \mathsf{dual}\ S_{2}}{\Gamma;\Lambda;\Delta\vdash(\nu\,s)\,P\triangleright\diamond}\quad\textsc{(ResS)}\qquad\frac{\Gamma,a:\langle S\rangle;\Lambda;\Delta\vdash P\triangleright\diamond}{\Gamma;\Lambda;\Delta\vdash(\nu\,a)\,P\triangleright\diamond}\quad\textsc{(Res)}\end{array}

Figure 3
:
Typing Rules for
\mathsf{HO}
.

Figure

3
shows the typing rules; we briefly describe them and refer the reader to
[
19
]
for a full account.
The shared type
C\!\rightarrow\!\diamond
is derived using Rule
(Prom)
only
if the value has a linear type with an empty linear
environment.
Rule
(EProm)
allows us to freely use a shared
type variable as linear.
Abstraction values are typed with Rule
(Abs)
.
Application typing
is governed by Rule
(App)
:
the type
C
of an application name
u

must match the type of the application variable
x
(
C\!\multimap\!\diamond
or
C\!\rightarrow\!\diamond
).
Rules
(Req)
and
(Acc)
type interaction along shared names; the type of the sent/received object
V
(i.e.,
U
) should match the type of the subject
s
(
\langle U\rangle
).
In Rule
(Send)
, the type
U
of the value
V
should appear as a prefix in the session type
!\langle U\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}S
of
u
.
Rule
(Rcv)
is its dual.

To state type soundness, we require two auxiliary definitions on session environments.
First, a session environment
\Delta
is
balanced
(written
\mathsf{balanced}(\Delta)
) if whenever
s:S_{1},\overline{s}:S_{2}\in\Delta
then
S_{1}\ \mathsf{dual}\ S_{2}
.
Second, we define the reduction relation
\longrightarrow
on session environments as:

\displaystyle\Delta,s:!\langle U\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}S_{1},\overline{s}:?(U){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}S_{2}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow

\displaystyle\Delta,s:S_{1},\overline{s}:S_{2}

\displaystyle\Delta,s:\oplus\{l_{i}:S_{i}\}_{i\in I},\overline{s}:\&\{l_{i}:S_{i}^{\prime}\}_{i\in I}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow

\displaystyle\Delta,s:S_{k},\overline{s}:S_{k}^{\prime}\ (k\in I)

Theorem 2.1

(Type Soundness
[
19
]
)
.

Suppose
\Gamma;\emptyset;\Delta\vdash P\triangleright\diamond

with

\mathsf{balanced}(\Delta)
.
Then
P\longrightarrow P^{\prime}
implies
\Gamma;\emptyset;\Delta^{\prime}\vdash P^{\prime}\triangleright\diamond

and
\Delta=\Delta^{\prime}
or
\Delta\longrightarrow\Delta^{\prime}

with
\mathsf{balanced}(\Delta^{\prime})
.

Remark 2.2

(Typed Polyadic Communication)
.

When using processes with polyadic communication (cf.
Remark

2.1
),
we shall assume the extension of the type system defined in
[
19
]
.

Example 2.4

(Typing name-passing constructs)
.

In
Example

2.1
we recalled how to encode name-passing constructs in
\mathsf{HO}
; now we show that this translation is typed.
Following the name-passing encoding from
[
18
,
20
]

we define a syntactic sugar for types.
The following typing rules for name-passing are derivable:

\frac{\Gamma;\Lambda_{1};\Delta_{1}\vdash P\triangleright\diamond\quad\Gamma;\Lambda_{2};\Delta_{2}\vdash\widetilde{b}\triangleright\widetilde{C}}{\Gamma;\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2};\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},t:!\langle\ulcorner\widetilde{C}\urcorner\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}\vdash t!\langle\ulcorner\widetilde{b}\urcorner\rangle.P\triangleright\diamond}\quad\textsc{left=(SendN)}\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma;\Lambda_{1};\Delta_{1}\vdash P\triangleright\diamond\quad\Gamma;\Lambda_{2};\Delta_{2}\vdash\widetilde{x}\triangleright\widetilde{C}\end{array}}{\Gamma\backslash x;\Lambda_{1}\backslash\Lambda_{2};\Delta_{1}\backslash\Delta_{2},t:?(\ulcorner\widetilde{C}\urcorner){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}\vdash t?(\ulcorner\widetilde{x}\urcorner).P\triangleright\diamond}\quad\textsc{left=(RcvN)}

Example 2.5

(Typing Recursive Servers)
.

Here we show how to type the processes from
Example

2.2
. Let us define:

\displaystyle T=\mu\mathsf{t}.!\langle U\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathsf{t}\qquad C=\langle T\!\multimap\!\diamond\rangle

where
U
is some value type. We recall process
P

from
Example

2.2
with the additional typing information on bound names

r
and
a
:

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle=(\nu\,r:T)\,(\nu\,a:C)\,\big(a!\langle V\rangle.\mathbf{0}\;|\;a!\langle W\rangle.\mathbf{0}\;|\;S_{a}\;|\;\overline{r}?(x_{1}).\overline{r}?(x_{2}).Q\big)

\displaystyle V

\displaystyle=\lambda y.\,(y!\langle V^{\prime}\rangle.S_{a}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$y$}\!/\mbox{\small$r$}\})

\displaystyle W

\displaystyle=\lambda z.\,(z!\langle W^{\prime}\rangle.S_{a}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$z$}\!/\mbox{\small$r$}\})

where
S_{a}
stands for
a?(x).(x\,{r})
.
Let us assume
there is a shared environment

\Gamma
under which
V^{\prime}
and
W^{\prime}
implement type
U
:

\displaystyle\Gamma;\emptyset;\emptyset

\displaystyle\vdash V^{\prime}\triangleright U

(2)

\displaystyle\Gamma;\emptyset;\emptyset

\displaystyle\vdash W^{\prime}\triangleright U

(3)

Also, we assume that process
\overline{r}?(x_{1}).\overline{r}?(x_{2}).Q
is well-typed under
the following environments:

\displaystyle\Gamma,a:C;\emptyset;\overline{r}:\overline{T}\vdash\overline{r}?(x_{1}).\overline{r}?(x_{2}).Q\triangleright\diamond

(4)

Under these assumptions,
it holds that the body of process
P
correctly implements name
a

with type
C
, i.e.,

\Gamma;\emptyset;r:T\vdash(\nu\,a:C)\,\big(a!\langle V\rangle.\mathbf{0}\;|\;a!\langle W\rangle.\mathbf{0}\;|\;S_{a}\big)\triangleright\diamond

We detail the corresponding typing derivations:

(LVar)

\Gamma,a:C;x:T\!\multimap\!\diamond;\emptyset\vdash x\triangleright T\!\multimap\!\diamond

(Sess)

\Gamma,a:C;\emptyset;y:T\vdash y\triangleright T

(App)

\Gamma,a:C;x:T\!\multimap\!\diamond;y:T\vdash x\,{y}\triangleright\diamond

(5)

(
5
)

(Sh)

\Gamma,a:C;\emptyset;\emptyset\vdash a\triangleright\langle T\!\multimap\!\diamond\rangle

(LVar)

\Gamma,a:C;x:T\!\multimap\!\diamond;\emptyset\vdash x\triangleright T\!\multimap\!\diamond

(Acc)

\Gamma,a:C;\emptyset;y:T\vdash a?(x).(x\,{y})\triangleright\diamond

(6)

(
6
)

(
2
)

(Send)

\Gamma,a:C;\emptyset;y:T\vdash y!\langle V^{\prime}\rangle.S_{a}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$y$}\!/\mbox{\small$r$}\}\triangleright\diamond

(Sess)

\Gamma,a:C;\emptyset;y:T\vdash y\triangleright T

(Abs)

\Gamma,a:C;\emptyset;\emptyset\vdash V\triangleright T\!\multimap\!\diamond

(7)

(Nil)

\Gamma,a:C;\emptyset;\emptyset\vdash\mathbf{0}\triangleright\diamond

(Sh)

\Gamma,a:C;\emptyset;\emptyset\vdash a\triangleright\langle T\!\multimap\!\diamond\rangle

(
7
)

(Req)

\Gamma,a:C;\emptyset;\emptyset\vdash a!\langle V\rangle.\mathbf{0}\triangleright\diamond

(8)

In the following derivation tree the
right-hand side is shown similarly to (
8
)
using assumption (
3
) instead of (
2
):

(
8
)

\Gamma,a:C;\emptyset;\emptyset\vdash a!\langle W\rangle.\mathbf{0}\triangleright\diamond

(Par)

\Gamma,a:C;\emptyset;\emptyset\vdash a!\langle V\rangle.\mathbf{0}\;|\;a!\langle W\rangle.\mathbf{0}\triangleright\diamond

(9)

Finally we have:

(
9
)

\Gamma,a:C;\emptyset;r:T\vdash S_{a}\triangleright\diamond

(Par)

\Gamma,a:C;\emptyset;r:T\vdash a!\langle V\rangle.\mathbf{0}\;|\;a!\langle W\rangle.\mathbf{0}\;|\;S_{a}\triangleright\diamond

(
4
)

(Par)

\Gamma,a:C;\emptyset;r:T,\overline{r}:\overline{T}\vdash a!\langle V\rangle.\mathbf{0}\;|\;a!\langle W\rangle.\mathbf{0}\;|\;S_{a}\;|\;\overline{r}?(x_{1}).\overline{r}?(x_{2}).Q\triangleright\diamond

(Res)

\Gamma;\emptyset;r:T,\overline{r}:\overline{T}\vdash(\nu\,a:C)\,\big(a!\langle V\rangle.\mathbf{0}\;|\;a!\langle W\rangle.\mathbf{0}\;|\;S_{a}\;|\;\overline{r}?(x_{1}).\overline{r}?(x_{2}).Q\big)\triangleright\diamond

(ResS)

\Gamma;\emptyset;\emptyset\vdash(\nu\,r:T)\,(\nu\,a:C)\,\big(a!\langle V\rangle.\mathbf{0}\;|\;a!\langle W\rangle.\mathbf{0}\;|\;S_{a}\;|\;\overline{r}?(x_{1}).\overline{r}?(x_{2}).Q\big)\triangleright\diamond

Above, we have omitted details of the right-hand
side derivation; it the same as (
6
)
with name
y
substituted with
r
.

Example 2.6

(Typing Nested Abstractions)
.

Here we show how to type process
P
from
Example

2.3
.
Let types
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
be defined as

C_{i}=\langle S_{i}\!\multimap\!\diamond\rangle

where
i\in\{1,2\}
and
S_{i}
stands for a tail-recursive type.
For simplicity, we assume that value
V
has the following typing:

\displaystyle a:C_{1},b:C_{2};\emptyset;\emptyset\vdash V\triangleright(S_{1},S_{2})\!\multimap\!\diamond

(10)

The following holds:

\displaystyle\emptyset;\emptyset;\emptyset\vdash(\nu\,a:C_{1})\,(\nu\,b:C_{2})\,R\;|\;S_{a}\;|\;S_{b}\triangleright\diamond

(11)

In the following typing derivations, we rely on the following two typing rules for polyadic elements; they can be derived from monadic typing rules
from
Figure

3
(see
Remark

A.1

for details):

(PolySess)

\Gamma;\emptyset;\widetilde{u}:\widetilde{S}\vdash\widetilde{u}\triangleright\widetilde{S}

\leadsto\in\{\multimap,\rightarrow\}

\Gamma;\Lambda;\Delta_{1}\vdash V\triangleright\widetilde{C}\leadsto\diamond

\Gamma;\emptyset;\Delta_{2}\vdash\widetilde{u}\triangleright\widetilde{C}

(PolyApp)

\Gamma;\Lambda;\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}\vdash V\,{\widetilde{u}}

Now, we detail the typing derivations that
show (
11
):

(
10
)

(PolySess)

a:C_{1},b:C_{2};\emptyset;y:S_{1},z:S_{2}\vdash y,z\triangleright S_{1},S_{2}

(PolyApp)

a:C_{1},b:C_{2};\emptyset;y:S_{1},z:S_{2}\vdash V\,{(y,z)}\triangleright\diamond

(12)

(
12
)

(Sess)

a:C_{1},b:C_{2};\emptyset;z:S_{2}\vdash z\triangleright S_{2}

(Abs)

a:C_{1},b:C_{2};\emptyset;y:S_{1}\vdash\lambda z.\,V\,{(y,z)}\triangleright S_{2}\!\multimap\!\diamond

(13)

(Nil)

a:C_{1},b:C_{2};\emptyset;\emptyset\vdash\mathbf{0}\triangleright\diamond

(Sh)

a:C_{1},b:C_{2};\emptyset;\emptyset\vdash b\triangleright\langle S_{2}\!\multimap\!\diamond\rangle

(
13
)

(Req)

a:C_{1},b:C_{2};\emptyset;y:S_{1}\vdash b!\langle\lambda z.\,V\,{(y,z)}\rangle\triangleright\diamond

(14)

(
14
)

(Sess)

a:C_{1},b:C_{2};\emptyset;y:S_{1}\vdash y\triangleright S_{1}

(Abs)

a:C_{1},b:C_{2};\emptyset;\emptyset\vdash{\lambda y.\,b!\langle\lambda z.\,V\,{(y,z)}\rangle}\triangleright S_{1}\!\multimap\!\diamond

(15)

a:C_{1},b:C_{2};\emptyset;\emptyset\vdash\mathbf{0}\triangleright\diamond

(Sh)

a:C_{1},b:C_{2};\emptyset;\emptyset\vdash a\triangleright\langle S_{1}\!\multimap\!\diamond\rangle

(
15
)

(Req)

a:C_{1},b:C_{2};\emptyset;\emptyset\vdash a!\big\langle\lambda y.\,b!\langle\lambda z.\,V\,{(y,z)}\rangle\big\rangle\triangleright\diamond

(16)

Finally we have:

(
16
)

a:C_{1},b:C_{2};\emptyset;\emptyset\vdash S_{a}\triangleright\diamond

(Par)

a:C_{1},b:C_{2};\emptyset;\emptyset\vdash R\;|\;S_{a}\triangleright\diamond

a:C_{1},b:C_{2};\emptyset;\emptyset\vdash S_{b}\triangleright\diamond

(Par)

a:C_{1},b:C_{2};\emptyset;\emptyset\vdash R\;|\;S_{a}\;|\;S_{b}\triangleright\diamond

(Res)

a:C_{1};\emptyset;\emptyset\vdash(\nu\,b:C_{2})\,R\;|\;S_{a}\;|\;S_{b}\triangleright\diamond

(Res)

\emptyset;\emptyset;\emptyset\vdash(\nu\,a:C_{1})\,(\nu\,b:C_{2})\,R\;|\;S_{a}\;|\;S_{b}\triangleright\diamond

In the above typing derivation, we remark that the judgments

\displaystyle a:C_{1},b:C_{2};\emptyset;\emptyset

\displaystyle\vdash S_{a}\triangleright\diamond

(17)

\displaystyle a:C_{1},b:C_{2};\emptyset;\emptyset

\displaystyle\vdash S_{b}\triangleright\diamond

(18)

are shown similarly as in (
6
)
from
Example

2.5
.
Indeed,
to derive (
17
) reusing
the derivation tree from (
6
)
we need to substitute
y
with
r
and then weaken
the shared environment (
6
)
with
b:C_{2}
(see
A.2
).
Similarly,
by substituting
a
with
b
and
y
with
r

in the derivation tree (
6
)
and
then by weakening the shared environment with
a:C_{1}
in
its conclusion we can obtain (
18
).

Notation 2.2

(Type Annotations)
.

We shall often annotate bound names and variables with their respective type.
We will write, e.g.,
(\nu\,s:S)\,P
to denote that the type of
s
in
P
is
S
.
Similarly for values: we shall write
\lambda u:C.\,P
.
Also, letting
\leadsto\in\{\multimap,\rightarrow\}
, we may write

\lambda u:{C}^{\leadsto}.\,P
to denote that the value is linear (if
\leadsto=\multimap
) or shared (if
\leadsto=\rightarrow
).
That is, we write
\lambda u:{C}^{\leadsto}.\,P
if

\Gamma;\Lambda;\Delta\vdash\lambda{u}.\,P\triangleright{{C}}\leadsto\diamond
, for some
\Gamma
,
\Lambda
, and
\Delta
.

Having introduced the core session process language
\mathsf{HO}
, we now move to detail its type-preserving decomposition into minimal session types.

3
Decomposing Session-Typed Processes

In this section we define the fragment of
minimal session types
and present a
decomposition
of well-typed processes: given a process
P
typable with (standard) session types, our decomposition returns a process, denoted
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P})}
, typable with minimal session types.

The definition of
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P})}
follows Parrow’s
trios
for the
\pi
-calculus
[
21
]
.
A trio is a process with exactly three sequential prefixes.
Roughly speaking, if
P
is a process with
k
sequential actions, then
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P})}
will contain
k
trios running in parallel: each of them will enact exactly one action from
P
.
The decomposition is carefully designed to ensure that trios trigger each other by preserving the sequencing in
P
.

This section is organized as follows.
First, in
Section

3.1
, we use examples to discuss some key ideas of the decomposition.
Then, in
Section

3.2
, we give the full definitions of minimal session types and the decomposition functions for types and processes, denoted
{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-})}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-})}
, respectively.
The former “slices” a session type
S
and returns a list of minimal session types, corresponding to individual actions in
S
; the latter breaks down an
\mathsf{HO}
process into a parallel composition of processes.
We demonstrate these notions on a number of examples in
Section

3.3
.
Finally, in
Section

3.4
we establish the
static correctness
result (
3.1
):
if
P
is well-typed
under session types
S_{1},\ldots,S_{n}
, then
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P})}
is typable using the
minimal session types
{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S_{1}})},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S_{n}})}
.
The issue of dynamic correctness, i.e., the operational correspondence between
P
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P})}
, is treated separately in
Section

4
.

Remark 3.1

(Color Convention)
.

We use colors to differentiate the operations on processes (in
pink
) and on types (in
green
).
The usage of the colors is for visual aid only, and is not important for the mathematical content of the presented material.

3.1
Key Ideas

Source process
P_{1}
:
P_{1}
P_{2}
P_{3}
\mathbf{0}
u:?(\mathsf{str})
u:?(\mathsf{int})
u:!\langle\mathsf{bool}\rangle
Decomposed process
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{1}})}
:
Q_{1}
Q^{\prime}_{1}
u_{1}:?(\mathsf{str})
\parallel
Q_{2}
Q^{\prime}_{2}
u_{2}:?(\mathsf{int})
\parallel
Q_{3}
Q^{\prime}_{3}
u_{3}:!\langle\mathsf{bool}\rangle
\parallel
Q_{4}
x:\mathsf{str}
x:\mathsf{str}
,
y:\mathsf{int}

Figure 4
:
Our decomposition function

{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-})}
, illustrated. Nodes
represent process states, ‘
\parallel
’ represents
parallel composition of processes, black arrows stand for actions, and red arrows
indicate synchronizations that preserve the sequentiality of the source process by activating trios and propagating (bound) values.

Consider a process
P_{1}
that implements the (standard) session type
S=?(\mathsf{str}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}?(\mathsf{int}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}!\langle\mathsf{bool}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}
along name
u
.
In
P_{1}
, name
u
is not a single-use resource; rather, it is used several times to implement the communication actions in
S
;
Figure

4
(top) graphically depicts the actions and the corresponding states.

The decomposition
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{1}})}
is illustrated
in the bottom part of
Figure

4
: it is defined as the parallel composition of four processes

Q_{i}
(for
i\in\{1,\ldots,4\}
).
Each process
Q_{1}
,
Q_{2}
, and
Q_{3}
mimic one action of
P_{1}
on an indexed name
u_{i}
, while
Q_{4}
simulates the termination of the session.
This way, a single name
u
in
P_{1}
is decomposed into a sequence of names
u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}
in
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{1}})}
.

The processes
Q_{1}
,
Q_{2}
,
Q_{3}
, and
Q_{4}
are composed in parallel, but we would like to retain the same sequentiality of actions on the channels
u_{i}
as we have on the channel
u
.
To that end, each process
Q_{i}
, with the exception of
Q_{1}
, does not perform its designated action on
u_{i}
until it gets activated by the previous process.
In turn, after
Q_{i}
performs an action on
u_{i}
it evolves to a state
Q^{\prime}_{i}
, which is responsible for activating the next process
Q_{i+1}
.
In
Figure

4
, the activations are indicated by red arrows.
In general, the decomposition orchestrates the activation of sub-processes, following the sequencing prescribed by the session types of the given process.
Therefore, assuming a well-typed source process, our decomposition codifies the sequentiality in
session types into the process level.

The activation mechanism includes the
propagation
of values across sub-processes (cf. the labels on red arrows).
This establishes a flow of values from sub-processes binding them to those that use them (i.e., it makes variable bindings explicit). For example, in
P_{1}
, the Boolean value being sent over as part of the session
S
might depend on the previously received string and integer values.
Therefore, both of those values have to be propagated to the process
Q_{3}
, which is responsible for sending out the Boolean.

In this example a single name
u:S
is decomposed into a sequence
\widetilde{u}=(u_{1},\ldots,u_{n})
: each
u_{i}\in\widetilde{u}
is a single-use resource, as prescribed by its minimal session type.
Such is the case for non-recursive types
S
.
When
S
is recursive, the situation is more interesting: each action of
S
can be repeated many times, and therefore the names
\widetilde{u}
should be propagated across trios to enable potentially many uses.
As an example, consider the recursive session type

S=\mu\mathsf{t}.?(\mathsf{int}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}!\langle\mathsf{\mathsf{int}}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}{\mathsf{t}}
, in which an input and an output actions are repeated indefinitely.
Consider the following process

\displaystyle R_{1}=\underbrace{r?(z).}_{{T_{1}}}\underbrace{r!\langle-z\rangle.}_{{T_{2}}}\underbrace{r?(z).}_{{T_{3}}}\underbrace{r!\langle z\rangle.}_{{T_{4}}}\underbrace{V\,{r}}_{{T_{5}}}

which makes use of the channel
r:S
and where
V
has type
S\!\rightarrow\!\diamond
.

Figure

5
(top) gives the first four actions of
R_{1}
and the corresponding sates: the body of type
S
prescribes two actions on name
r
, performed sequentially in
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
; subsequent actions (enabled in
R_{3}

and
R_{4}
) correspond to a “new instance” of the body of
S
.

The decomposition
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}R_{1}})}
, depicted in
Figure

5
(bottom), generates a trio for each prefix in
R_{1}
; we denote prefixes with their corresponding trios
{T_{1}},\ldots,{T_{5}}
.
The type decomposition function on types,
{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-})}
, slices
S
into two
minimal tail-recursive types
:
M_{1}=\mu\mathsf{t}.?(\textsf{int}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathsf{t}
and
M_{2}=\mu\mathsf{t}.!\langle\textsf{int}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathsf{t}
.

In the recursive case, a key idea is that trios that mimic actions prescribed by a recursive session types should reuse names, which should be propagated across trios.
This way, for instance, trios
T_{1}
and
T_{3}
mimic the same (input) action, and so they both should use the same name (
r_{1}
).
To achieve this, we devise a mechanism that propagates names with tail-recursive types (such as
(r_{1},r_{2})
) through the trios.
These propagation actions are represented by blue arrows in
Figure

5
(bottom).
In our example,
T_{3}
gathers the complete decomposition of names from preceding trios
(r_{1},r_{2})
; it mimics an input action on
r_{1}
and makes
(r_{1},r_{2})
available to future trios (i.e.,
T_{4}
and
T_{5}
).

Since the same tail-recursive names can be (re)used infinitely often, we propagate tail-recursive names through the following process.
All the names
{\widetilde{r}}
corresponding to the decomposition of a tail-recursive name
r
are bound in the process

c^{r}?(x).x\,{\widetilde{r}},

which is similar to the servers discussed in
Example

2.2
.
We call these processes
recursive propagators
, and each tail-recursive name in the original process
P
has a dedicated propagator in
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P})}
on the channel
c^{r}
.
Whenever a trio has to perform an action
\alpha(r_{i})
on one of the decomposed tail-recursive names (i.e., a decomposition of an input action ‘
r?(y).
’ or an output action ‘
r!\langle V\rangle.
’ on the name
r
), it first has to request the name from the corresponding recursive propagator by performing an output action
c^{r}!\big\langle N\big\rangle
, where value
N
is the abstraction

N=\lambda\widetilde{z}.\,\alpha({z_{i}}).\big(\overline{c_{k+1}}!\langle\widetilde{w}\rangle\;|\;c^{r}?(x).x\,{\widetilde{z}}\big).

A synchronization on
c^{r}
will result in the reduction:

c^{r}?(x).x\,{\widetilde{r}}\;|\;c^{r}!\big\langle N\big\rangle\longrightarrow\alpha({r_{i}}).\big(\overline{c_{k+1}}!\langle\widetilde{w}\rangle\;|\;c^{r}?(x).x\,{\widetilde{r}}\big).

The resulting process first simulates
\alpha(r)
and subsequently reinstates the recursive propagator on
c^{r}
, for the benefit of the other trios requiring access to the names
\widetilde{r}
.
See
Examples

3.9
and
3.10
below (Page
3.9
) for further illustration of this method.

This decomposition strategy handles
\mathsf{HO}
processes with recursive types which are
simple
and
contractive
.
That is, recursive types of the form
\mu\mathsf{t}.S
, where the body
S\neq\mathsf{t}
does not itself contain recursive types.
Unless stated otherwise, we consider
tail-recursive
session types such as, e.g.,
S=\mu\mathsf{t}.?(\mathsf{int}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}?(\mathsf{bool}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}!\langle\mathsf{bool}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathsf{t}
.
Non-tail-recursive session types such as
\mu\mathsf{t}.?((\widetilde{T},\mathsf{t})\!\rightarrow\!\diamond){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}
, used in the fully-abstract encoding of
\mathsf{HO}\pi
into
\mathsf{HO}

[
18
]
, can also be accommodated; see
Example

3.3
below.

Source process
R_{1}
:
R_{1}
R_{2}
R_{3}
R_{4}
R_{5}
r:?(\mathsf{int})
r:!\langle\mathsf{int}\rangle
r:?(\mathsf{int})
r:!\langle\mathsf{int}\rangle
Decomposed process
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}R_{1}})}
:
T_{1}

\parallel

\parallel

\parallel
T_{4}
T^{\prime}_{4}

\parallel
T_{5}

,
r_{2}
r_{1}
,
r_{2}
r_{1}
,
r_{2}
r_{1}
,
