Figure 5
:
Decomposition of processes with recursive session types, illustrated. Dashed blue arrows represent the propagation of tail-recursive names (
r_{1}
,
r_{2}
) across trios.

3.2
The Decomposition

Here we formally present the decomposition of
\mathsf{HO}
processes.
We start introducing some preliminary definitions, including the definition of an auxiliary function, called the
breakdown function
.

Following Parrow
[
21
]
we adopt some useful terminology
and notation on trios.
The
context
of a trio is a tuple of variables

\widetilde{x}
, possibly empty, which makes variable bindings explicit.
We use
a reserved set of
propagator names
(or simply
propagators
),
denoted with
c_{k},c_{k+1},\ldots
, to carry contexts and trigger the
subsequent trio. A process with less than three sequential prefixes is called a

degenerate trio
. Also, a
leading trio
is the one that receives a
context, performs an action, and triggers the next trio; a
control trio

only activates other trios.

The breakdown function works on both processes and values. The breakdown of
process
P
is denoted by
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{x}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\big)}
, where
k
is the index for the
propagators
c_{k}
, and
\widetilde{x}
is the context to be received by the
previous trio. Similarly, the breakdown of a value
V
is denoted by

{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{x}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V}\big)}
.

3.2.1
Minimal Session Types and Decomposing Types

We start by introducing minimal session types as a fragment of
Definition

2.2
:

Definition 3.1

(Minimal Session Types (MSTs))
.

The syntax of
minimal session types
for
\mathsf{HO}
is defined as follows:

\displaystyle U

\displaystyle\;::=\;\widetilde{C}\!\rightarrow\!\diamond\mathrel{\big|}\widetilde{C}\!\multimap\!\diamond

\displaystyle C

\displaystyle\;::=\;M\mathrel{\big|}{\langle U\rangle}

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle\;::=\;\gamma\mathrel{\big|}\penalty\ !\langle\widetilde{U}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\gamma\mathrel{\big|}\penalty\ ?(\widetilde{U}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\gamma\mathrel{\big|}\mu\mathsf{t}.M

\displaystyle\gamma

\displaystyle\;::=\;\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}\mathrel{\big|}\mathsf{t}

The above definition is minimal in its use of sequencing, which is only present in
recursive session types such as
\mu\mathsf{t}.!\langle U\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathsf{t}
and

\mu\mathsf{t}.?(U){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathsf{t}
—these are tail-recursive session types with exactly one session prefix.
Clearly, this minimal type structure induces a reduced set of typable
\mathsf{HO}
processes.
A type system for
\mathsf{HO}
with minimal session types can be straightforwardly obtained by specializing the definitions, typing rules, and results summarized in
Section

2.2
.

We refer to
\mathsf{HO}
processes and terms typeable with minimal session types as
MST processes and terms
, respectively.

We now define how to “slice” a standard session type into a
list
of
minimal session types.
We need the following auxiliary definition.

Definition 3.2

(Predicates on Types and Names)
.

Let
C
be a channel type.

•

We write
\mathsf{tr}(C)
to indicate that
C
is a tail-recursive session type.

•

Given
u:C
, we write
\mathsf{lin}(u)
if a session type (i.e.
C=S
for some
S
) that is not tail recursive.

With a slight abuse of notation, we write
\mathsf{tr}(u)
to mean
u:C
and
\mathsf{tr}(C)
(and similarly for
\neg\mathsf{tr}(u)
).

Definition 3.3

(Decomposing Session Types)
.

Given the session, higher-order, and shared types of
Definition

2.2
, the
type decomposition function

{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-})}
is defined using the auxiliary function
{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{R}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-})}
as in
Figure

6
.
We write
|{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S})}|
to denote the length of
{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S})}
(and similarly for
{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{R}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-})}
).

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}!\langle U\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}{S}})}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}!\langle{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}U})}\rangle&\text{if $S=\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}$}\\
!\langle{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}U})}\rangle\,,{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S})}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}?(U){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}{S}})}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}?({\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}U})})&\text{if $S=\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}$}\\
?({\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}U})})\,,{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S})}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mu\mathsf{t}.S})}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{R}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S})}&\text{if $\mathsf{tr}(\mu\mathsf{t}.S)$}\\
\mu\mathsf{t}.{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S})}&\text{if $\neg\mathsf{tr}(\mu\mathsf{t}.S)$ and ${\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S})}$ is a singleton}\end{cases}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}})}

\displaystyle=\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{t}})}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{t}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C\!\multimap\!\diamond})}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C})}\!\multimap\!\diamond

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C\!\rightarrow\!\diamond})}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C})}\!\rightarrow\!\diamond

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\langle U\rangle})}

\displaystyle=\langle{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}U})}\rangle

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{R}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{t}})}

\displaystyle=\epsilon

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{R}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}!\langle U\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}S})}

\displaystyle=\mu\mathsf{t}.!\langle{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}U})}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathsf{t},{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{R}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S})}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{R}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}?(U){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}S})}

\displaystyle=\mu\mathsf{t}.?({\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}U})}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathsf{t},{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{R}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S})}

Figure 6
:
Decomposing session types into minimal session types (
Definition

3.3
)

The decomposition is self-explanatory;
intuitively, if a session type
S
contains
k
input/output actions, the list
{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S})}
will contain
k
minimal session types.
For a tail recursive
\mu\mathsf{t}.S
,
{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mu\mathsf{t}.S})}
is a list of minimal recursive session types, obtained
using the auxiliary function
{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{R}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-})}
on
S
: if
S
has
k
prefixes
then the list
{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mu\mathsf{t}.S})}
will contain
k
minimal recursive session types.

We illustrate
Definition

3.3
with three examples.

Example 3.1

(Decomposition a Non-recursive Type)
.

Let
S=?(\mathsf{int}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}?(\mathsf{int}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}!\langle\mathsf{bool}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}
be the session type given in
Section

1
.
Then
{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S})}
denotes the list
?(\mathsf{int})\,,?(\mathsf{int})\,,!\langle\mathsf{bool}\rangle
.

\lhd

Example 3.2

(Decomposing a Recursive Type)
.

Let
S=\mu\mathsf{t}.S^{\prime}
be a recursive session type, with

S^{\prime}=?(\mathsf{int}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}?(\mathsf{bool}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}!\langle\mathsf{bool}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathsf{t}
.
By
Definition

3.3
, since
S
is tail-recursive,
{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S})}={\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{R}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S^{\prime}})}
.
Further,
{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{R}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S^{\prime}})}=\mu\mathsf{t}.?(\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}int})}}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathsf{t},{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{R}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}?(\mathsf{bool}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}!\langle\mathsf{bool}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathsf{t}})}
. By definition of

{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{R}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-})}
, we obtain

{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S})}=\mu\mathsf{t}.?(\mathsf{int}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathsf{t},\penalty\ \mu\mathsf{t}.?(\mathsf{bool}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathsf{t},\penalty\ \mu\mathsf{t}.!\langle\mathsf{bool}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathsf{t},{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{R}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t})}

(using
{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{int}})}=\mathsf{int}
and

{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{bool}})}=\mathsf{bool}
). Since
{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{R}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{t}})}=\epsilon
, we
obtain

{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S})}=\mu\mathsf{t}.?(\mathsf{int}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathsf{t},\penalty\ \mu\mathsf{t}.?(\mathsf{bool}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathsf{t},\penalty\ \mu\mathsf{t}.!\langle\mathsf{bool}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathsf{t}

\lhd

In addition to tail-recursive types that are handled by
{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{R}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-})}
, we need to
support non-tail-recursive types of form
\mu\mathsf{t}.?((\widetilde{T},\mathsf{t})\!\rightarrow\!\diamond){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}
that are essential for the encoding of recursion in
\mathsf{HO}\pi
into
\mathsf{HO}
.
The following example illustrates such a decomposition.

Example 3.3

(Decomposing a Non-tail-recursive Type)
.

Let

S=\mu\mathsf{t}.?((?(\mathsf{str}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}!\langle\mathsf{str}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}},\mathsf{t})\!\rightarrow\!\diamond){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}

be a non-tail-recursive type. We obtain the following decomposition:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S})}

\displaystyle=\mu\mathsf{t}.{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}?((?(\mathsf{str}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}!\langle\mathsf{str}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}},\mathsf{t})\!\rightarrow\!\diamond){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}})}

\displaystyle=\mu\mathsf{t}.{?({{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(?(\mathsf{str}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}!\langle\mathsf{str}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}},\mathsf{t})\!\rightarrow\!\diamond})}})}

\displaystyle=\mu\mathsf{t}.?({{{(?(\mathsf{str}),!\langle\mathsf{str}\rangle,\mathsf{t})\!\rightarrow\!\diamond}}})=M

We can see that we have generated
minimal non-tail-recursive type
M
.

\lhd

Now, we illustrate the encoding of
\mathsf{HO}\pi
recursive
processes into
\mathsf{HO}
from
[
18
]
using the
non-tail-recursive type
S
given in the above example.

Example 3.4

(Encoding Recursion)
.

Consider the process

P=\mu X.a?(m).a!\langle m\rangle.X
, which contains recursion and so it is not an
\mathsf{HO}
process.
Still,
P
can be encoded into
\mathsf{HO}
as follows
[
18
]
:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.36,0.54,0.66}\llbracket}P{\color[rgb]{0.36,0.54,0.66}\rrbracket}=a?(m).a!\langle m\rangle.(\nu\,s)\,{(V\,{(a,s)}\;|\;\overline{s}!\langle V\rangle)}

where the value
V
is an abstraction that potentially reduces to
{\color[rgb]{0.36,0.54,0.66}\llbracket}P{\color[rgb]{0.36,0.54,0.66}\rrbracket}
:

\displaystyle V=\lambda(x_{a},y_{1}).\,y_{1}?(z_{x}).x_{a}?(m).x_{a}!\langle m\rangle.(\nu\,s)\,{(z_{x}\,{(x_{a},s)}\;|\;\overline{s}!\langle z_{x}\rangle.\mathbf{0})}

As detailed in
[
18
]
, this encoding relies on non-tail-recursive types.
In particular, the bound name
s
in
{\color[rgb]{0.36,0.54,0.66}\llbracket}P{\color[rgb]{0.36,0.54,0.66}\rrbracket}
is typed
with the following type, discussed above in
Example

3.3
:

\displaystyle S=\mu\mathsf{t}.?((?(\mathsf{str}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}!\langle\mathsf{str}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}},\mathsf{t})\!\rightarrow\!\diamond){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}

We compose
{\color[rgb]{0.36,0.54,0.66}\llbracket}P{\color[rgb]{0.36,0.54,0.66}\rrbracket}
with an appropriate client process to illustrate the encoding of recursion. Below
R
stands for some unspecified process such that
a\in\mathtt{rn}(R)
:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.36,0.54,0.66}\llbracket}P{\color[rgb]{0.36,0.54,0.66}\rrbracket}\;|\;a!\langle W\rangle.a?(b).R

\displaystyle\longrightarrow^{2}(\nu\,s)\,{(V\,{(a,s)}\;|\;\overline{s}!\langle V\rangle)}\;|\;R

\displaystyle\longrightarrow(\nu\,s)\,{(s?(z_{x}).a?(m).a!\langle m\rangle.(\nu\,s^{\prime})\,{(z_{x}\,{(a,s^{\prime})}\;|\;\overline{s^{\prime}}!\langle z_{x}\rangle)}\;|\;\overline{s}!\langle V\rangle)}\;|\;R

\displaystyle\longrightarrow a?(m).a!\langle m\rangle.(\nu\,s^{\prime})\,{(V\,{(a,s^{\prime})}\;|\;\overline{s^{\prime}}!\langle V\rangle)}\;|\;R

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.36,0.54,0.66}\llbracket}P{\color[rgb]{0.36,0.54,0.66}\rrbracket}\;|\;R

3.2.2
Decomposing Processes

As we have seen, each session type
S
is decomposed into
{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S})}
, a list of minimal session types.
Accordingly, given an assignment
s:S
, we decompose
s
into a series of names, one for each action in
S
.
We use
indexed names
to formalize the names used by minimally typed processes.
Formally, an indexed name is a pair
(n,i)
with
i\in\mathbb{N}
, which we denote as
n_{i}
.
We refer to processes with indexed names as
indexed processes
.

The decomposition of processes is defined in
Definition

3.9
, and it relies on a breakdown function, denoted
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{x}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-}\big)}
, which operates on indexed processes.
Before we dive into those functions we present some auxiliary definitions.

Preliminaries.

To handle the unfolding of recursive types, we shall use the following auxiliary function, which decomposes guarded recursive types, by first ignoring all the actions until the recursion.

Definition 3.4

(Decomposing an Unfolded Recursive Type)
.

Let
S
be a session type. The function
{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{R}^{\star}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-})}
: is defined as follows

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{R}^{\star}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mu\mathsf{t}.S})}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{R}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S})}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{R}^{\star}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}?(U){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}S})}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{R}^{\star}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S})}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{R}^{\star}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}!\langle U\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}S})}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{R}^{\star}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S})}

Example 3.5
.

Let
T=?(\mathsf{bool}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}!\langle\mathsf{bool}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}S
be a derived unfolding
of
S
from
Example

3.2
. Then, by
Definition

3.3
,

{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{R}^{\star}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T})}
is the list of minimal recursive types obtained as follows:
first,
{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{R}^{\star}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T})}={\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{R}^{\star}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}!\langle\mathsf{bool}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mu\mathsf{t}.S^{\prime}})}
and after
one more step,
{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{R}^{\star}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}!\langle\mathsf{bool}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mu\mathsf{t}.S^{\prime}})}={\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{R}^{\star}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mu\mathsf{t}.S^{\prime}})}
. Finally, we have
{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{R}^{\star}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mu\mathsf{t}.S^{\prime}})}={\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{R}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S^{\prime}})}
.
We get the same list of minimal types as in
Example

3.2
:
{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{R}^{\star}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T})}=\mu\mathsf{t}.?(\mathsf{int}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathsf{t},\mu\mathsf{t}.?(\mathsf{bool}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathsf{t},\mu\mathsf{t}.!\langle\mathsf{bool}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathsf{t}
.

\lhd

Given an unfolded recursive session type
S
, the auxiliary function
{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S}\rangle}
returns the position of the top-most prefix of
S
within its body.

Definition 3.5

(Index function)
.

Let
S
be an (unfolded) recursive session type. The function
{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S}\rangle}
is defined as follows:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S}\rangle}=\begin{cases}{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S^{\prime}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$S$}\!/\mbox{\small$\mathsf{t}$}\}}\rangle^{\star}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}}&\text{if}\ S=\mu\mathsf{t}.S^{\prime}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S}\rangle^{\star}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

where
{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S}\rangle^{\star}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}l}}}
:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mu\mathsf{t}.S}\rangle^{\star}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}l}}}

\displaystyle=|{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{R}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S})}|-l+1

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}!\langle U\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}S}\rangle^{\star}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}l}}}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S}\rangle^{\star}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}l+1}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}?(U){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}S}\rangle^{\star}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}l}}}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S}\rangle^{\star}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}l+1}}}

Example 3.6
.

Let

S^{\prime}=?(\mathsf{bool}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}!\langle\mathsf{bool}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}S
where

S
is as in
Example

3.2
.
Then
{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S^{\prime}}\rangle}=2
since the top-most prefix of
S^{\prime}
(‘
?(\mathsf{bool}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}
’) is
the second prefix in the body of
S
.

\lhd

In order to determine the required number of propagators (
c_{k},c_{k+1},\ldots
) required in the breakdown of processes and values, we define the
degree
of a process:

Definition 3.6

(Degree of a Process)
.

Let
P
be an
\mathsf{HO}
process.
The
degree
of
P
, denoted
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\lbag{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\rbag}
, is defined as
follows:

{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\lbag{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\rbag}=\begin{cases}{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\lbag{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}\rbag}+1&\text{if $P=u_{i}!\langle V\rangle.Q$ or $P=u_{i}?(y).Q$}\\
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\lbag{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}\rbag}&\text{if $P=(\nu\,s:S)\,P^{\prime}$}\\
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\lbag{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}\rbag}+{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\lbag{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}R}\rbag}+1&\text{if $P=Q\;|\;R$}\\
1&\text{if $P=V\,{u_{i}}$ or $P=\mathbf{0}$}\end{cases}

We define an auxiliary function that “initializes” the indices of a tuple of
names, for turning a regular process into an indexed process.

Definition 3.7

(Initializing an indexed process)
.

Let
\widetilde{u}=(a,b,s,s^{\prime},r,r^{\prime},\ldots)
be a finite tuple of names.
We shall write
\mathsf{init}(\widetilde{u})
to denote the tuple of indexed names

(a_{1},b_{1},s_{1},s^{\prime}_{1},r_{1},r_{1}^{\prime},\ldots)
.

Definition 3.8

(Subsequent index substitution)
.

Let
n_{i}
be an indexed name. We define
\mathsf{next}(n_{i})=(\mathsf{lin}(n_{i}))\,\mathbf{?}\,\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$n_{i+1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$n_{i}$}\}\mathbf{:}\,\{\}
.

Remark 3.2
.

Recall that we write
‘
c_{k}?()
’ and ‘
\overline{c_{k}}!\langle\rangle
’
to denote input and output prefixes in which the value communicated along
c_{k}
is not relevant.
While ‘
c_{k}?()
’ stands for ‘
c_{k}?(x)
’,
‘
\overline{c_{k}}!\langle\rangle
’ stands for ‘
\overline{c_{k}}!\langle\lambda x.\,\mathbf{0}\rangle
’.
Their corresponding minimal types are

?(\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}\!\rightarrow\!\diamond)

and

!\langle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}\!\rightarrow\!\diamond\rangle
,
which are
denoted by

?(-)
and
!\langle-\rangle
, respectively.

Given a typed process
P
, we write
\mathtt{rn}(P)
to denote the set of free names of

P
whose types are recursive. As mentioned above, for each
r\in\mathtt{rn}(P)
with

r:{S}
we shall rely on a control trio of the form

c^{r}?(x).x\,{\widetilde{r}}
, where
\widetilde{r}=r_{1},\ldots,r_{|{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S})}|}
.

Definition 3.9

(Decomposition of a Process)
.

Let
P
be a closed
\mathsf{HO}
process with
\widetilde{u}=\mathtt{fn}(P)
and
\widetilde{v}=\mathtt{rn}(P)
.
The
decomposition
of
P
, denoted
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P})}
, is
defined as:

{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P})}=(\nu\,\widetilde{c})\,(\nu\,\widetilde{c}_{r})\,\Big(\overline{c_{k}}!\langle\rangle.\mathbf{0}\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\sigma}\big)}\;|\;\prod_{r\in\tilde{v}}c^{r}?(x).x\,{\widetilde{r}}\Big)

where:
k>0
;

\widetilde{c}=(c_{k},\ldots,c_{k+{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\lbag{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\rbag}-1})
;

\widetilde{c_{r}}=\bigcup_{r\in\tilde{v}}c^{r}
;

\sigma=\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathsf{init}(\widetilde{u})$}\!/\mbox{\small$\widetilde{u}$}\}
.

Notice that when
\mathtt{rn}(P)=\emptyset
, then

{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P})}=(\nu\,\widetilde{c})\,(\overline{c_{k}}!\langle\rangle.\mathbf{0}\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\sigma}\big)})
.
We now discuss the breakdown of process
P
, denoted
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{x}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\big)}
.

The Breakdown Function.

Given a context
\widetilde{x}
and a
k>0
, the breakdown of an indexed
process
P
, denoted
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{x}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\big)}
, is defined recursively on the structure of processes. The definition of
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{x}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-}\big)}
relies on an auxiliary
breakdown function on values, denoted
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{x}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-}\big)}
.
When
V=y
, then the breakdown function is simply the identity:

{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{x}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}y}\big)}=y
.

P

{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{x}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\big)}

u_{i}!\langle V\rangle.{Q}

\bullet\penalty\ \neg\mathsf{tr}(S)
:

c_{k}?(\widetilde{x}).u_{i}!\big\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{y}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V\sigma}\big)}\big\rangle.\overline{c_{k+1}}!\langle\widetilde{w}\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k+1}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{w}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q\sigma}\big)}

\bullet\penalty\ \mathsf{tr}(S)
:

c_{k}?(\widetilde{x}).c^{u}!\big\langle N_{V}\big\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k+1}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{w}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}\big)}

where:

N_{V}=\lambda\widetilde{z}.\,z_{{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S}\rangle}}!\big\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{y}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V}\big)}\big\rangle.{}

\big(\overline{c_{k+1}}!\langle\widetilde{w}\rangle\;|\;c^{u}?(x).x\,{\widetilde{z}}\big)

u_{i}:S

\widetilde{y}=\mathtt{fv}(V)

\widetilde{w}=\mathtt{fv}(Q)

\sigma=\mathsf{next}(u_{i})

\widetilde{z}=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{|{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{R}^{\star}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S})}|})

u_{i}?(y).Q

\bullet\penalty\ \mathsf{tr}(S)
:

c_{k}?(\widetilde{x}).u_{i}?(y).\overline{c_{k+1}}!\langle\widetilde{w}\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k+1}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{w}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q\sigma}\big)}

\bullet\penalty\ \neg\mathsf{tr}(S)
:

c_{k}?(\widetilde{x}).c^{u}!\big\langle N_{y}\big\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k+1}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{w}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}\big)}

\widetilde{c}=(c_{1},\ldots,c_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\lbag{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\rbag}})

\widetilde{c_{r}}=\bigcup_{r\in\tilde{y}}c^{r}

Table 1
:
The breakdown function for processes and values.

The breakdown function relies on type information, in two ways.
First, names are decomposed based on their session types.
Second, for most constructs the shape of decomposed process depends on whether the associated session type is tail-recursive or not.
The definition of the breakdown function is given in
Table

1
.
Next, we describe each of the cases of the definition.
In
Section

3.3
(Page
3.3
) we develop several examples.

Output:

The decomposition of
u_{i}!\langle V\rangle.Q
is arguably the most interesting case, as both the sent value
V
and the continuation
Q
have to be decomposed.
We distinguish two cases:

•

If
\neg\mathsf{tr}(u_{i})
then
u_{i}
is linear or shared, and then we have:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\tilde{x}}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}u_{i}!\langle V\rangle.Q}\big)}=c_{k}?(\widetilde{x}).u_{i}!\big\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{y}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V\sigma}\big)}\big\rangle.\overline{c_{k+1}}!\langle\widetilde{w}\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k+1}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{w}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q\sigma}\big)}

This decomposition consists of a leading trio that
mimics an output action in parallel with the breakdown of
Q
.
The context
\widetilde{x}
must include the free variables of
V
and
Q
, which are
denoted
\widetilde{y}
and
\widetilde{w}
, respectively. These tuples are not
necessarily disjoint: variables with shared types can appear free in both
V

and
Q
. The value
V
is then broken down with parameters
\widetilde{y}
and
k+1
; the latter serves to consistently generate propagators for the
trios in the breakdown of
V
, denoted
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{y}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V\sigma}\big)}
(see below).
The substitution
\sigma
increments the index of session
names; it is applied to both
V
and
Q
before they are broken down.
By taking
\sigma=\mathsf{next}(u_{i})
we
distinguish two cases (see
Definition

3.8
):

–

If name
u_{i}
is linear (i.e., it has a session type) then its future
occurrences are renamed into
u_{i+1}
, and
\sigma=\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$u_{i+1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$u_{i}$}\}
;

–

Otherwise, if
u_{i}
is shared, then
\sigma=\{\}
.

Note that if
u_{i}
is linear then it appears either in
V
or
Q
and
\sigma

affects only one of them. The last prefix activates the
breakdown of
Q
with its corresponding context
\widetilde{w}
.

In case
V=y
, the same strategy applies; because
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{y}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}y\sigma}\big)}=y
, we have:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{x}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}u_{i}!\langle y\rangle.Q}\big)}=c_{k}?(\widetilde{x}).u_{i}!\langle y\rangle.\overline{c_{k+1}}!\langle\widetilde{w}\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k+1}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{w}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q\sigma}\big)}

Notice that variable
y
is not propagated further if it does not appear free in
Q
.

•

If
\mathsf{tr}(u_{i})
then
u_{i}
is tail-recursive and then we have:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{x}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}u_{i}!\langle V\rangle.Q}\big)}=\penalty\ \penalty\

\displaystyle c_{k}?(\widetilde{x}).c^{u}!\big\langle N_{V}\big\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k+1}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{w}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}\big)}

\displaystyle\text{where:}\penalty\ \penalty\ N_{V}=\penalty\ \penalty\

\displaystyle\lambda\widetilde{z}.\,z_{{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S}\rangle}}!\big\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{y}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V}\big)}\big\rangle.\big(\overline{c_{k+1}}!\langle\widetilde{w}\rangle\;|\;c^{u}?(x).x\,{\widetilde{z}}\big)

The decomposition consists of a leading trio that mimics the output action
running in parallel with the breakdown of
Q
. After receiving the context

\widetilde{x}
, the leading trio sends an abstraction
N_{V}
along
c^{u}
, which
performs several tasks. First,
N_{V}
collects the sequence of names
\tilde{u}
;
then, it mimics the output action of
P
along one of such names (
u_{{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S}\rangle}}
) and
triggers the next trio, with context
\widetilde{w}
; finally, it reinstates the
server on
c^{u}
for the next trio that uses
u
.
Notice that indexing is not relevant in this case.

In case
V=y
, we have
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{y}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}y\sigma}\big)}=y
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\lbag{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}y}\rbag}=0
, hence:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{x}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}u_{i}!\langle y\rangle.Q}\big)}=c_{k}?(\widetilde{x}).c^{u}!\big\langle\lambda\widetilde{z}.\,z_{{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S}\rangle}}!\langle y\rangle.\big(\overline{c_{k+1}}!\langle\widetilde{w}\rangle\;|\;c^{u}?(x).x\,{\widetilde{z}}\big)\big\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k+1}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{w}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}\big)}

Input:

To decompose a process
u_{i}?(y).Q
we distinguish two cases, as before:
(i) name
u_{i}
is linear or shared or (ii) tail-recursive.
In case (i), the breakdown is defined as follows:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{x}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}u_{i}?(y).Q}\big)}=c_{k}?(\widetilde{x}).u_{i}?(y).\overline{c_{k+1}}!\langle\widetilde{w}\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k+1}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{w}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q\sigma}\big)}

where
\widetilde{w}=\mathtt{fv}(Q)
.
A leading trio mimics the input action and possibly extends the context with the
received variable
y
. The substitution
\sigma
is defined as in the output
case.

In case (ii), when
u_{i}
has tail-recursive session type
S
, the decomposition is as in the output case:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{x}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}u_{i}?(y).Q}\big)}=c_{k}?(\widetilde{x}).c^{u}!\big\langle\lambda\widetilde{z}.\,z_{{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S}\rangle}}?(y).\big(\overline{c_{k+1}}!\langle\widetilde{w}\rangle\;|\;c^{u}?(x).x\,{\widetilde{z}}\big)\big\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k+1}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{w}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}\big)}

Application:

For simplicity we consider the breakdown of applications of the form
V\,{(\widetilde{r},u_{i})}
, where
every
r_{i}\in\widetilde{r}
is such that
\mathsf{tr}(r_{i})
and only
u_{i}
is such that
\neg\mathsf{tr}(u_{i})
.
The general case (involving different orders in names and multiple names with non-recursive types) is similar. We have:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{x}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V\,{(\widetilde{r},u_{i})}}\big)}=

\displaystyle c_{k}?(\widetilde{x}).\overbracket{c^{r_{1}}!\big\langle\lambda\widetilde{z}_{1}.c^{r_{2}}!\langle\lambda\widetilde{z}_{2}.\cdots.c^{r_{n}}!\langle\lambda\widetilde{z}_{n}.}^{n=|\tilde{r}|}{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{x}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V}\big)}\,{(\widetilde{z}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{z}_{n},\widetilde{m})}\rangle\,\rangle\big\rangle

Let us first discuss how names in
(\widetilde{r},u_{i})
are decomposed using types.
Letting
|\tilde{r}|=n
and
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
, for each
r_{i}\in\widetilde{r}
(with

r_{i}:S_{i}
) we generate a sequence
\widetilde{z}_{i}=(z^{i}_{1},\ldots,z^{i}_{|{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{R}^{\star}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S_{i}})}|})
as in the output case.
We decompose name
u_{i}
(with
u_{i}:C
) as
\widetilde{m}=(u_{i},\ldots,u_{i+|{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C})}|-1})
.

The decomposition first receives a context
\widetilde{x}
for value
V
: we break down
V

with
\widetilde{x}
as a context since these variables need to be propagated to the
abstracted process.
Subsequently, an output on
c^{r_{1}}
sends a value containing
n
abstractions that occur nested
within output prefixes—this is similar to the mechanism for partial instantiation shown in
Example

2.3
.
For each
j\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
, each abstraction
binds
\widetilde{z}_{j}
and sends the next abstraction along
c^{r_{j+1}}
.
The innermost abstraction abstracts over
\widetilde{z}_{n}
and encloses
the process
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{x}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V}\big)}\,{(\widetilde{z}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{z}_{n},\widetilde{m})}
, which effectively mimics the application.
This abstraction nesting binds all variables
\widetilde{z}_{i}
, the
decompositions of all tail-recursive names (
\widetilde{r}
).

The breakdown of a value application of the form

y\,{(\widetilde{r},u_{i})}
results into the following specific case:

{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{x}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}y\,{(\widetilde{r},u_{i})}}\big)}=c_{k}?(\widetilde{x}).\overbracket{c^{r_{1}}!\big\langle\lambda\widetilde{z}_{1}.c^{r_{2}}!\langle\lambda\widetilde{z}_{2}.\cdots.c^{r_{n}}!\langle\lambda\widetilde{z}_{n}.}^{n=|\tilde{r}|}y\,{(\widetilde{z}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{z}_{n},\widetilde{m})}\rangle\,\rangle\big\rangle

Restriction:

The decomposition of
(\nu\,s:C)\,{P^{\prime}}
depends on
C
:

•

If
\neg\mathsf{tr}(C)
then

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{x}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\nu\,s:C)\,{P^{\prime}}}\big)}=(\nu\,\widetilde{s}:{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C})})\,{\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{x}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}\sigma}\big)}}

By construction,
\widetilde{x}=\mathtt{fv}(P^{\prime})
. Similarly as in the decomposition of

u_{i}
into
\widetilde{m}
discussed above, we use the type
C
of
s
to obtain
the tuple
\widetilde{s}
of length
|{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C})}|
.
We initialize the index of

s
in
P^{\prime}
by applying the substitution
\sigma
. This substitution depends on

C
: if it is a shared type then
\sigma=\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$s_{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$s$}\}
; otherwise, if
C
is
a session type, then
\sigma=\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$s_{1}\overline{s_{1}}$}\!/\mbox{\small$s\overline{s}$}\}
.

•

Otherwise, if
\mathsf{tr}(C)
then we have:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{x}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\nu\,s:C)\,{P^{\prime}}}\big)}=(\nu\,\widetilde{s}:\mathcal{R}(S))\,(\nu\,c^{s})\,c^{s}?(x).x\,{\widetilde{s}}\;|\;(\nu\,c^{\bar{s}})\,c^{\bar{s}}?(x).x\,{\widetilde{\overline{s}}}\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{x}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}\big)}

We decompose
s
into
\widetilde{s}=(s_{1},\ldots,s_{|{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S})}|})
and

\overline{s}
into
\widetilde{\overline{s}}=(\overline{s_{1}},\ldots,\overline{s_{|{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S})}|}})
. Notice
that as
\mathsf{tr}(C)
we have

C\equiv\mu\mathsf{t}.S
, therefore
{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C})}={\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{R}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S})}
.
The breakdown introduces two servers in parallel with the breakdown of

P^{\prime}
; they provide names for
s
and
\overline{s}
along
c^{s}

and
c^{\overline{s}}
, respectively. The server on
c^{s}
(resp.

c^{\overline{s}}
) receives a value
and applies it to the sequence
\widetilde{s}
(resp.

\widetilde{\overline{s}}
). We restrict over
\widetilde{s}
and propagators

c^{s}
and
c^{\overline{s}}
.

Composition:

The breakdown of a process
Q\;|\;R
is as follows:

{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{x}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q\;|\;R}\big)}=c_{k}?(\widetilde{x}).\overline{c_{k+1}}!\langle\widetilde{y}\rangle.\overline{c_{k+l+1}}!\langle\widetilde{w}\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k+1}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{y}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}\big)}\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k+l+1}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{w}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}R}\big)}

A control trio triggers the breakdowns of
Q
and
R
; it does not mimic any
action of the source process. The tuple
\widetilde{y}\subseteq\widetilde{x}
(resp.
\widetilde{w}\subseteq\widetilde{x}
) collects the free variables in
Q
(resp.
R
). To avoid
name conflicts, the trigger for the breakdown of
R
is
\overline{c_{k+l+1}}
, with
l={\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\lbag{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}\rbag}
.

Inaction:

To breakdown
\mathbf{0}
, we define a degenerate trio with only one input prefix
that receives a context that by construction will always be empty (i.e.,
\widetilde{x}=\epsilon
, cf.
Remark

3.2
):

{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{x}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}\big)}=c_{k}?().\mathbf{0}

Value:

For simplicity, let us consider values of the form

V=\lambda(\widetilde{y},z):{(\widetilde{S},C)}^{\leadsto}.\,P
,
where
\mathsf{tr}(y_{i})
holds for every
y_{i}\in\widetilde{y}
and
\neg\mathsf{tr}(z)
, and

\leadsto\in\{\multimap,\rightarrow\}
.
The general case is defined similarly.
We have:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{x}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lambda(\widetilde{y},z):{(\widetilde{S},C)}^{\leadsto}.\,P}\big)}

\displaystyle=\lambda(\widetilde{y^{1}},\ldots,\widetilde{y^{n}},\widetilde{z}):{(\widetilde{M})}^{\leadsto}.\,N\quad\text{where:}

\displaystyle\widetilde{M}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S_{1}})},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S_{n}})},{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C})}

\displaystyle N

\displaystyle=(\nu\,\widetilde{c})\,(\nu\,\widetilde{c}_{r})\,\prod_{i\in|\widetilde{y}|}c^{y_{i}}?(x).x\,{\widetilde{y}^{i}}\;|\;\overline{c_{1}}!\langle\widetilde{x}\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{x}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$z_{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$z$}\}}\big)}

Every
y_{i}
(with
y_{i}:S_{i}
) is
decomposed into
\widetilde{y}^{i}=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{|{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S_{i}})}|})
. We use

C
to decompose
z
into
\widetilde{z}
.
We abstract over
\widetilde{y}^{1},\ldots,\widetilde{y}^{n},\widetilde{z}
;
the body of the abstraction (i.e.
N
) is the composition of recursive names
propagators, the control trio, and the breakdown of
P
, with name index
initialized with the substitution
\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$z_{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$z$}\}
.
For every
y_{i}\in\widetilde{y}
there is a server

c^{y_{i}}?(x).x\,{\widetilde{y}^{i}}

as a subprocess in the
abstracted composition—the rationale for these servers is as in previous cases.
We restrict the
propagators

\widetilde{c}=(c_{1},\ldots,c_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\lbag{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\rbag}})
: this
enables us to type the value in a shared environment when
\leadsto=\rightarrow
.
Also, we restrict special propagator names
\widetilde{c}_{r}=\bigcup_{r\in\tilde{v}}c^{r}
.

3.3
The Decomposition by Example

We illustrate the decompositions by means of several examples.

3.3.1
Decomposing Processes with Non-Recursive Names

Example 3.7
.

Consider process
P=(\nu\,u)\,(Q\;|\;R)

whose body implements end-points of channel
u
with
session type
S=?(U){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}?(\textsf{bool}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}
,
with
U=(?(\textsf{bool}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}})\!\multimap\!\diamond
,
where:

\displaystyle Q

\displaystyle=u?(x).\overbrace{u?(y).(\nu\,s)\,\big(x\,{\overline{s}}\;|\;s!\langle y\rangle\big)}^{Q^{\prime}}

\displaystyle R

\displaystyle=\overline{u}!\langle V\rangle.\overline{u}!\langle\textsf{true}\rangle.\mathbf{0}

\displaystyle V

\displaystyle=\lambda z.\,z?(b).\mathbf{0}

The process
P
reduces as follows:

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle\longrightarrow(\nu\,u)\,\left(u?(y).(\nu\,s)\,\big(V\,{\overline{s}}\;|\;s!\langle y\rangle\big)\;|\;\overline{u}!\langle\textsf{true}\rangle.\mathbf{0}\right)\longrightarrow(\nu\,s)\,\big(V\,{\overline{s}}\;|\;s!\langle\textsf{true}\rangle\big)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow(\nu\,s)\,\big(\overline{s}?(b).\mathbf{0}\;|\;s!\langle\textsf{true}\rangle\big)=P^{\prime}

By

3.9
we have that the decomposition of

is as

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P})}=(\nu\,c_{1},\ldots,c_{10})\,(\overline{c_{1}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\sigma}\big)})

where

\sigma=\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$u_{1}\overline{u}_{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$u\overline{u}$}\}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\sigma}\big)}

\displaystyle=(\nu\,u_{1},u_{2})\,c_{1}?().\overline{c_{2}}!\langle\rangle.\overline{c_{8}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q\sigma}\big)}\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}8}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}R\sigma}\big)}

and

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q\sigma}\big)}

\displaystyle=c_{2}?().u_{1}?(x).\overline{c_{3}}!\langle x\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}3}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q^{\prime}\sigma^{\prime}}\big)}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}3}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q^{\prime}\sigma^{\prime}}\big)}

\displaystyle=c_{3}?(x).u_{2}?(y).\overline{c_{4}}!\langle x,y\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}4}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\nu\,s)\,\big(x\,{\overline{s}}\;|\;s!\langle y\rangle\big)}\big)}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}4}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x,y}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\nu\,s)\,\big(x\,{\overline{s}}\;|\;s!\langle y\rangle\big)}\big)}

\displaystyle=(\nu\,s_{1})\,\big(c_{4}?(x,y).\overline{c_{5}}!\langle x\rangle.\overline{c_{6}}!\langle y\rangle\;|\;c_{5}?(x).x\,{\overline{s}_{1}}\;|\;c_{6}?(y).s_{1}!\langle y\rangle.\overline{c_{7}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{7}?().\mathbf{0}\big)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}8}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}R\sigma}\big)}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}9}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}u_{2}!\langle\textsf{true}\rangle.\mathbf{0}}\big)}

\displaystyle=c_{9}?().\overline{u}_{2}!\langle\textsf{true}\rangle.\overline{c_{10}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{10}?().\mathbf{0}

\displaystyle=\lambda z_{1}.\,((\nu\,c^{V}_{1},c^{V}_{2})\,\overline{c{{}^{V}_{1}}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c{{}^{V}_{1}}?().z_{1}?(b).\overline{c{{}^{V}_{2}}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c{{}^{V}_{2}}?().\mathbf{0})

Example 3.8

(Breaking Down Name-Passing)
.

Consider the following process
P
, in which a channel
m
is passed, through which a boolean value is sent back:

\displaystyle P=(\nu\,u)\,(u!\langle\ulcorner m\urcorner\rangle.\overline{m}?(b)\;|\;\overline{u}?(\ulcorner x\urcorner).x!\langle\textsf{true}\rangle)

After expanding the syntactic sugar of name-passing, we get a process
P=(\nu\,u)\,(Q\;|\;R)
, where

\displaystyle Q=u!\langle V\rangle.\overline{m}?(y).(\nu\,s)\,{(y\,{s}\;|\;\overline{s}!\langle\lambda b.\,\mathbf{0}\rangle)}

\displaystyle V=\lambda z.\,z?(x).(x\,{m})

\displaystyle R=\overline{u}?(y).(\nu\,s)\,{(y\,{s}\;|\;\overline{s}!\langle W\rangle)}

\displaystyle W=\lambda x.\,x!\langle W^{\prime}\rangle\text{ with }W^{\prime}=\lambda z.\,z?(x).(x\,{\textsf{true})}

Note that to mimic the name-passing synchronization, we require exactly four reduction steps:

\displaystyle P\longrightarrow^{4}{\color[rgb]{0.36,0.54,0.66}\llbracket}\overline{m}?(b)\;|\;m!\langle\textsf{true}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.36,0.54,0.66}\rrbracket}\longrightarrow^{4}\mathbf{0}

(19)

We will now investigate the decomposition of
P
and its reduction chain.
First, we use
Definition

3.6
to compute
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\lbag{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}\rbag}=6
, and similarly,
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\lbag{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}R}\rbag}=5
.
Therefore,
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\lbag{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{P}}\rbag}=12
.
Following
Definition

3.9
, we see that
\sigma=\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$m_{1}\overline{m_{1}}$}\!/\mbox{\small$m\overline{m}$}\}
, which we silently apply.
Taking
k=1
, the breakdown of
P
and its subprocesses is shown in
Table

2
.
