\displaystyle P^{\prime\prime}=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \overline{m_{1}}?(y).\overline{c_{4}}!\langle y\rangle\;|\;m_{1}!\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}W^{\prime}}\big)}\rangle.\overline{c{{}^{W^{\prime}}_{2}}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c{{}^{W^{\prime}}_{2}}?()

\displaystyle\;|\;(\nu\,s_{1})\,(c_{4}?(y).\overline{c_{5}}!\langle y\rangle.\overline{c_{6}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{5}?(y).y\,{s_{1}}\;|\;c_{6}?().\overline{s_{1}}!\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lambda b.\,\mathbf{0}}\big)}\rangle.\overline{c_{7}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{7}?())

Remarkably,
P^{\prime\prime}
is standing by to mimic the encoded exchange of value
true
. Indeed, the decomposition of the four-step reduced process in (
19
) will reduce in three steps to a process that is equal (up to
\equiv_{\alpha}
) to the process we obtained here. This strongly suggests a tight operational correspondence between a process and its decomposition, which we will explore in
Section

4
.
\lhd

3.3.2
Decomposing Processes with Recursive Names

Next, we illustrate the decomposition of processes involving names with
tail-recursive types. Recall process
R_{1}
, which we used in
Section

3.1
to
motivate the need for recursive propagators:

\displaystyle R_{1}={r?(z).}{r!\langle-z\rangle.}{r?(z).}{r!\langle z\rangle.}{V\,{r}}

Two following examples illustrate the low-level workings of the propagation
mechanism of the decomposition
in
Figure

5
.
The first example illustrates how the
propagation of recursive names works in the case of
input and output actions on names with recursive types (the “first part” of

R_{1}
). The second example shows how an application where a value is applied to
a tuple of names with recursive names is broken down (the “second part” of

R_{1}
).

Example 3.9

(Decomposing Processes with Recursive Names (I))
.

Let
P=r?(x).r!\langle x\rangle.P^{\prime}
be a process where
r
has type

S=\mu\mathsf{t}.?(\mathsf{int}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}!\langle\mathsf{\mathsf{int}}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}{\mathsf{t}}
and
r\in\mathtt{fn}(P^{\prime})
.
By
Definition

3.9
we have:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P})}=(\nu\,\widetilde{c})\,(\nu\,c^{r})\,\big(\overline{c_{1}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\big)}\;|\;c^{r}?(x).x\,{(r_{1},r_{2})}\big)

where
\widetilde{c}=(c_{1},\ldots,c_{|P|})
.
The control trio in the parallel composition provides a
decomposition of
r
on name
c^{r}
, which is
shared
.
The decomposition
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\big)}
is
defined as follows:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\big)}

\displaystyle=\overline{c_{1}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{1}?().c^{r}!\langle N_{1}\rangle\;|\;c_{2}?(y).c^{r}!\langle N_{2}\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}3}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}\big)}\big)

\displaystyle N_{1}

\displaystyle=\lambda(z_{1},z_{2}).\,z_{1}?(x).c^{r}?(x).x\,{(z_{1},z_{2})}

\displaystyle N_{2}

\displaystyle=\lambda(z_{1},z_{2}).\,z_{2}!\langle x\rangle.\overline{c_{3}}!\langle\rangle.c^{r}?(x).x\,{(z_{1},z_{2})}

Each trio in
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\big)}
that mimics some action on
r

requests the sequence
\tilde{r}
from the server on
c^{r}
. We can see that
this request is realized by a higher-order communication: trios send
abstractions (
N_{1}
and
N_{2}
) to the server; these abstractions contain
further actions of trios and it will be applied to the sequence
\tilde{r}
.
Hence, the formal arguments for these values are meant to correspond to

\tilde{r}
.

After two reductions (the trio activation on
c_{1}

and the communication on
c^{r}
), we have:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P})}\longrightarrow^{2}(\nu\,c_{2},\ldots,c_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\lbag{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\rbag}})\,\,r_{1}?(x).\overline{c_{2}}!\langle x\rangle.c^{r}?(x).x\,{(r_{1},r_{2})}\;|\;c_{2}?(y).c^{r}!\langle N_{2}\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}3}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}\big)}=P_{1}

By synchronizing with the top-level server on
c^{r}
, the bound names
in
N_{1}
are instantiated with
r_{1},r_{2}
.
Now, the first trio in
P_{1}
is able
to mimic the action on
r_{1}
that is followed by the activation of the next
trio on
c_{2}
. Then, the server on
c^{r}
gets reinstantiated making names
r_{1},r_{2}

available for future trios.
The break down of the output action follows the same pattern.

\lhd

Example 3.10

(Decomposing Processes with Recursive Names (II))
.

Let

S=\mu\mathsf{\mathsf{t}}.?(\mathsf{int}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}!\langle\mathsf{int}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathsf{t}

and

T=\mu\mathsf{\mathsf{t}}.?(\mathsf{bool}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}!\langle\mathsf{bool}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathsf{t}
, and
define
Q=V\,{(u,v)}
as a process where
u:S
and
v:T
, where
V

is some value of type
(S,T)\!\rightarrow\!\diamond
.
By
Definition

3.9
,
the decomposition of
Q
is as in the previous example, except that now there
are two servers, one for
u
and one for
v
:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q})}

\displaystyle=(\nu\,c_{1}\tilde{c})\,(\nu\,c^{u}c^{v})\,\big(c^{u}?(x).x\,{(u_{1},u_{2})}\;|\;c^{v}?(x).x\,{(v_{1},v_{2})}\;|\;\overline{c_{1}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}\big)}\big)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}\big)}

\displaystyle=c_{1}?().c^{u}!\big\langle\lambda(x_{1},x_{2}).\,c^{v}!\langle\lambda(y_{1},y_{2}).\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V}\big)}\,{(x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2})}\rangle\big\rangle

with
\tilde{c}=(c_{2},\ldots,c_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\lbag{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}\rbag}})
. Process
Q

is broken down in such a way that it communicates with both servers to collect

\tilde{u}
and
\tilde{v}
.
To this end,
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}\big)}
is a process in which abstractions are
nested using output prefixes and whose innermost process is an application.
After successive communications with multiple servers this innermost
application will have collected all names in
\tilde{u}
and
\tilde{v}
.

Observe that we use two nested outputs, one for each name with recursive types
in
Q
.
We now look at the reductions of
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q})}
to analyze how the communication of nested abstractions allows us to collect all name sequences needed. After the first reduction along
c_{1}
we have:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q})}\longrightarrow

\displaystyle(\nu\,\tilde{c})\,(\nu\,c^{u}c^{v})\,\big(c^{u}?(x).x\,{(u_{1},u_{2})}\;|\;c^{v}?(x).x\,{(v_{1},v_{2})}\;|\;

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad c^{u}!\big\langle\lambda(x_{1},x_{2}).\,c^{v}!\langle\lambda(y_{1},y_{2}).\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V}\big)}\,{(x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2})}\rangle\big\rangle\big)=R^{1}

From
R^{1}
we have a synchronization along name
c^{u}
:

\displaystyle R^{1}\longrightarrow

\displaystyle(\nu\,\tilde{c})\,(\nu\,c^{u}c^{v})\,\big((\lambda(x_{1},x_{2}).\,c^{v}!\langle\lambda(y_{1},y_{2}).\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V}\big)}\,{(x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2})}\rangle)\,{(u_{1},u_{2})}\;|\;c^{v}?(x).x\,{(v_{1},v_{2})}\big)=R^{2}

Upon receiving the value, the server applies it to
(u_{1},u_{2})
, thus
obtaining the following process:

\displaystyle R^{2}\longrightarrow

\displaystyle(\nu\,\tilde{c})\,(\nu\,c^{u}c^{v})\,\big(c^{v}!\langle\lambda(y_{1},y_{2}).\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V}\big)}\,{(u_{1},u_{2}},y_{1},y_{2})\rangle\;|\;c^{v}?(x).x\,{(v_{1},v_{2})}\big)=R^{3}

Up to here, we have partially instantiated name variables of a value
with the sequence
\tilde{u}
. Next, the first trio in
R^{3}
can communicate with the server on name
c^{v}
:

\displaystyle R^{3}\longrightarrow

\displaystyle(\nu\,\tilde{c})\,(\nu\,c^{u}c^{v})\,\big(\lambda(y_{1},y_{2}).\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V}\big)}\,{(u_{1},u_{2},y_{1},y_{2})}\,{(v_{1},v_{2})}\big)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow

\displaystyle(\nu\,\tilde{c})\,(\nu\,c^{u}c^{v})\,\big({{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V}\big)}\,{(u_{1},u_{2},v_{1},v_{2})}}\big)

This completes the instantiation of name variables with appropriate sequences of names with recursive types.
At this point,
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q})}
can proceed to mimic the application in
Q
.

\lhd

Example 3.11

(Breakdown of Recursion Encoding)
.

We recall process
{\color[rgb]{0.36,0.54,0.66}\llbracket}P{\color[rgb]{0.36,0.54,0.66}\rrbracket}
from
Example

3.4
:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.36,0.54,0.66}\llbracket}P{\color[rgb]{0.36,0.54,0.66}\rrbracket}

\displaystyle=a?(m).a!\langle m\rangle.(\nu\,s)\,{(V\,{(a,s)}\;|\;\overline{s}!\langle V\rangle)}

\displaystyle V

\displaystyle=\lambda(x_{a},y_{1}).\,y_{1}?(z_{x}).x_{a}?(m).x_{a}!\langle m\rangle.(\nu\,s)\,{(z_{x}\,{(x_{a},s)}\;|\;\overline{s}!\langle z_{x}\rangle.\mathbf{0})}

Here, bound name
s
is typed with
S
, from
Example

3.3
, defined as:

\displaystyle S=\mu\mathsf{t}.?((?(\mathsf{str}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}!\langle\mathsf{str}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}},\mathsf{t})\!\rightarrow\!\diamond){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}

We now analyze
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.36,0.54,0.66}\llbracket}P{\color[rgb]{0.36,0.54,0.66}\rrbracket}})}
and its reduction chain.
By
Definition

3.6
, we have
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\lbag{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.36,0.54,0.66}\llbracket}P{\color[rgb]{0.36,0.54,0.66}\rrbracket}}\rbag}=7
.
Then, we choose
k=1
and observe that
\sigma=\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$a_{1}\overline{a_{1}}$}\!/\mbox{\small$a\overline{a}$}\}
. Following
Definition

3.9
, we get:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.36,0.54,0.66}\llbracket}P{\color[rgb]{0.36,0.54,0.66}\rrbracket}})}=(\nu\,c_{1},\ldots,c_{7})\,(\nu\,c^{a})\,(c^{a}?(x).x\,{(a_{1},a_{2})}\;|\;\overline{c_{1}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.36,0.54,0.66}\llbracket}P{\color[rgb]{0.36,0.54,0.66}\rrbracket}\sigma}\big)})

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.36,0.54,0.66}\llbracket}P{\color[rgb]{0.36,0.54,0.66}\rrbracket}}\big)}=c_{1}?().c^{a}!\langle\lambda(z_{1},z_{2}).\,z_{1}?(m).\overline{c_{2}}!\langle m\rangle.c^{a}?(x).x\,{(z_{1},z_{2})}\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\;|\;c_{2}?(m).c^{a}!\langle\lambda(z_{1},z_{2}).\,z_{2}!\langle m\rangle.\overline{c_{3}}!\langle\rangle.c^{a}?(x).x\,{(z_{1},z_{2})}\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\;|\;(\nu\,s_{1})\,\big(c_{3}?().\overline{c_{4}}!\langle\rangle.\overline{c_{5}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{4}?().\overline{c^{a}}!\langle\lambda(z_{1},z_{2}).\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V}\big)}\,{(z_{1},z_{2},s_{1})}\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\;|\;c_{5}?().\overline{s}_{1}!\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V}\big)}\rangle.\overline{c_{7}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{7}?()\big)

In accordance with
Example

3.3
, the type of
s_{1}
in the decomposed process is

\displaystyle M=\mu\mathsf{t}.?({{{(?(\mathsf{str}),!\langle\mathsf{str}\rangle,\mathsf{t})\!\rightarrow\!\diamond}}}).

The decomposition relies twice on
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V}\big)}
, the breakdown of value
V
, which we give below.
For this, we observe that
V

is an abstraction of a process
Q
with
|Q|=7
.
We also
\alpha
-convert the process abstracted in
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V}\big)}

renaming bound propagators
c_{1},\ldots,c_{7}
to

c^{V}_{1},\ldots,c^{V}_{7}
to avoid name clashes.

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V}\big)}

\displaystyle=\lambda(x_{a_{1}},x_{a_{2}},y_{1}).\,(\nu\,c{{}^{V}_{1}},\ldots,c{{}^{V}_{7}})\,\big(\overline{c{{}^{V}_{1}}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}\big)}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$c{{}^{V}_{1}},\ldots,c{{}^{V}_{7}}$}\!/\mbox{\small$c{{}_{1}},\ldots,c{{}_{7}}$}\}\;|\;c^{x_{a}}?(x).x\,{(x_{a_{1}},x_{a_{2}})}\big)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}\big)}

\displaystyle=c{{}_{1}}?().y_{1}?(z_{x}).\overline{c_{2}}!\langle z_{x}\rangle

\displaystyle\quad\;|\;c_{2}?(z_{x}).c^{a}!\langle\lambda(z_{1},z_{2}).\,z_{1}?(m).\overline{c_{3}}!\langle z_{x},m\rangle.c^{a}?(x).x\,{(z_{1},z_{2})}\rangle

\displaystyle\quad\;|\;c_{3}?(z_{x}).c^{a}!\langle\lambda(z_{1},z_{2}).\,z_{2}!\langle m\rangle.\overline{c_{4}}!\langle z_{x}\rangle.c^{a}?(x).x\,{(z_{1},z_{2})}\rangle

\displaystyle\quad\;|\;(\nu\,s_{1})\,\big(c_{4}?(x_{z}).\overline{c_{5}}!\langle z_{x}\rangle.\overline{c_{6}}!\langle z_{x}\rangle\;|\;

\displaystyle\quad\qquad\qquad c_{5}?(z_{x}).c^{a}!\langle\lambda(z_{1},z_{2}).\,z_{x}\,{(z_{1},z_{2},s_{1})}\rangle\;|\;c_{6}?(z_{x}).\overline{s_{1}}!\langle z_{x}\rangle.\overline{c_{7}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{7}?()\big)

We follow the reduction chain on
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.36,0.54,0.66}\llbracket}P{\color[rgb]{0.36,0.54,0.66}\rrbracket}})}
until it is ready to mimic the first action with channel
a
, which is an input. First,
c_{1}
will synchronize, after which
c^{a}
sends the abstraction to which then
(a_{1},a_{2})
is applied. We obtain
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.36,0.54,0.66}\llbracket}P{\color[rgb]{0.36,0.54,0.66}\rrbracket}})}\longrightarrow^{3}(\nu\,c_{2},\ldots,c_{7},c^{a})\,P^{\prime}
, where

\displaystyle P^{\prime}=a_{1}?(m).\overline{c_{2}}!\langle m\rangle.c^{a}?(x).x\,{(a_{1},a_{2})}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\;|\;c_{2}?(m).c^{a}!\langle\lambda(z_{1},z_{2}).\,z_{2}!\langle m\rangle.\overline{c_{3}}!\langle\rangle.c^{a}?(x).x\,{(z_{1},z_{2})}\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\;|\;(\nu\,s_{1})\,\big(c_{3}?().\overline{c_{4}}!\langle\rangle.\overline{c_{5}}!\langle\rangle.\;|\;c_{4}?().\overline{c^{a}}!\langle\lambda(z_{1},z_{2}).\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V}\big)}\,{(z_{1},z_{2},s_{1})}\rangle\;|\;

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad c_{5}?().\overline{s}_{1}!\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V}\big)}\rangle.\overline{c_{7}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{7}?()\big)

Note that this process is awaiting an input on channel
a_{1}
, after which

c_{2}
can synchronize with its dual. At that point,
c^{a}
is ready to receive
another abstraction that mimics an input on
a_{1}
. This strongly suggests a
tight operational correspondence between a process
P
and its decomposition
in the case where
P
performs higher-order recursion.

\hfill\lhd

3.4
Static Correctness

Having presented and illustrated our decomposition, we may now state its technical results.
Given an environment
\Delta=\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}
, below we write
\Delta_{1}\circ\Delta_{2}
to indicate the split of
\Delta
into a
\Delta_{1}
containing non-recursive names and a
\Delta_{2}
containing recursive names.

We extend the decomposition function
{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-})}
to typing
environments in the obvious way.
We rely on the following notation.
Given a tuple of names
\widetilde{s}=s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}
and a tuple of
(session) types
\widetilde{S}=S_{1},\ldots,S_{n}
of the same length, we write

\widetilde{s}:\widetilde{S}
to denote a list of typing assignments
s_{1}:S_{1},\ldots,s_{n}:S_{n}
.

Definition 3.10

(Decomposition of Environments)
.

Let
\Gamma
,
\Lambda
, and
\Delta
be typing environments.
We define
{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma})}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Lambda})}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta})}
inductively as follows:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta,u_{i}:S})}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta})},(u_{i},\ldots,u_{i+|{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S})}|-1}):{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S})}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma,u_{i}:\langle U\rangle})}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma})},u_{i}:{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\langle U\rangle})}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma,x:U})}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma})},x:{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}U})}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Lambda,x:U})}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Lambda})},x:{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}U})}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\emptyset})}

\displaystyle=\emptyset

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
P
be an indexed
\mathsf{HO}
process and
V
be a value.

1.

If
\Gamma;\Lambda;\Delta\circ\Delta_{\mu}\vdash P\triangleright\diamond
then

{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{1}})},\Phi;\emptyset;{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta})},\Theta\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{x}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\big)}\triangleright\diamond
,
where:

•

k>0

•

\widetilde{r}=\text{dom}(\Delta_{\mu})

•

\Phi=\prod_{r\in\tilde{r}}c^{r}:\langle{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{R}^{\star}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta_{\mu}(r)})}\!\multimap\!\diamond\rangle

•

\widetilde{x}=\mathtt{fv}(P)

•

\Gamma_{1}=\Gamma\setminus\widetilde{x}

•

\text{dom}(\Theta)=\{c_{k},\ldots,c_{k+{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\lbag{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\rbag}-1}\}\cup\{\overline{c_{k+1}},\ldots,\overline{c_{k+{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\lbag{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\rbag}-1}}\}

•

\Theta(c_{k})=?(U_{1},\ldots,U_{n})
, where
({\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma})},{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Lambda})})(\widetilde{x})=(x_{1}:U_{1},\ldots,x_{n}:U_{n})

•

\mathsf{balanced}(\Theta)

2.

If
\Gamma;\Lambda;\Delta\circ\Delta_{\mu}\vdash V\triangleright\widetilde{T}\!\multimap\!\diamond
then

{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma})},\Phi;{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Lambda})};{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta})}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{x}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V}\big)}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widetilde{T}})}\!\multimap\!\diamond
,
where:

•

\widetilde{x}=\mathtt{fv}(V)

•

\Phi=\prod_{r\in\tilde{r}}c^{r}:\langle{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{R}^{\star}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta_{\mu}(r)})}\!\multimap\!\diamond\rangle

Proof.

By mutual induction on the structure of
P
and
V
.
See
Section

B.1
for details.
∎

Using the above lemma we can prove our static correctness result, which explains how our decomposition induces minimal session types.

Theorem 3.1

(Static Correctness)
.

Let
P
be a closed
\mathsf{HO}
process (i.e.
\mathtt{fv}(P)=\emptyset
) with
\widetilde{u}=\mathtt{fn}(P)
.
If
\Gamma;\emptyset;\Delta\circ\Delta_{\mu}\vdash P\triangleright\diamond
,
then
{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma\sigma})};\emptyset;{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta\sigma})},{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta_{\mu}\sigma})}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P})}\triangleright\diamond
, where
\sigma=\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathsf{init}(\widetilde{u})$}\!/\mbox{\small$\widetilde{u}$}\}
.

Proof.

Directly from the definitions, using
3.1
.
See
Section

B.2
for details.
∎

4
Dynamic Correctness

In this section, we establish the dynamic correctness of our decomposition, stated in terms of a typed behavioral equivalence.
More specifically, we would like to show that any typed process
P
is equivalent to its decomposition
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P})}
.
But how do we even state it formally?
Both
P
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P})}
are typed
\mathsf{HO}
processes (as any minimally typed process is also an
\mathsf{HO}
process), so we can consider compare them as
\mathsf{HO}
terms inside the
\mathsf{HO}
type system.
The conventional notion of typed equivalence for
\mathsf{HO}
processes is contextual equivalence, which is given a local characterization in terms of
higher-order bisimulations

[
19
]
.
In our case, however, contextual equivalence is not the right choice: contextual equivalence applies to processes of the
same type
, whereas the process
P
and its decomposition
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P})}
have different types and typing contexts.
Instead of using contextual equivalence, we generalize the notion of higher-order bisimilarity to a notion that we call
MST bisimilarity
, which relates processes of (potentially) different types.

This section is organized as follows.
In
Section

4.1
we recall the notion of higher-order bisimulation, used for characterizing behavioral equivalence in
\mathsf{HO}
, and discuss its limitations for our purposes.
We use higher-order bisimulation as a basis to give a formal definition of MST bisimulation in
Section

4.2
, which we will use as a notion of behavioral equivalence for comparing
P
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P})}
.
In order to show that our decomposition is correct, in
Section

4.3
we exhibit a bisimulation relation
\mathbin{\mathcal{S}}
which relates a process and its decomposition, containing a number of intermediate pairs, working from a motivating example in
Section

4.3.1
.
Finally, in
Section

4.4
we show that
\mathbin{\mathcal{S}}
is indeed an MST bisimulation.

4.1
Behavioral Equivalence in
\mathsf{HO}
and its Limitations

Let us begin by recalling the notion of
\mathsf{HO}
bisimulation, defined in
[
19
]
to characterize contextual equivalence of
\mathsf{HO}
processes.

Definition 4.1

(Definition 17 in
[
19
]
)
.

A typed relation
\Re
is an
\mathsf{HO}
bisimulation
if
for all
\Gamma_{1};\Lambda_{1};\Delta_{1}\vdash P_{1}\ \Re\ \Gamma_{2};\Lambda_{2};\Delta_{2}\vdash Q_{1}
,

1)

Whenever

\Gamma_{1};\Lambda_{1};\Delta_{1}\vdash P_{1}\mathrel{\xmapsto{(\nu\,\widetilde{m_{1}})\,n!\langle V_{1}\rangle}}\Lambda^{\prime}_{1};\Delta^{\prime}_{1}\vdash P_{2}

then there exist

Q_{2}
,
\Delta^{\prime}_{2}
, and
\Lambda^{\prime}_{2}
such that

\Gamma_{2};\Lambda_{2};\Delta_{2}\vdash Q_{1}\mathrel{{\mathrel{\to}^{(\nu\,\widetilde{m_{2}})\,n!\langle V_{2}\rangle}}}\Lambda^{\prime}_{2};\Delta_{2}^{\prime}\vdash Q_{2}

where, for a fresh
t
,

\Gamma_{1};\Lambda_{1};\Delta^{\prime\prime}_{1}\vdash{(\nu\,\widetilde{m_{1}})(P_{2}\;|\;t\hookleftarrow_{\mathtt{H}}V_{1})}\ \Re\ \Gamma_{2};\Lambda_{2};\Delta^{\prime\prime}_{2}\vdash{(\nu\,\widetilde{m_{2}})(Q_{2}\;|\;t\hookleftarrow_{\mathtt{H}}V_{2})}

2)

Whenever

\Gamma_{1};\Lambda_{1};\Delta_{1}\vdash P_{1}\mathrel{\xmapsto{\ell}}\Lambda_{1}^{\prime};\Delta_{1}^{\prime}\vdash P_{2}
, with

\ell
not an output, then there exist

Q_{2}
,
\Lambda_{2}^{\prime}
, and
\Delta^{\prime}_{2}
such that

\Gamma_{2};\Lambda_{2};\Delta_{2}\vdash Q_{1}\mathrel{{\mathrel{\to}^{\hat{\ell}}}}\Lambda_{2}^{\prime};\Delta_{2}^{\prime}\vdash Q_{2}

and

\Gamma_{1};\Lambda_{1}^{\prime};\Delta_{1}^{\prime}\vdash P_{2}\ \Re\ \Gamma_{2};\Lambda_{2}^{\prime};\Delta_{2}^{\prime}\vdash Q_{2}
.

3)

The symmetric cases of 1, 2.

The largest such bisimulation is called
\mathsf{HO}
bisimilarity
, denoted by
\approx^{\mathtt{H}}
.

There are two points worth highlighting in this definition.
Firstly, the labeled transition system
\mathrel{\xmapsto{\ell}}
used in the definition of
\approx^{\mathtt{H}}
is what is called the
refined transition system
, different from the standard labeled transition system for the higher-order
\pi
-calculus.
The idea behind the refined transition system is that we want to disallow arbitrary inputs
P\mathrel{\xmapsto{x(V)}}P^{\prime}
; having to consider such transitions in the definition of bisimilarity is undesirable, because it involves input of an arbitrary (higher-order) value
V
, making the definition very much non-local and ensuring that the bisimulations are very large.
As it turns out, due to the typed nature of the system, it suffices to consider inputs of the processes of a very particular kind—
characteristic values
, defined based on the type.

Secondly, because the inputs are restricted in the refined LTS, there is some price to pay in the handling of the outputs.
If an output action
{P_{1}}\mathrel{\xmapsto{(\nu\,\widetilde{m_{1}})\,n!\langle V_{1}\rangle}}{P_{2}}
is matched by an output action
{Q_{1}}\mathrel{{\mathrel{\to}^{(\nu\,\widetilde{m_{2}})\,n!\langle V_{2}\rangle}}}{Q_{2}}
, then we need to ensure that that the output processes
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
are somehow related.
We have to ensure this in the output clause, because on the receiving end transitions inputing values
V_{1}
or
V_{2}
might not even be considered.
To that extent, we package the values
V_{1}
or
V_{2}
in
trigger processes
(denoted
t\hookleftarrow_{\mathtt{H}}V_{1}
and
t\hookleftarrow_{\mathtt{H}}V_{2}
), which are defined based on the typing.
We then make them part of the processes that are considered at the “next step” of the bisimulation.

This notion of
\mathsf{HO}
bisimilarity works for processes of the same type.
For our case, we need to compare processes of different but related types.
To that extent we make several changes to the definition above.
Firstly, during the decomposition a single name
x
in a source process is decomposed into a sequence of names
x_{1},\dots,x_{k}
in the target process.
So in the definition of MST bisimilarity we match an action on a name
x
with an action on an
indexed
name
x_{i}
.
Secondly, such discrepancy between names might arise in input and output values.
This also needs to be considered as part of the definition.
For this, we need to accommodate the difference between characteristic values and trigger processes for MST and
\mathsf{HO}
bisimilarities.
In the next subsection we work out the details sketched above.

4.2
MST Bisimilarity

In this section we define a generalized version of
\mathsf{HO}
bisimilarity allowing for comparing MST and
\mathsf{HO}
process terms.
Our goal is to define
MST bisimilarity
(denoted
\approx^{\mathtt{M}}
), a typed behavioral equivalence, which we give in
Definition

4.9
.
To define
\approx^{\mathtt{M}}
, we require some auxiliary definitions, in particular:

•

A refined LTS on typed processes (
Definition

4.5
);

•

A relation
\bowtie
on values (
Definition

4.6
) and on names (
Definition

4.14
);

•

A revised notion of trigger processes (
Definition

4.8
).

Refined LTS and characteristic values.

The idea behind defining the refined LTS is to restrict the input of arbitrary processes (values) and make the transition system image-finite (modulo names).

The
refined LTS
for
\mathsf{HO}
is defined in
[
19
]
in three layers.
First comes the
untyped LTS

P\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\ell}}P^{\prime}
, which describes reductions of untyped processes in the usual style of the LTS semantics for
\pi
-calculus.
Secondly, there is a notion of the
environmental LTS

(\Gamma_{1};\Lambda_{1};\Delta_{1})\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\ell}}(\Gamma_{2};\Lambda_{2};\Delta_{2})
, which describes reductions of typing environments.
This LTS describes the way a typing context can evolve in accordance with its session types.
On top of these layers there are notions of
refined environmental LTS
and
refined LTS for processes
.
The former restricts the environmental LTS to inputs on characteristic values, as we discussed in
Section

4.1
.
Finally, the refined LTS for processes restricts the untyped LTS to those actions which are supported by the refined environmental LTS.

We follow this approach for defining the refined LTS for MST processes.
Both the untyped LTS for processes and the environmental LTS for MST processes coincides with the same LTSs for
\mathsf{HO}
(or, to be more precise, with its restriction to minimal session types).
It remains, then, to define the refined environmental LTS for MST processes, with the idea that the
refined LTS restricts inputs to the inputs on
minimal characteristic values
and
minimal trigger values
.

Definition 4.2

(Minimal trigger value)
.

Given a value type
{C}\leadsto\diamond
and fresh (indexed) name
t_{1}
, the
minimal trigger
value
on
t_{1}
of type
{{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C})}}\leadsto\diamond
is defined as the abstraction

\lambda\widetilde{x}.\,t_{1}?(y).y\,{\widetilde{x}}

where
\widetilde{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{|{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C})}|})
.

Definition 4.3

(Minimal characteristic values)
.

Let
u
be a name and
i>0
.
We define
\langle-\rangle^{u}_{i}
and
\langle-\rangle
on types as follows.

\begin{array}[]{rclcrcl}\langle?(L){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}S\rangle^{u}_{i}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathtt{def}\ }}{{=}}&u_{i}?(x).(t_{1}!\langle\ulcorner u_{i+1},\ldots,u_{i+|{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S})}|}\urcorner\rangle.\mathbf{0}\;|\;\langle L\rangle^{x}_{i})&&\langle S\rangle&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathtt{def}\ }}{{=}}&\widetilde{s}\penalty\ \penalty\ (|\widetilde{s}|=|{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S})}|,\widetilde{s}\textrm{ fresh})\\
\langle!\langle L\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}S\rangle^{u}_{i}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathtt{def}\ }}{{=}}&u_{i}!\langle\langle L\rangle\rangle.t_{1}!\langle\ulcorner u_{i+1},\ldots,u_{i+|{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S})}|}\urcorner\rangle.\mathbf{0}&&\langle\langle L\rangle\rangle&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathtt{def}\ }}{{=}}&a_{1}\penalty\ \penalty\ (a_{1}\textrm{ fresh})\\
\langle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}\rangle^{u}_{i}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathtt{def}\ }}{{=}}&\mathbf{0}&&\langle C\!\rightarrow\!\diamond\rangle&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathtt{def}\ }}{{=}}&\lambda(x_{1},\ldots,x_{|{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C})}|}).\,\langle C\rangle^{x}_{1}\\
\langle\mu\mathsf{t}.S\rangle^{u}_{i}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathtt{def}\ }}{{=}}&\langle S\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\mathsf{t}$}\}\rangle^{u}_{i}\par&&\langle C\!\multimap\!\diamond\rangle&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathtt{def}\ }}{{=}}&\lambda(x_{1},\ldots,x_{|{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C})}|}).\,\langle C\rangle^{x}_{1}\\
\langle\langle L\rangle\rangle^{u}_{i}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathtt{def}\ }}{{=}}&u_{1}!\langle\langle L\rangle\rangle.t_{1}!\langle\ulcorner u_{1}\urcorner\rangle.\mathbf{0}\\
\langle C\!\rightarrow\!\diamond\rangle^{x}_{i}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathtt{def}\ }}{{=}}&x\,{\langle C\rangle}\\
\langle C\!\multimap\!\diamond\rangle^{x}_{i}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathtt{def}\ }}{{=}}&x\,{\langle C\rangle}\end{array}

where
t_{1}
is a fresh (indexed) name.
In this definition we use name-passing constructs, as outlined in
Example

2.1
.

Definition 4.4

(Refined environmental LTS)
.

The refined LTS, denoted
\mathrel{\xmapsto{\penalty\ \ell\penalty\ }}_{\texttt{m}}
, is defined
on top of the environmental LTS using the following rules:

\frac{(\Gamma_{1};\Lambda_{1};\Delta_{1})\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\ell}}(\Gamma_{2};\Lambda_{2};\Delta_{2})\ell\not=n?\langle V\rangle}{(\Gamma_{1};\Lambda_{1};\Delta_{1})\mathrel{\xmapsto{\penalty\ \ell\penalty\ }}_{\texttt{m}}(\Gamma_{2};\Lambda_{2};\Delta_{2})}\quad\textsc{{\footnotesize[{MTr}]}}

\frac{(\Gamma_{1};\Lambda_{1};\Delta_{1})\mathrel{\xrightarrow{n?\langle V\rangle}}(\Gamma_{2};\Lambda_{2};\Delta_{2})\left(V\equiv\langle L\rangle\right)\vee\left(V\equiv\lambda{\widetilde{x}}.\,t_{1}?(y).(y\,{{\widetilde{x}}})\right)\quad\textrm{ {\small with $t_{1}$ fresh}}}{(\Gamma_{1};\Lambda_{1};\Delta_{1})\mathrel{\xmapsto{n?\langle V\rangle}}_{\texttt{m}}(\Gamma_{2};\Lambda_{2};\Delta_{2})}\quad\textsc{{\footnotesize[{MRcv}]}}

where
\lambda{\widetilde{x}}.\,t_{1}?(y).(y\,{{\widetilde{x}}})
is
a minimal trigger value of type
{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C})}
(
Definition

4.2
).

Finally, the refined LTS for MST processes is just a combination of the untyped LTS with the refined environmental LTS:

Definition 4.5

(Refined LTS)
.

The environmental refined LTS extends to the typed refined LTS on processes.
We write
\Gamma_{1};\Lambda_{1};\Delta_{1}\vdash P_{1}\mathrel{\xmapsto{\penalty\ \ell\penalty\ }}_{\texttt{m}}\Lambda^{\prime}_{1};\Delta^{\prime}_{1}\vdash P_{2}

when

•

P_{1}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\ell}}P_{2}
, and

•

(\Gamma_{1};\Lambda_{1};\Delta_{1})\mathrel{\xmapsto{\penalty\ \ell\penalty\ }}_{\texttt{m}}(\Gamma_{2};\Lambda_{2};\Delta_{2})
.

We write
\mathrel{{\mathrel{\to}^{\ell}}}_{\texttt{m}}
for the weak version of the transition
\mathrel{\xmapsto{\ell}}_{\texttt{m}}
.
Notice that while the untyped LTS and the non-refined environmental LTS coincide
with that of
\mathsf{HO}
, the refinement that we impose on the environmental LTS is
different from its
\mathsf{HO}
counterpart. Specifically in Rule
[MRcv]
we take
special care to use minimal
characteristic processes
\langle-\rangle
, instead of general
\mathsf{HO}
characteristic process
[\!\!(-)\!\!]_{\mathsf{c}}

as defined in
[
19
]
.

Relating trigger and characteristic values.

As we mentioned earlier, the notion of bisimulation that we consider requires matching transitions of the source
\mathsf{HO}
term with the transitions of the target MST term.
However, the two transitions might differ on the inputs of characteristic values.
We accommodate for that difference by establishing a relation between the trigger and characteristic values of
\mathsf{HO}
and MST.

Definition 4.6
.

We define the relation
\bowtie
between
\mathsf{HO}
processes and indexed processes inductively as:

\frac{|\tilde{x}|=|{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C})}|}{\lambda x:C.\,t?(y).y\,{x}\bowtie\lambda\tilde{x}:{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C})}.\,t_{1}?(y).y\,{\tilde{x}}}\qquad\frac{}{[\!\!({C}\leadsto\diamond)\!\!]_{\mathsf{c}}\bowtie\langle{C}\leadsto\diamond\rangle{}}

where
\lambda{\widetilde{x}}:{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C})}.\,t_{1}?(y).(y\,{{\widetilde{x}}})
is
a minimal trigger value of type
{{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C})}}\leadsto\diamond
(
Definition

4.2
)
and
[\!\!(-)\!\!]_{\mathsf{c}}
denotes the characteristic values
defined in
[
19
]
.
We write
\lambda x:C.\,t_{1}?(y).y\,{x}
to mean that value

\lambda x.\,t_{1}?(y).y\,{x}
is of type
{C}\leadsto\diamond
.

Trigger processes and MST bisimilarity.

Before we give the definition of MST bisimilarity, we establish the following notations:

Definition 4.7

(Indexed name)
.

Given a name
n
, we write
\breve{n}
to either denote

n
or any indexed name
n_{i}
, with
i>0
.

Definition 4.8

(Trigger process)
.

Given a value
V
, a trigger process for a fresh (indexed) name

t_{1}
is defined as:

t_{1}\hookleftarrow_{\mathtt{H}}V\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathtt{def}\ }}{{=}}t_{1}?(\widetilde{x}).(V\ \widetilde{x})

where
|\widetilde{x}|=|\widetilde{C}|
for
V:{\widetilde{C}}\leadsto\diamond
.

Lemma 4.1
.

If
\Gamma;\Lambda;\Delta\vdash V\triangleright{\widetilde{C}}\leadsto\diamond
,
then
\Gamma;\Lambda;\Delta,t_{1}:?(\widetilde{C})\vdash t_{1}\hookleftarrow_{\mathtt{H}}V\triangleright\diamond
.

Finally, we are ready to formally define
MST
bisimilarity.

Definition 4.9

(MST Bisimilarity)
.

A typed relation
\Re
is an
MST bisimulation
if
for all
\Gamma_{1};\Lambda_{1};\Delta_{1}\vdash P_{1}\ \Re\ \Gamma_{2};\Lambda_{2};\Delta_{2}\vdash Q_{1}
,

1)

Whenever

\Gamma_{1};\Lambda_{1};\Delta_{1}\vdash P_{1}\mathrel{\xmapsto{(\nu\,\widetilde{m_{1}})\,n!\langle V_{1}\rangle}}\Lambda^{\prime}_{1};\Delta^{\prime}_{1}\vdash P_{2}

then there exist

Q_{2}
,
\Delta^{\prime}_{2}
, and
\Lambda^{\prime}_{2}
such that

\Gamma_{2};\Lambda_{2};\Delta_{2}\vdash Q_{1}\mathrel{{\mathrel{\to}^{(\nu\,\widetilde{m_{2}})\,\breve{n}!\langle V_{2}\rangle}}}_{\texttt{m}}\Lambda^{\prime}_{2};\Delta_{2}^{\prime}\vdash Q_{2}

where, for a fresh
t
,

\Gamma_{1};\Lambda_{1};\Delta^{\prime\prime}_{1}\vdash{(\nu\,\widetilde{m_{1}})(P_{2}\;|\;t\hookleftarrow_{\mathtt{H}}V_{1})}\ \Re\ \Gamma_{2};\Lambda_{2};\Delta^{\prime\prime}_{2}\vdash{(\nu\,\widetilde{m_{2}})(Q_{2}\;|\;\breve{t}\hookleftarrow_{\mathtt{H}}V_{2})}

2)

Whenever
\Gamma_{1};\Lambda_{1};\Delta_{1}\vdash P_{1}\mathrel{\xmapsto{n?(V_{1})}}\Lambda_{1}^{\prime};\Delta_{1}^{\prime}\vdash P_{2}

then there exist
Q_{2}
,
\Lambda_{2}^{\prime}
, and
\Delta_{2}^{\prime}
such that

\Gamma_{2};\Lambda_{2};\Delta_{2}\vdash Q_{1}\mathrel{{\mathrel{\to}^{\breve{n}?(V_{2})}}}_{\texttt{m}}\Lambda^{\prime}_{2},\Delta_{2}^{\prime}\vdash Q_{2}

where

V_{1}\bowtie V_{2}

and

\Gamma_{1};\Lambda_{1}^{\prime};\Delta_{1}^{\prime}\vdash P_{2}\ \Re\ \Gamma_{2};\Lambda_{2}^{\prime};\Delta_{2}^{\prime}\vdash Q_{2}
,

3)

The symmetric cases of 1 and 2.

The largest such bisimulation is called
MST bisimilarity
, denoted by
\approx^{\mathtt{M}}
.

In all clauses,
we use the refined LTS (
Definition

4.5
) and rely on notation

\breve{n}
(
Definition

4.7
). In the output clause, we use the
triggers (
Definition

4.8
). In the input clause, we use the
relation
\bowtie
on values (
Definition

4.6
).

We discuss differences between MST bisimilarity and higher-order bisimilarity as defined in
[
19
]
.
First, an action in
P_{1}
must be matched by an action on an indexed name in
Q_{1}
, and refined LTS actions in
P_{1}
are matched by minimal refined
LTS actions in
Q_{1}
(
Definition

4.6
). As a consequence of the latter,
in the input case the observed values are not identical but related by
\bowtie
(
Definition

4.6
).
In other words, whenever
P_{1}

receives a trigger or a characteristic value, then
Q_{1}
should receive their minimal
counterparts (
Definitions

4.2
and
4.3
). Further, as names could be
indexed on the right-hand side, the typing environments could differ for open processes, so the MST bisimilarity assumes different typing environments on both sides.

4.3
The Bisimulation Relation

Our goal is to complement our static correctness result (
3.1
) by
proving the following statement about the decomposition of processes (
Definition

3.9
):

Theorem 4.1
.

Let
P
be an
\mathsf{HO}
process such that
\Gamma;\Delta;\Lambda\vdash P\triangleright\diamond
.
We have

\Gamma;\Lambda;\Delta\vdash P\ \approx^{\mathtt{M}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma})};{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Lambda})};{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta})}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P})}

To show that
P
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P})}
are MST-bisimilar, we provide a concrete bisimulation relation
\mathbin{\mathcal{S}}
that contains
(P,{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P})})
.
Defining
\mathbin{\mathcal{S}}
to be just the set of such pairs is, however, not going to work; instead, the relation
\mathbin{\mathcal{S}}
should also contain pairs corresponding to “intermediate” states in which the process and its decomposition may get “desynchronized”.
Before we give the concrete definition of
\mathbin{\mathcal{S}}
we look at an example, illustrating the need for such intermediate pairs.

4.3.1
A Motivating Example

Consider the following process:

\displaystyle P_{1}=u?(t).v?(x).(\nu\,s:S)\,(u!\langle x\rangle.\mathbf{0}\;|\;t\,{s}\;|\;\overline{s}!\langle x\rangle.\mathbf{0})\;|\;\overline{v}!\langle V\rangle.\mathbf{0}

where
u:?(\langle U_{t}\rangle){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}!\langle U_{V}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}
and
v:S
with

S={?(U_{V})}
,
U_{t}=S\!\rightarrow\!\diamond
, and

U_{V}
is some shared value type, i.e.
U_{V}=S_{V}\!\rightarrow\!\diamond
, for some

S_{V}
.
Further,

is some value, such that
V=\lambda y:S_{V}.\,R
.

Thus,

is typed using the typing of its constituents:

\emptyset;\emptyset;\overline{v}:\overline{S}\vdash\overline{v}!\langle V\rangle.\mathbf{0}\triangleright\diamond

\emptyset;\emptyset;u:?(\langle U_{t}\rangle){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}!\langle U_{V}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}},v:S\vdash u?(t).v?(x).(\nu\,s:S)\,(u!\langle x\rangle.\mathbf{0}\;|\;t\,{s}\;|\;\overline{s}!\langle x\rangle.\mathbf{0})\triangleright\diamond

The decomposition of

, denoted schematically in

.
First, let us consider a possible (refined) transition of
P_{1}
, an input on

\displaystyle P_{1}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{u?\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}V_{C}}\rangle}}v?(x).(\nu\,s:S)\,(u!\langle x\rangle.\mathbf{0}\;|\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}V_{C}}\,{s}\;|\;\overline{s}!\langle x\rangle.\mathbf{0})\;|\;\overline{v}!\langle V\rangle.\mathbf{0}=P_{2}

where
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}V_{C}}=[\!\!(U_{t})\!\!]_{\mathsf{c}}=\lambda y:S.\,y?(x^{\prime}).(!\langle\rangle.\mathbf{0}\;|\;x^{\prime}\,{s^{\prime}})
is the
characteristic value
of

.
1
1

1

We use
blue

.

Process

can weakly match this input action on the indexed name

.

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}V_{C}}

We have:

\displaystyle Q_{1}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\tau}}Q^{\prime}_{1}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\tau}}Q^{\prime\prime}_{1}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{u_{1}?\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}V^{m}_{C}}\rangle}}(\nu\,\widetilde{c}_{\bullet})\,\overline{c_{3}}!\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}V^{m}_{C}}\rangle\;|\;c_{3}?(t).v_{1}?(x).\overline{c_{4}}!\langle t,x\rangle\;|\;\overline{c_{11}}!\langle\rangle

\displaystyle\quad\qquad\qquad\;|\;(\nu\,s_{1})\,(c_{4}?(t,x).\overline{c_{5}}!\langle x\rangle.\overline{c_{7}}!\langle t,x\rangle\;|\;c_{5}?(x).u_{2}!\langle x\rangle.\overline{c_{6}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{6}?()\;|\;

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\;|\;c_{7}?(t,x).\overline{c_{8}}!\langle t\rangle.\overline{c_{9}}!\langle x\rangle\;|\;c_{8}?(t).t\,{s_{1}}\;|\;c_{9}?(x).\overline{s_{1}}!\langle x\rangle.\overline{c_{10}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{10}?())

\displaystyle\quad\qquad\qquad\;|\;c_{11}?().\overline{v_{1}}!\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V}\big)}\rangle.\overline{c_{12}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{12}?()=Q_{2}

where
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}V^{m}_{C}}=\langle U_{t}\rangle=\lambda(y_{1}).\,y_{1}?(x^{\prime}).(t_{1}!\langle\rangle.\mathbf{0}\;|\;x^{\prime}\,{\widetilde{s}^{\prime}})
,
with
y_{1}:?(S)
,
|\widetilde{s}^{\prime}|=|{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S_{V}})}|
, and
\widetilde{c}_{\bullet}=c_{3},\ldots,c_{12}
.

Hence, we should have
P_{2}\penalty\ \mathbin{\mathcal{S}}\penalty\ Q_{2}
.
Observe that
Q_{2}
is not exactly the decomposition of
P_{2}
.
First,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}V^{m}_{C}}
is not the breakdown of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}V_{C}}
.
Second,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}V^{m}_{C}}
is not at the same position in
Q_{2}
as
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}V_{C}}
; the later being in the application position and the former being pushed through several propagators.
Therefore, the relation

\,\mathcal{S}
needs to (1) relate
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}V_{C}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}V^{m}_{C}}
and (2) account for the fact that a value related to

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}V_{C}}
and thus it needs to be propagated (as in
Q_{2}
).
To address the first point, we establish a relation
\boxtimes
between characteristic values and their minimal counterparts.
For the second point, we record this fact by “decomposing” the process as
P_{2}=P^{\prime}_{2}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$V_{C}$}\!/\mbox{\small$t$}\}
, and propagating the information about this substitution when computing the set of processes that are related to
P_{2}
.

The same considerations we mentioned also apply to the value
V
, which is
transmitted internally, via a synchronization:

\displaystyle P_{2}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\tau}}(\nu\,s)\,(u!\langle V\rangle.\mathbf{0}\;|\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}V_{C}}\,{s}\;|\;\overline{s}!\langle V\rangle.\mathbf{0})=P_{3}

Value
V
transmitted in
P_{2}
should be related to its corresponding
breakdown
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V}\big)}
, which should be propagated through
the decomposition:

\displaystyle Q_{2}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\tau}}Q^{\prime}_{2}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\tau}}Q^{\prime\prime}_{2}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{\tau}}(\nu\,\widetilde{c}_{\bullet\bullet})\,\overline{c_{4}}!\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}V^{m}_{C}},{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V}\big)}\rangle

\displaystyle\quad\qquad\qquad\;|\;(\nu\,s_{1})\,(c_{4}?(t,x).\overline{c_{5}}!\langle x\rangle.\overline{c_{7}}!\langle t,x\rangle\;|\;c_{5}?(x).u_{2}!\langle x\rangle.\overline{c_{6}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{6}?()\;|\;

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\;|\;c_{7}?(t,x).\overline{c_{8}}!\langle t\rangle.\overline{c_{9}}!\langle x\rangle\;|\;c_{8}?(t).t\,{s_{1}}\;|\;c_{9}?(x).\overline{s_{1}}!\langle x\rangle.\overline{c_{10}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{10}?())\;|\;

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\;|\;\overline{c_{12}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{12}?()=Q_{3}

where
\widetilde{c}_{\bullet\bullet}=c_{4},\ldots,c_{10},c_{12}
.

Now, in
P_{3}
we can observe the output of
V
along
u
:

\displaystyle P_{3}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{u!\langle V\rangle}}(\nu\,s)\,(\mathbf{0}\;|\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}V_{C}}\,{s}\;|\;\overline{s}!\langle x\rangle.\mathbf{0})=P_{4}

Process
Q_{3}
mimics this action by sending the process

{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V}\big)}

along name
u_{2}
:

\displaystyle Q_{3}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\xRightarrow{u_{2}!\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V}\big)}\rangle}}(\nu\,\widetilde{c}_{*})\,\overline{c_{7}}!\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}V^{m}_{C}},{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V}\big)}\rangle\;|\;\overline{c_{6}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{6}?()\;|\;

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\;|\;c_{7}?(t,x).\overline{c_{8}}!\langle t\rangle.\overline{c_{9}}!\langle x\rangle\;|\;c_{8}?(t).t\,{s_{1}}\;|\;c_{9}?(x).\overline{s_{1}}!\langle x\rangle.\overline{c_{10}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{10}?())=Q_{4}

where
\widetilde{c}_{*}=c_{6},\ldots,c_{10}
.
Following the definition of higher-order bisimilarity,
we should have:

\displaystyle P_{4}\parallel{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t^{\prime}\hookleftarrow_{\mathtt{H}}V}\penalty\ \mathbin{\mathcal{S}}\penalty\ Q_{4}\parallel{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t_{1}^{\prime}\hookleftarrow_{\mathtt{H}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V}\big)}}

for a fresh
t^{\prime}
, where we have used ‘
\parallel
’ (rather than ‘
|
’) to denote process composition: we find it convenient to highlight those sub-processes in parallel that originate from trigger and characteristic processes.

We can see that the trigger process for
V
on the left-hand side should be matched with a trigger process for the
breakdown
of
V
on the
right-hand side.
Moreover, the definition of trigger processes should be generalized to
polyadic values, as
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V}\big)}
could be polyadic
(see
Definition

4.8
).

Let us briefly consider how
P_{4}\parallel{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t^{\prime}\hookleftarrow_{\mathtt{H}}V}

evolves after due to the synchronization in sub-process

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}V_{c}}\,{s}
within
P_{4}
:

\displaystyle P_{4}\parallel{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t^{\prime}\hookleftarrow_{\mathtt{H}}V}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\tau}}(\nu\,s)\,({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}s?(x^{\prime}).(t!\langle\rangle\;|\;x^{\prime}\,{s^{\prime}})}\parallel\overline{s}!\langle V\rangle.\mathbf{0})\parallel{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t^{\prime}\hookleftarrow_{\mathtt{H}}V}=P_{6}\parallel{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t^{\prime}\hookleftarrow_{\mathtt{H}}V}

We can see that
Q_{4}
can mimic this synchronization after a few administrative
reductions on propagators:

\displaystyle Q_{4}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\xRightarrow{\tau}}(\nu\,c_{9}c_{10})\,\overline{c_{9}}!\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V}\big)}\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}s_{1}?(x^{\prime}).(t_{1}!\langle\rangle\;|\;x^{\prime}\,{\widetilde{s}^{\prime}})}\;|\;c_{9}?(x).\overline{s_{1}}!\langle x\rangle.\overline{c_{10}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{10}?())\parallel{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t_{1}^{\prime}\hookleftarrow_{\mathtt{H}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V}\big)}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=Q_{6}\parallel{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t_{1}^{\prime}\hookleftarrow_{\mathtt{H}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V}\big)}}

Therefore, we need to have:

\displaystyle P_{6}\parallel{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t^{\prime}\hookleftarrow_{\mathtt{H}}V}\penalty\ \mathbin{\mathcal{S}}\penalty\ Q_{6}\parallel{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t_{1}^{\prime}\hookleftarrow_{\mathtt{H}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V}\big)}}

To ensure that this pair is in
\mathbin{\mathcal{S}}
, we introduce an auxiliary relation, denoted
\diamond
(
Definition

4.15
), which allows us to account for the sub-processes that originate from
characteristic values or trigger processes (in
blue
).
We need to account for them separately, because one of them is not the decomposition of the other.
We thus decree:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}s?(x^{\prime}).(t!\langle\rangle\;|\;x^{\prime}\,{s^{\prime}})}

\displaystyle\diamond{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}s_{1}?(x^{\prime}).(t_{1}!\langle\rangle\;|\;x^{\prime}\,{\widetilde{s}^{\prime}})}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t^{\prime}\hookleftarrow_{\mathtt{H}}V}

\displaystyle\diamond{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t_{1}^{\prime}\hookleftarrow_{\mathtt{H}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V}\big)}}

Next, the synchronization on
s
in
P_{6}
is mimicked by
Q_{6}
with a
synchronization on
s_{1}
:

\displaystyle P_{6}\parallel{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t\hookleftarrow_{\mathtt{H}}V}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\tau}}(t!\langle\rangle.\mathbf{0}\;|\;V\,{s^{\prime}})\parallel{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t^{\prime}\hookleftarrow_{\mathtt{H}}V}=P_{8}\parallel{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t^{\prime}\hookleftarrow_{\mathtt{H}}V}

\displaystyle Q_{6}\parallel{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t_{1}^{\prime}\hookleftarrow_{\mathtt{H}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V}\big)}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\xRightarrow{\tau}}(\nu\,c_{10})\,(t_{1}!\langle\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V}\big)}\,{\widetilde{s}^{\prime}})\;|\;\overline{c_{10}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{10}?())=Q_{8}\parallel{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t_{1}^{\prime}\hookleftarrow_{\mathtt{H}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V}\big)}}

Finally, we can see that after the output on the trigger name
t

there is an application that activates
R
, the body of
V
:

\displaystyle P_{8}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\xrightarrow{t!\langle\rangle}}V\,{s^{\prime}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\tau}}R\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$s^{\prime}$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\}

\displaystyle Q_{8}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\xrightarrow{t!\langle\rangle}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V}\big)}\,{\widetilde{s}^{\prime}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\tau}}(\nu\,\widetilde{c}_{**})\,\overline{c_{12}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}12}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}R}\big)}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\widetilde{s}^{\prime}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\widetilde{y}$}\}\equiv{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}R\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$s^{\prime}$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\}})}

We reached the point where we relate process
R\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$s^{\prime}$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\}
with its
decomposition
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}R\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$s^{\prime}$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\}})}
. Hence, the remaining pairs in
\mathbin{\mathcal{S}}
are obtained
in the same way.

Key insights.

We summarize some key insights from the example:

•

A received value can either be a pure value or a characteristic value.
In the former case, the pure value has to be related to its decomposition, but in the later case the value should be related to an MST characteristic value of the same type.
We define the relation
\boxtimes
on values to account for this (
Definition

4.13
).

•

Trigger processes mentioned in the output case of MST bisimilarity should be matched with their minimal counterparts, and the same applies to processes originating from such trigger processes.
The relation
\diamond
accounts for this (see
Definition

4.15
).

•

Any value in process
P
could have been previously received. The definition
of
\mathbin{\mathcal{S}}
takes this into account by explicitly relating processes with
substitutions (see
Definition

4.17
). That is, for
P
, it relates

P^{\prime}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}
such that
P^{\prime}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}=P
.
Here, the substitution
\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}
records values that should be propagated.

4.3.2
The relation
\mathbin{\mathcal{S}}

In this section we give the definition of the relation
\mathbin{\mathcal{S}}
(
Definition

4.17
), following the insights gathered from the example.
More specifically, we define

•

a relation
\boxtimes
on values, which includes the relation
\bowtie
from
Definition

4.6
, (
Definition

4.13
);

•

a relation
\diamond
on processes, for relating characteristic and trigger processes with their MST counterparts, (
Definition

4.15
);

•

a set
\mathcal{C}^{\tilde{W}}_{\tilde{x}}\big(P\big)
of processes
correlated
to a process
P\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}
, (
Table

3
).

Because we will be working extensively with indexed processes, we will use the following function, which returns a set of all valid indexing substitutions for a list of names.

Definition 4.10

(Indexed names substitutions)
.

Let

\widetilde{u}=(a,b,r,\overline{r},r^{\prime},\overline{r}^{\prime},s,\overline{s},s^{\prime},\overline{s}^{\prime},\ldots)

be a finite tuple of names,
where
a,b,\ldots
denote shared names,
r,\overline{r},r^{\prime},\overline{r}^{\prime},\ldots
denote tail-recursive names , and
s,\overline{s},s^{\prime},\overline{s}^{\prime},\ldots
denote linear (non tail-recursive names).
We write
\mathsf{index}(\widetilde{u})
to denote

\displaystyle\mathsf{index}(\widetilde{u})=\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$a_{1},b_{1},r_{1},\overline{r}_{1},r^{\prime}_{1},\overline{r}^{\prime}_{1},s_{i},\overline{s}_{i},s^{\prime}_{j},\overline{s}^{\prime}_{j},\ldots$}\!/\mbox{\small$a,b,r,\overline{r},r^{\prime},\overline{r}^{\prime},s,\overline{s},s^{\prime},\overline{s}^{\prime},\ldots:i,j,\ldots>0$}\}

Any substitution
\sigma\in\mathsf{index}(\mathtt{fn}(P))
turns an
\mathsf{HO}
process
P
into an indexed process
P\sigma
.

Correlated values.

The main ingredient in defining the relation
\mathbin{\mathcal{S}}
is the the set
\mathcal{C}^{\tilde{W}}_{\tilde{x}}\big(P\big)
, which contains processes
correlated
to process
P
with a substitution
\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}
.
The substitution, as discussed above, denotes previously received values, and we assume that
\mathtt{fv}(P)=\widetilde{x}
.
Essentially,

\mathcal{C}^{-}_{-}\big(-\big)
computes a breakdown of
P\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}
in parallel with an activating trio, that mimics the original actions of
P
up to transitions on propagators.
The activating trio propagates not the original values
{\tilde{W}}
, but the values related to
{\tilde{W}}
.
To do that we introduce the set
\mathcal{C}^{-}_{-}\big(V\big)
of correlated values and the relation
\boxtimes
on values, which are defined mutually recursively in the three following definitions.

Definition 4.11

(Broken down values)
.

Given a value
V
, the set
\mathcal{C}\big(V\big)
is defined as follows:

\mathcal{C}\big(V\big)=\bigcup\big\{\mathcal{C}^{\tilde{W}}_{\tilde{x}}\big(V^{\prime}\big):V=V^{\prime}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}\mbox{ and $V^{\prime}$ is not a variable}\big\}

We extend
\mathcal{C}\big(-\big)
to work on a list of values
\widetilde{V}
component-wise,
that is:

\mathcal{C}\big(V_{1},\ldots,V_{n}\big)=\{B_{1},\ldots,B_{n}:B_{i}\in\mathcal{C}\big(V_{i}\big)\ \text{for}\ i\in 1\ldots n\}.

This way, the elements in
\mathcal{C}\big(V\big)
differ in the propagated values
\widetilde{W}
. Consider the following example:

Example 4.1
.

Let
V=\lambda y.\,y!\langle V_{1}\rangle.y!\langle V_{2}\rangle.\mathbf{0}
.
There are four possibilities of
V^{\prime}
,
\widetilde{W}
, and
\widetilde{x}

such that
V=V^{\prime}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}
.
That is,

•

V=V^{1}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$V_{1}V_{2}$}\!/\mbox{\small$x_{1}x_{2}$}\}

where
V_{1}=\lambda y.\,y!\langle x_{1}\rangle.y!\langle x_{2}\rangle.\mathbf{0}

•

V=V^{2}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$V_{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$x_{1}$}\}
where
V^{2}=\lambda y.\,y!\langle x_{1}\rangle.y!\langle V_{2}\rangle.\mathbf{0}

•

V=V^{3}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$V_{2}$}\!/\mbox{\small$x_{2}$}\}
where

V^{3}=\lambda y.\,y!\langle V_{1}\rangle.y!\langle x_{2}\rangle.\mathbf{0}

•

Finally, we can take the identity substitution
\widetilde{W}=\epsilon
and

\widetilde{x}=\epsilon
.

Thus, we have

\mathcal{C}\big(V\big)=\big\{\mathcal{C}^{V_{1}V_{2}}_{x_{1}x_{2}}\big(V^{1}\big),\ \mathcal{C}^{V_{1}}_{x_{1}}\big(V^{2}\big),\ \mathcal{C}^{V_{2}}_{x_{2}}\big(V^{3}\big),\ \mathcal{C}^{\epsilon}_{\epsilon}\big(V\big)\big\}
.

Definition 4.12
.

Given a value
V
, the set
\mathcal{C}^{\tilde{W}}_{\tilde{x}}\big(V\big)
, where
\mathtt{fn}(V)=\widetilde{x}
is defined as follows:

\mathcal{C}^{\tilde{W}}_{\tilde{x}}\big(V\big)=\big\{{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{x}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V}\big)}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{B}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}\mid\widetilde{W}\boxtimes\widetilde{B}\big\}.

Definition 4.13

(Relating values)
.

The relation
\boxtimes
on values (with indexed names) is defined as follows:

V_{1}\boxtimes V_{2}\iff\begin{cases}\exists V^{\prime}_{1},\,\sigma\in\mathsf{index}(\mathtt{fn}(V^{\prime}_{1})).\ V_{1}=V^{\prime}_{1}\sigma\wedge V^{\prime}_{1}\bowtie V_{2}&\mbox{ if $V_{1}$ is a characteristic or a trigger value}\\
V_{2}\in\mathcal{C}\big(V_{1}\big)&\mbox{ otherwise. }\end{cases}

where
\bowtie
is the relation from
Definition

4.6
.

Thus, in the definition of
\mathcal{C}^{\tilde{W}}_{\tilde{x}}\big(V\big)
, the value
V
is related to the triggered break down values with
\widetilde{B}
substituted for
\widetilde{x}
such that
\widetilde{W}\boxtimes\widetilde{B}
.

Additionally,
to define
\mathcal{C}^{\tilde{W}}_{\tilde{x}}\big(-\big)
for processes,
we have to observe the behavior of processes enclosed in the received trigger and characteristic values.
Further, we have to observe the behavior of
trigger processes of shape
t\hookleftarrow_{\mathtt{H}}V
.
For this we need to define a relation
\diamond
on processes that contains pairs

([\!\!(C)\!\!]^{x},\penalty\ \langle C\rangle^{x}_{1}),(t?(y).y\,{x},\penalty\ t_{1}?(y).y\,{\widetilde{x}}),(t\hookleftarrow_{\mathtt{H}}V,\penalty\ t_{1}\hookleftarrow_{\mathtt{H}}W)

where
x:C
and
|\widetilde{x}|=|{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C})}|
and
V\boxtimes W
.

Before we define
\diamond
we need the following auxiliary definition:

Definition 4.14

(Relating names)
.

We define
\diamond
as the relation on names defined as

\frac{}{\epsilon\diamond\epsilon}\qquad\frac{\Gamma;\Lambda;\Delta\vdash n_{i}\triangleright C}{n_{i}\diamond(n_{i},\ldots,n_{i+|{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C})}|-1})}\qquad\frac{\tilde{n}\diamond\tilde{m}_{1}\ \quad n_{i}\diamond\tilde{m}_{2}}{\tilde{n},n_{i}\diamond\tilde{m}_{1},\tilde{m}_{2}}

where
\epsilon
denotes the empty list.

Now, we are ready to relate processes, modulo indexed names
(cf.
Definition

4.7
), using the relation
\diamond
defined as follows:

Definition 4.15

(
\diamond
Indexed process relation)
.

We define the relation
\diamond
as

\frac{V\boxtimes Wx_{i}\diamond\tilde{x}}{V\,{x_{i}}\diamond W\,{\tilde{x}}}\quad\textsc{{\footnotesize[{IPApp}]}}\qquad\frac{P\diamond P^{\prime}Q\diamond Q^{\prime}}{P\;|\;Q\diamond P^{\prime}\;|\;Q^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{{\footnotesize[{IPPar}]}}\\
\frac{}{\mathbf{0}\diamond\mathbf{0}}\quad\textsc{{\footnotesize[{IPInact}]}}\qquad\frac{P\diamond P^{\prime}\tilde{m}_{1}\diamond\tilde{m}_{2}}{(\nu\,\tilde{m}_{1})\,P\diamond(\nu\,\tilde{m}_{2})\,P^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{{\footnotesize[{IPNews}]}}\\
\frac{P\sigma\diamond P^{\prime}V\sigma\boxtimes W\sigma=\mathsf{next}(n_{i})}{n_{i}!\langle V\rangle.P\diamond n_{i}!\langle W\rangle.P^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{{\footnotesize[{IPSnd}]}}\qquad\frac{P\sigma\diamond P^{\prime}\sigma=\mathsf{next}(n_{i})}{n_{i}?(y).P\diamond n_{i}?(y).P^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{{\footnotesize[{IPRcv}]}}

We can now show the property that we wanted, namely that: the bodies of trigger values and minimal trigger values (
Definition

4.2
) are related; the bodies of characteristic values and minimal characteristic values (
Definition

4.3
) are related; and that the trigger processes and minimal trigger processes
(
Definition

4.8
) are related, with appropriate name substitutions.

Lemma 4.2
.

We have:

\big\{([\!\!(C)\!\!]^{x}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$x_{i},t_{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$x,t$}\},\ \langle C\rangle^{x}_{i}),\penalty\ (t_{1}?(y).y\,{x}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$x_{i}$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\},\ t_{1}?(y).y\,{\widetilde{x}}),\penalty\ (t\hookleftarrow_{\mathtt{H}}V\sigma,\ t_{1}\hookleftarrow_{\mathtt{H}}W)\big\}\subset\diamond

where
i,j>0
,
x:C
,
\widetilde{x}=(x_{i},\ldots,x_{i+|{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C})}|-1})
,

\sigma\in\mathsf{index}(\widetilde{u})
,

\widetilde{u}=\mathtt{fn}(V)
, and

V\sigma\boxtimes W
.

Proof (Sketch).

We may notice that
\diamond
relates process
up to incremented indexed names and values related by
V\sigma\boxtimes W

for some
\sigma
. More precisely, free names as subject of actions are indexed and incremented
accordingly in
a related process, and names as objects of output actions
are broken down in a related process, by
V\sigma\bowtie W

when
V\sigma=m_{i}
, that is
m_{i}\bowtie\tilde{m}

where
m_{i}:C
and
\tilde{m}=(m_{i},\ldots,m_{i+|{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C})}|-1})
.

For the first pair
([\!\!(C)\!\!]^{x}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$x_{i},t_{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$x,t$}\},\penalty\ \langle C\rangle^{x}_{i})
by
inspection of

Definition

4.7
we can observe that
\langle C\rangle^{x}_{i}

is essentially
[\!\!(C)\!\!]^{x}
with its subject names indexed and
incremented (starting with
index
i
) and objects names broken down. Thus, it is contained in

\diamond
.
Similarly,
(t?(y).y\,{x}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$x_{i}$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\},\penalty\ t_{1}?(y).y\,{\widetilde{x}})
is contained by observing that

x_{i}\diamond\widetilde{x}
.
Finally, for
(t\hookleftarrow_{\mathtt{H}}V\sigma,\penalty\ t_{1}\hookleftarrow_{\mathtt{H}}W)
,
by
Definition

4.8
,
V\sigma\boxtimes W
.
∎

Correlated Processes.

Finally, we can use the introduced notions to define the set
\mathcal{C}^{-}_{-}\big(-\big)
of correlated processes.
As mentioned, the set
\mathcal{C}^{\tilde{W}}_{\tilde{x}}\big(P\big)
contains processes correlated to process
P
with a substitution
\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}
.
The definition of
\mathcal{C}^{-}_{-}\big(-\big)
is given in
Table

3
.
Before looking into the details, we first describe how the
\mathcal{C}^{-}_{-}\big(-\big)
is used.

We introduce auxiliary notions for treating
free (tail-recursive) names in processes.

Definition 4.16

(Auxiliary Notions)
.

Let
P
be an
\mathsf{HO}
process.

•

We write
\mathsf{fpn}(P)
to denote the set of free propagator names in
P
.

•

We define
\mathtt{rfv}(P)
to denote free tail-recursive names in
values in
P
.

•

We define
\mathsf{cr}(P)
to denote free names of form
c^{r}
in
P
.

•

We define
\mathsf{rfni}(P)
such that
r\in\mathsf{rfni}(P)
if and only if

(r_{i},\ldots,r_{j})\subseteq\mathtt{rn}(P)
for some
i,j>0
.

•

Given
r:S
and
\widetilde{r}=(r_{1},\ldots,r_{|{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S})}|})
,
we write
\mathcal{R}_{\tilde{v}}
to denote the process

\mathcal{R}_{\tilde{v}}=\prod_{r\in\tilde{v}}c^{r}?(x).x\,{\widetilde{r}}

Definition 4.17

(Relation
\mathbin{\mathcal{S}}
)
.

Let
P\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}
be a well-typed process
such that
\mathtt{fn}(P)\cap\mathtt{fn}(\widetilde{W})=\emptyset
, and let the

\mathcal{C}
-set be as in
Table

3
.
We define the relation
\mathbin{\mathcal{S}}
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbin{\mathcal{S}}

\displaystyle=\big\{\big(P\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\},(\nu\,\widetilde{c}_{r})\,(\nu\,\widetilde{c})\,R\big):\ R\in\mathcal{C}^{\tilde{W}\sigma}_{\tilde{x}}\big(P\sigma\big)

\displaystyle\qquad\text{with }\widetilde{u}=\mathtt{fn}(P\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}),\ \sigma\in\mathsf{index}(\widetilde{u}),\ \widetilde{c}_{r}=\mathsf{cr}(R),\ \widetilde{c}=\mathsf{fpn}(R)\big\}

Now we describe the definition of
\mathcal{C}^{-}_{-}\big(-\big)
in
Table

3
.
Essentially,
\mathcal{C}^{-}_{-}\big(-\big)
computes a breakdown of
P\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}
in parallel with an activating trio, that mimics the original actions of
P
up to transitions on propagators.
This is done with the help of
\mathcal{J}^{-}_{-}\big(-\big)
(also given in
Table

3
), which computes a closure of a process with respect to
\tau
-transitions on propagators.

To define the
\mathcal{C}
-set we distinguish processes that do not appear in the given process, but that are composed in parallel by the clauses of MST bisimilarity (
Definition

4.9
).
For this we use the following notions:

Definition 4.18

(Trigger Collections)
.

We let
H,H^{\prime}
to range over
trigger collections
: processes of the form
P_{1}\;|\;\cdots\;|\;P_{n}
(with
n\geq 1
),
where each
P_{i}
is a trigger process or a process that originates from a trigger or from a characteristic value.

Example 4.2
.

Let
H_{1}=t\hookleftarrow_{\mathtt{H}}V\;|\;[\!\!(C)\!\!]^{u}\;|\;t^{\prime}!\langle n\rangle.\mathbf{0}

where
t,t^{\prime},u,n
are channel names,

V
is a value, and

C
a channel type. Then,
we could see that
t^{\prime}!\langle n\rangle.\mathbf{0}

originates from a characteristic value. Thus,
H_{1}
is a trigger collection.

Notice that we write
P
to denote a “pure” process that is not composed with a trigger collection.
For processes with trigger collections, the following notation is relevant:

Definition 4.19

(Process in parallel with a trigger or a characteristic process)
.

We write

P\parallel Q
to stand for
P\;|\;Q

where either
P
or
Q
is a trigger collection.

\mathcal{C}^{\tilde{W}}_{\tilde{x}}\big(P\big)

\widetilde{y}=\mathtt{fv}(Q_{1})
,
