:
The sets
\mathcal{C}^{\tilde{W}}_{\tilde{x}}\big(P\big)
and
\mathcal{J}^{\tilde{W}}_{\tilde{x}}\big(P\big)
.

Now we can describe all the cases in the definitions of
the
\mathcal{J}
-set and the
\mathcal{C}
-set in

Table

3
(Page
3
).
Observe that the second and third columns in
Table

3
are closely
related: the third column lists side conditions for the definitions in the
second column.
Note that in each case we assume the substitution
\rho=\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}
.
We start with the cases for
\mathcal{C}^{\tilde{W}}_{\tilde{x}}\big(P\big)
:

Parallel with a trigger collection:

The
\mathcal{C}
-set of
Q_{1}\parallel Q_{2}
is defined as:

\displaystyle\{R_{1}\parallel R_{2}:R_{1}\in\mathcal{C}^{\tilde{W}_{1}}_{\tilde{y}}\big(Q_{1}\big),\ R_{2}\in\mathcal{C}^{\tilde{W}_{2}}_{\tilde{w}}\big(Q_{2}\big)\}

By
Definition

4.19
, either
Q_{1}
or
Q_{2}
is a trigger collection.
Notice that a composition
Q_{1}\;|\;Q_{2}
(where both
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
are “pure”)
is handled by
\mathcal{J}\big(-\big)
, see below.
We treat
Q_{1}\parallel Q_{2}
compositionally: we split the
substitution into parts concerning
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
, i.e.,

\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}=\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}_{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{y}$}\}\cdot\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}_{2}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{w}$}\}

such that
\widetilde{y}=\mathtt{fv}(Q_{1})
and
\widetilde{w}=\mathtt{fv}(Q_{2})
, and relate it to a parallel composition whose
components come from a corresponding
\mathcal{C}
-set.

Restriction:

The
\mathcal{C}
-set of
(\nu\,m:C)\,Q

is inductively defined as:

\displaystyle\left\{(\nu\,\widetilde{m}:{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C})})\,{\,R}:(\nu\,\tilde{c}^{m})\,R\in\mathcal{C}^{\tilde{W}}_{\tilde{x}}\big(Q\sigma\big)\right\}

where
\sigma=\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$m_{1}\overline{m_{1}}$}\!/\mbox{\small$m\overline{m}$}\}
and
\widetilde{m}=(m_{1},\ldots,m_{|{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C})}|})
is the decomposition of
m
under
C
. The
elements are processes from the
\mathcal{C}
-set of
Q
with names
\widetilde{m}

restricted. In the case when restricted name
m
is a tail-recursive
then we also restrict the special propagator names
c^{m}
and
c^{\overline{m}}

which appear in
R
.
Notice that the processes of the form
(\nu\,m)\,(Q_{1}\parallel Q_{2})
, which are induced by the output clause of MST bisimilarity, are treated in this case in the definition of
\mathcal{C}\big(-\big)
.

Pure process:

The
\mathcal{C}
-set of a pure process
Q
is defined as
follows:

\big\{\mathcal{R}_{\tilde{v}}\;|\;\overline{c_{k}}!\langle\widetilde{B}\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{x}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}\big)}:\widetilde{W}\boxtimes\widetilde{B}\big\}\cup\big\{\mathcal{R}_{\tilde{v}\setminus\tilde{r}}\;|\;R:R\in\mathcal{J}^{\tilde{W}}_{\tilde{x}}\big(Q\big),\penalty\ \widetilde{r}=\mathsf{rfni}(R)\big\}

where
\widetilde{v}=\mathtt{rn}(Q\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\})
.
The elements in the first set are
essentially the decomposition of
Q
(without restrictions of recursive
propagators, which are handled in
\mathbin{\mathcal{S}}
) up to
different possibilities of values
\widetilde{B}
that are
\boxtimes
-related
to
\widetilde{W}
(see
Definition

4.13
). Here, we remark that

\mathcal{R}_{\tilde{v}}
is
recursive name providers for all tail-recursive names of
Q

and
\widetilde{W}
(by
\widetilde{v}=\mathtt{rn}(Q\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\})
).
The second set contains elements of the
\mathcal{J}
-set of
Q
in
parallel with
\mathcal{R}_{\tilde{v}\setminus\tilde{r}}
where
\widetilde{r}=\mathsf{rfni}(R)
.
By
•
‣
Definition

4.16
we can see that
\mathsf{rfni}(R)
denotes tail-recursive names
already gathered in
R
by communications that consumed

\mathcal{R}_{\tilde{r}}
: thus, we have
\mathcal{R}_{\tilde{v}\setminus\tilde{r}}

as providers at top level.

In this sense, the processes from the second set can be seen as reducts of the processes from the first set.
For example, if we examine the
\mathcal{C}
-set corresponding to the process
P_{2}
from
Figure

7
, we note that the process
Q_{2}
belongs to the first set, and the processes
Q^{\prime}_{2}
and
Q^{\prime\prime}_{3}
belong to the second set.

Trigger collection:

The
\mathcal{C}
-set of a trigger collection
H

contains its minimal counterparts, defined using the relation
\diamond
(
Definition

4.15
):

\displaystyle\big\{\mathcal{R}_{\tilde{v}}\parallel H^{\prime}:H\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}\diamond H^{\prime}\big\}.

where
\widetilde{v}=\mathtt{rn}(H\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\})
.
In this case we do not use the information on the substitution
\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}
,
because the substitution information is needed for values that are, or were, propagated.
However, because
H
is a trigger collection, it will only contain propagators as part of values.
The substitutions related the propagators in values are already handled by the relation
\boxtimes
, invoked by
\diamond
.
As in the case with pure processes, the process
\mathcal{R}_{\tilde{r}}
is the recursive names provider for the tail-recursive names of
H
.

We now discuss the cases for
\mathcal{J}^{\tilde{W}}_{\tilde{x}}\big(P\big)
:

Output:

The
\mathcal{J}
-set of
u_{i}!\langle V_{1}\rangle.Q
depends on whether
(i)
u_{i}
is linear or shared name (i.e.,
\neg\mathsf{tr}(u_{i})
) or
(ii)
u_{i}
is a tail-recursive name (i.e.,
\mathsf{tr}(u_{i})
).
In sub-case (i)
\mathcal{J}
-set is defined as follows:

\big\{u_{i}!\langle V_{2}\rangle.\overline{c_{k}}!\langle\widetilde{B}_{2}\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{z}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q\sigma}\big)}:

V_{1}\sigma\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}_{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{y}$}\}\boxtimes V_{2},\ \widetilde{W}_{2}\boxtimes\widetilde{B}_{2}\big\}

where
\sigma=\mathsf{next}(u_{i})
. By the definition,
the substitution
\sigma
depends on whether
u_{i}
is linear or shared: in
the former case, we use a substitution that increments
u_{i}
; in the latter
case we use an identity substitution.
We split
\widetilde{W}
into
\widetilde{W}_{1}
and
\widetilde{W}_{2}
, associated to the emitted value

V_{1}
and the continuation
Q
, respectively.

Instead of the emitted value
V_{1}
we consider values
V_{2}
that are
\boxtimes
-related to
V_{1}\sigma\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}_{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{y}$}\}
.
This way, we uniformly handle cases when (i)
V_{1}
is a pure value, (ii) variable, and (iii) a characteristic value.
In particular, if
V_{1}
is a pure value, the set
\mathcal{C}^{\tilde{W}_{1}}_{\tilde{y}}\big(V_{1}\sigma\big)
is included in all the values
\boxtimes
-related to
V_{1}\sigma\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}_{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{y}$}\}
.

Further, the propagator
c_{k}
actives the next trio with the values
\widetilde{B}_{2}

such that
\widetilde{W}_{2}\boxtimes\widetilde{B}_{2}
: as
\widetilde{W}_{2}
denotes
previously received values, we take a context of
\boxtimes
-related values.
Again, received values could be either trigger and characteristic values
(required to be observed by MST bisimilarity, cf.
Definition

4.9
) or
pure values originated from internal actions. Again, by
\boxtimes

(
Definition

4.13
) we account for both cases.

In sub-case (ii), when
u_{i}
is a tail-recursive name, the elements are as follows:

\displaystyle\big\{c^{u}!\big\langle M^{\tilde{B}_{2}}_{V_{2}}\big\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{w}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}\big)},\ M^{\tilde{B}_{2}}_{V_{2}}\,{\widetilde{u}}\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{w}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}\big)},\ {u_{{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S}\rangle}}!\big\langle V_{2}\big\rangle.}{}(\overline{c_{k}}!\langle\widetilde{B}_{2}\rangle\;|\;c^{u}?(x).x\,{\widetilde{u}})\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{w}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}\big)}

\displaystyle\qquad:V_{1}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}_{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{y}$}\}\boxtimes V_{2},\ \widetilde{W}_{2}\boxtimes\widetilde{B}_{2}\big\}

\displaystyle\text{where }M^{\tilde{B}}_{V}=\lambda\widetilde{z}.\,z_{{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S}\rangle}}!\big\langle V\big\rangle.{}(\overline{c_{k}}!\langle\widetilde{B}\rangle\;|\;c^{u}?(x).x\,{\widetilde{z}})

The first element is a process obtained by the activation from the preceding
trio. The second element is a result of a communication of the first element
with top-level provider
\mathcal{R}_{u_{i}}
(
Definition

4.16
) on channel
c^{u}
.
By this synchronization, the decomposition of recursive name
u
, that is

\widetilde{u}
, is gathered in application
M^{\tilde{B}_{2}}_{V_{2}}\,{\widetilde{u}}
.
Finally, the third
element represents the result of the application: it is a process ready to
mimic the original output action on
u_{{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S}\rangle}}
. Differently from sub-case
(i), here we do not have to increment index of
u_{i}
in

Q
and
V_{1}
as indices of recursive names are obtained based on the type

S
, that is
{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S}\rangle}
.

Input:

The
\mathcal{J}
-set of
u_{i}?(y).Q
depends on whether
(i)
u_{i}
is linear or shared name (i.e.,
\neg\mathsf{tr}(u_{i})
) or
(ii)
u_{i}
is a tail-recursive name (i.e.,
\mathsf{tr}(u_{i})
).
In both sub-cases
\mathcal{J}
-set is defined similarly to
the output case, with only one caveat: we need to expand the context for the continuation with a newly received value
y
.
The
\mathcal{J}
-set in sub-case (i) is defined as follows:

\big\{u_{i}?(y).\overline{c_{k}}!\langle\widetilde{B}y\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{x}y}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q\sigma}\big)}:

\widetilde{W}\boxtimes\widetilde{B}\big\}

where
\sigma=\mathsf{next}(u_{i})
.
The
\mathcal{J}
-set in sub-case (ii) is defined as follows:

\big\{c^{u}!\big\langle M^{\tilde{B}}_{y}\big\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{x}y}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}\big)},\ M^{\tilde{B}}_{y}\,{\widetilde{u}}\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{x}y}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}\big)},\ {u_{{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S}\rangle}}?(y).(\overline{c_{k}}!\langle\widetilde{B}y\rangle\;|\;c^{u}?(x).x\,{\widetilde{z}})\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{x}y}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}\big)}}:\widetilde{W}\boxtimes\widetilde{B}\big\}

where:

M^{\tilde{B}}_{y}=\lambda\widetilde{z}.\,z_{{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S}\rangle}}?(y).(\overline{c_{k}}!\langle\widetilde{B}y\rangle\;|\;c^{u}?(x).x\,{\widetilde{z}}).

The elements of the set represent steps of obtaining
name
u_{{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S}\rangle}}
, along which the original action is mimicked,
by synchronizing with the
top-level provider
\mathcal{R}_{u_{i}}
, obtained in the corresponding
\mathcal{C}
-set.

Application:

The
\mathcal{J}
-set of
{V_{1}\,{(\widetilde{r},u_{i})}}

where
\widetilde{r}
are tail-recursive names, is a union of two sets as follows:

\displaystyle\big\{\overbracket{c^{r_{l}}!\big\langle\lambda\widetilde{z}_{l}.c^{r_{{l}+1}}!\langle\lambda\widetilde{z}_{{l}+1}.\cdots.c^{r_{n}}!\langle\lambda\widetilde{z}_{n}.}^{|\tilde{r}|-{l}+1}Q_{l}\rangle\,\rangle\big\rangle,\

\displaystyle\quad(\lambda\widetilde{z}_{l}.\overbracket{c^{r_{l+1}}!\langle\lambda\widetilde{z}_{l+1}.\cdots.c^{r_{n}}!\langle\lambda\widetilde{z}_{n}.}^{|\tilde{r}|-l}Q_{l}\rangle\big\rangle)\ {\widetilde{r}_{l}}:1\leq l\leq n,\penalty\ \penalty\ V_{1}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}\boxtimes V_{2}\big\}

\displaystyle\cup\big\{V_{2}\,{\widetilde{r}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{r}_{n},\widetilde{m}}:V_{1}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}\boxtimes V_{2}\big\}

where:

\displaystyle\qquad Q_{l}=V_{2}\,{(\widetilde{r}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{r}_{l-1},\widetilde{z}_{l},\ldots,\widetilde{z}_{n},\widetilde{m})}

The first set contains intermediate processes emerging while collecting recursive
names using synchronizations with
recursive name providers. We can see that the body of the inner-most abstraction,

Q_{l}
, is an application of
V_{2}
(such that

V_{1}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{y}$}\}\boxtimes V_{2}
)
to partially instantiated recursive names:

l
denotes that decompositions of first
l-1
recursive names are
retrieved. The final tuple in arguments of
Q_{l}
,
\widetilde{m}=(u_{i},\ldots,u_{i+|{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C})}|-1})
, is a full decomposition of non-recursive
(linear or shared) name
u_{i}
.
Just like in the previous cases, by taking
V_{2}
as a

\boxtimes
-related value to
V_{1}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{y}$}\}
,
we uniformly handle all the three possibilities for
V_{1}
(pure value, variable,
and characteristic value).

In the first set, the first element is a process is ready to send an
abstraction to an appropriate name provider, in order to retrieve the decomposition of

l
-th recursive name.
The second element is a process that results from a
communication of the first element with a provider: an application which
will instantiate
l
-th recursive name in
Q_{l}
.
Finally, the second set contains application processes in which
the decompositions of all
n
recursive names are gathered,
and it is ready to mimic the silent action (application reduction) of the original
process.

Parallel composition:

The
\mathcal{J}
-set of
Q_{1}\;|\;Q_{2}
is defined using two sets:

\big\{\overline{c_{k}}!\langle\widetilde{B}_{1}\rangle.\overline{c_{k+l}}!\langle\widetilde{B}_{2}\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{y}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{1}}\big)}\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k+l}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{z}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{2}}\big)}:

\widetilde{W}_{1}\boxtimes\widetilde{B}_{1}
,
\widetilde{W}_{2}\boxtimes\widetilde{B}_{2}\big\}

\cup

\big\{(R_{1}\;|\;R_{2}):R_{1}\in\mathcal{C}^{\tilde{W}_{1}}_{\tilde{y}}\big(Q_{1}\big),R_{2}\in\mathcal{C}^{\tilde{W}_{2}}_{\tilde{z}}\big(Q_{2}\big)\big\}

The first set contains a control trio that is ready to activate the
decomposition of the two components in parallel.
Just like in the other cases, the control trio propagates values that are
\boxtimes
-related to
{\tilde{W}_{1}}
and
{\tilde{W}_{2}}
.
In order to close the set with
respect to the
\tau
-actions on propagators, the second set contains the
composition of processes drawn from the
\mathcal{C}
-sets of
Q_{1}
and

Q_{2}
, with appropriate substitutions.

4.4
Proving Operational Correspondence

Recall that we aim to establish
Theorem

4.1
.
To that end, we prove that
\mathbin{\mathcal{S}}
(
Definition

4.17
) is an MST bisimulation, by establishing two results:

•

4.6
covers the case in which the given process performs an action,
which is matched by an action of the decomposed process.
In terms of operational correspondence (see, e.g.,
[
11
]
), this establishes
completeness
of the decomposition.

•

4.7
covers the converse direction, in which the decomposed process performs an action, which is matched by the initial process.
This established the
soundness
of the decomposition.

For proving both operational completeness and soundness, we will need the following result.
Following Parrow
[
21
]
, we refer to prefixes
that do not correspond to prefixes of the original process, i.e. prefixes on propagators
c_{i}
,
as
non-essential prefixes
.
Then the relation
\mathbin{\mathcal{S}}
is closed under reductions that involve non-essential prefixes.

Lemma 4.3
.

Given an indexed process
P_{1}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}
,
the set
\mathcal{C}^{\tilde{W}}_{\tilde{x}}\big(P_{1}\big)
is closed under
\tau
-transitions on
non-essential prefixes. That is,
if
R_{1}\in\mathcal{C}^{\tilde{W}}_{\tilde{x}}\big(P_{1}\big)

and
R_{1}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\tau}}R_{2}
is inferred from the actions on non-essential prefixes,
then
R_{2}\in\mathcal{C}^{\tilde{W}}_{\tilde{x}}\big(P_{1}\big)
.

Proof.

By the induction on the structure of
P_{1}
.
See
Section

C.1
for more details.
∎

Operational Completeness.

We first consider transitions using the unrestricted and untyped LTS; in
4.6
we will consider transitions with the refined LTS.

Lemma 4.4
.

Assume
P_{1}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}
is a process such that

\Gamma_{1};\Lambda_{1};\Delta_{1}\vdash P_{1}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}\triangleright\diamond

with
\mathsf{balanced}(\Delta_{1})

and
P_{1}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}\mathbin{\mathcal{S}}Q_{1}
.

1.

Whenever

{P_{1}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}}{\mathrel{\xrightarrow{(\nu\,\widetilde{m}_{1})\,n!\langle V_{1}\rangle}}}{P_{2}}

, such that
\overline{n}\not\in\mathtt{fn}(P_{1}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\})
,
then there exist

Q_{2}
and
V_{2}
such that

{Q_{1}}{\mathrel{\xRightarrow{(\nu\,\widetilde{m}_{2})\,\breve{n}!\langle V_{2}\rangle}}}{Q_{2}}

and, for a fresh
t
,

{(\nu\,\widetilde{m}_{1})(P_{2}\parallel t\hookleftarrow_{\mathtt{H}}V_{1})}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}\mathbin{\mathcal{S}}{(\nu\,\widetilde{m}_{2})(Q_{2}\parallel t_{1}\hookleftarrow_{\mathtt{H}}V_{2})}

2.

Whenever
{P_{1}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}}{\mathrel{\xrightarrow{n?(V_{1})}}}{P_{2}}

, such that
\overline{n}\not\in\mathtt{fn}(P_{1}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\})
,
then there exist
Q_{2}
,
V_{2}
, and
\sigma
such that

{Q_{1}}{\mathrel{\xRightarrow{\breve{n}?(V_{2})}}}{Q_{2}}

where
V_{1}\sigma\boxtimes V_{2}

and

{P_{2}}{\mathbin{\mathcal{S}}}{Q_{2}}
,

3.

Whenever
{P_{1}}{\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\tau}}}{P_{2}}

then there exists
Q_{2}
such that

{Q_{1}}{\mathrel{\xRightarrow{\tau}}}{Q_{2}}

and

{P_{2}}{\mathbin{\mathcal{S}}}{Q_{2}}
.

Proof.

By transition induction. See
Section

C.2
for more details.
∎

The following statement builds upon the previous one to address the case of the
typed LTS (
Definition

4.5
):

Lemma 4.5
.

Assume
P_{1}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}
is a
process and
P_{1}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}\mathbin{\mathcal{S}}Q_{1}
.

1.

Whenever

\Gamma_{1};\Lambda_{1};\Delta_{1}\vdash P_{1}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{(\nu\,\widetilde{m_{1}})\,n!\langle V_{1}\rangle}}\Lambda^{\prime}_{1};\Delta^{\prime}_{1}\vdash P_{2}

then there exist

Q_{2}
,
V_{2}
,
\Delta_{2}^{\prime}
, and
\Lambda_{2}^{\prime}
such that

\Gamma_{2};\Lambda_{2};\Delta_{2}\vdash Q_{1}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{(\nu\,\widetilde{m_{2}})\,\breve{n}!\langle V_{2}\rangle}}\Lambda^{\prime}_{2};\Delta_{2}^{\prime}\vdash Q_{2}

and, for a fresh
t
,

{(\nu\,\widetilde{m_{1}})(P_{2}\parallel t\hookleftarrow_{\mathtt{H}}V_{1})}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}\mathbin{\mathcal{S}}{(\nu\,\widetilde{m_{2}})(Q_{2}\parallel t_{1}\hookleftarrow_{\mathtt{H}}V_{2})}

2.

Whenever

\Gamma_{1};\Lambda_{1};\Delta_{1}\vdash P_{1}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{n?(V_{1})}}\Lambda_{1}^{\prime};\Delta_{1}^{\prime}\vdash P_{2}

then there exist
Q_{2}
,
V_{2}
,
\sigma
,
\Lambda^{\prime}_{2}
, and
\Delta^{\prime}_{2}
such that

\Gamma_{2};\Lambda_{2};\Delta_{2}\vdash Q_{1}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{\breve{n}?(V_{2})}}\Lambda^{\prime}_{2},\Delta_{2}^{\prime}\vdash Q_{2}

where
V_{1}\sigma\boxtimes V_{2}

and

{P_{2}}{\mathbin{\mathcal{S}}}{Q_{2}}
,

3.

Whenever

\Gamma_{1};\Lambda_{1};\Delta_{1}\vdash P_{1}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\tau}}\Lambda_{1}^{\prime};\Delta_{1}^{\prime}\vdash P_{2}

then there exist
Q_{2}
,
\Lambda_{2}^{\prime}
, and
\Delta_{2}^{\prime}
such that

\Gamma_{2};\Lambda_{2};\Delta_{2}\vdash Q_{1}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{\tau}}\Lambda_{2}^{\prime};\Delta_{2}^{\prime}\vdash Q_{2}

and

{P_{2}}{\mathbin{\mathcal{S}}}{Q_{2}}
.

Proof.

The proof uses results of
4.4
.
We consider the first case, the other two being similar.

By the definition of the typed LTS we have:

\displaystyle\Gamma_{1};\Lambda_{1};\Delta_{1}\vdash P_{1}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}

(20)

\displaystyle(\Gamma_{1};\emptyset;\Delta_{1})\mathrel{\xrightarrow{(\nu\,\widetilde{m})\,n!\langle V\rangle}}(\Gamma_{1};\emptyset;\Delta_{2})

(21)

By (
21
) we further have

\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime};\Lambda^{\prime};\Delta^{\prime}\vdash V\triangleright U

\Delta^{\prime}\backslash(\cup_{j}\Delta_{j})\subseteq(\Delta,n:S)

\Gamma^{\prime};\emptyset;\Delta_{j}\vdash m_{j}\triangleright U_{j}

Summary.

Together,

4.6
and
4.7

imply that
\mathbin{\mathcal{S}}
is an MST-bisimilarity.
In summary, we have shown
Theorem

4.1
, i.e., that for any typed process
P
, we have that

\Gamma;\Lambda;\Delta\vdash P\ \approx^{\mathtt{M}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma})};{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Lambda})};{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta})}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P})}.

In this section we have defined a notion of MST bisimilarity, following the notion
\mathsf{HO}
bisimilarity for non-minimal processes.
Following the strategy of Parrow in the untyped setting, we defined a relation
\mathbin{\mathcal{S}}
containing all pairs
(P,{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P})})
, which we proved to be an MST bisimulation.

5
Optimizations of the Decomposition

In this section we discuss two optimizations that can be applied to the decomposition process.
These optimizations simplify the structure of the trios and the nature of the underlying communication discipline.

The first optimization replaces trios in the decomposition with
duos
(i.e., processes with two sequential prefixes).
The decomposition in
Section

3
follows Parrow’s approach in that it converts a process into a parallel composition of trios.
The use of trios seems to be necessary in (plain)
\pi
-calculus; in our first optimization we show that, by exploiting the higher-order nature of communications in
\mathsf{HO}
, the trios can be replaced by duos.

The second optimization replaces polyadic communications (sending and receiving several values at once) with monadic communications (sending and receiving only a single value per prefix).
In the decomposition, we use polyadic communications in order to propagate dependencies through sub-processes.
We show that the use of monadic communication prefixes is sufficient for that task.

From Trios to Duos.

In the first optimization we replace trios with
duos
, i.e., processes with two sequential prefixes.
This optimization is enabled by the higher-order nature of
\mathsf{HO}
.
In the translation we make of
thunk processes
, i.e., inactive processes that can be activated upon reception.
We write
\{\!\{P\}\!\}
to stand for the thunk process
\lambda x:\langle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}\!\rightarrow\!\diamond\rangle.\,P
, for a fresh
x\not\in\mathtt{fn}(P)
.
We write
\mathtt{run}\,{\{\!\{P\}\!\}}
to denote the application of a thunk to a (dummy) name of type
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}\!\rightarrow\!\diamond
.
This way, we have a reduction
\mathtt{run}\,{\{\!\{P\}\!\}}\longrightarrow P
.

The key idea behind replacing trios with duos is to transform a trio like

c_{k}?(\widetilde{x}).u!\langle V\rangle.c_{k+1}!\langle\widetilde{z}\rangle{}

into the composition of two duos, the second one being a “control” duo:

c_{k}?(\widetilde{x}).c_{0}!\big\langle\{\!\{u!\langle V\rangle.c_{k+1}!\langle\widetilde{z}\rangle\}\!\}\big\rangle\;|\;c_{0}?(y).{(\mathtt{run}\,{y})}

(24)

The first action (on
c_{k}
) is as before; the two remaining prefixes (on
u
and
c_{k+1}
) are encapsulated into a thunk.
This thunk is sent via an additional propagator (denoted
c_{0}
) to the control duo that activates it upon reception.
Because of this additional propagator, this transformation involves minor modifications in the definition of the degree function
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\lbag{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-}\rbag}
(cf.

Definition

3.6
).

In some cases, the breakdown function in
Section

3.2
already produces duos.
Breaking down input and output prefixes and parallel composition involves proper trios; following the scheme illustrated by (
24
), we can define a map
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\{\!|{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-}|\!\}}
to transform these trios into duos:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\{\!|{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c_{k}?(\widetilde{x}).u_{i}!\langle V\rangle.\overline{c_{k+1}}!\langle\widetilde{z}\rangle{}}|\!\}}

\displaystyle=c_{k}?(\widetilde{x}).\overline{c_{k+1}}!\big\langle\{\!\{u_{i}!\langle V\rangle.\overline{c_{k+2}}!\langle\widetilde{z}\rangle\}\!\}\big\rangle\;|\;c_{k+1}?(y).{(\mathtt{run}\,{y})}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\{\!|{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c_{k}?(\widetilde{x}).u_{i}?(y).\overline{c_{k+1}}!\langle\widetilde{x}^{\prime}\rangle{}}|\!\}}

\displaystyle=c_{k}?(\widetilde{x}).\overline{c_{k+1}}!\big\langle\{\!\{u_{i}?(y).\overline{c_{k+2}}!\langle\widetilde{x}^{\prime}\rangle\}\!\}\big\rangle\;|\;c_{k+1}?(y).{(\mathtt{run}\,{y})}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\{\!|{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c_{k}?(\widetilde{x}).\overline{c_{k+1}}!\langle\widetilde{y}\rangle.\overline{c_{k+l+1}}!\langle\widetilde{z}\rangle}|\!\}}

\displaystyle=c_{k}?(\widetilde{x}).\overline{c_{k+1}}!\big\langle\{\!\{\overline{c_{k+2}}!\langle\widetilde{y}\rangle.\overline{c_{k+l+2}}!\langle\widetilde{z}\rangle\}\!\}\big\rangle\;|\;c_{k+1}?(y).{(\mathtt{run}\,{y})}

In breaking down prefixes involving tail-recursive names
(
Table

1
)
we encounter trios of the following form:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}u_{i}?(y).Q}\big)}=c_{k}?(\widetilde{x}).c^{u}!\big\langle N_{y}\big\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k+1}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{w}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}\big)}\quad\text{where}\quad N_{y}=\lambda\widetilde{z}.\,z_{{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S}\rangle}}?(y).\big(\overline{c_{k+1}}!\langle\widetilde{w}\rangle\;|\;c^{u}?(x).x\,{\widetilde{z}}\big))

Here we can see that the top-level process is a duo and that only

N_{y}
packs a proper trio. By applying the same idea we can translate
this trio into the following composition of duos:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\{\!|{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z_{{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S}\rangle}}?(y).\big(\overline{c_{k+1}}!\langle\widetilde{w}\rangle\;|\;c^{u}?(x).x\,{\widetilde{z}}\big)}|\!\}}=z_{{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S}\rangle}}?(y).\overline{c_{k+1}}!\langle\{\!\{\overline{c_{k+2}}!\langle\widetilde{w}\rangle\;|\;c^{u}?(x).x\,{\widetilde{z}}\}\!\}\rangle\;|\;c_{k+1}?(y).{\mathtt{run}\,{y}}

This is the idea behind the breakdown of a process starting with an input prefix; the breakdown of a process with an output prefix follows the same lines.

From Polyadic to Monadic Communication.

Source process
P_{1}
:
P_{1}
P_{2}
P_{3}
\mathbf{0}
u:?(\mathsf{str})
u:?(\mathsf{int})
u:!\langle\mathsf{{{bool}}}\rangle
Monadic decomposition
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathsf{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{1}})}
:
Q_{1}
Q^{\prime}_{1}
u_{1}:?(\mathsf{str})
\parallel
Q_{2}
Q^{\prime}_{2}
u_{2}:?(\mathsf{int})
\parallel
Q_{3}
Q^{\prime}_{3}
u_{3}:!\langle\mathsf{{{bool}}}\rangle
\parallel
Q_{4}
x:\mathsf{str}
y:\mathsf{int}

Figure 8
:
Our monadic decomposition function

{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathsf{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-})}
, illustrated.
As in
Figure

4
,
nodes
represent process states, ‘
\parallel
’ represents
parallel composition of processes, black arrows stand for actions, and red arrows
indicate synchronizations that preserve the sequentiality of the source process;
also, blue arrows indicates synchronizations that propagate (bound) values.

Our second optimization replaces polyadic communications, used for the propagators, with monadic communications.
Recall that propagators in
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-}\big)}
serve two purposes: they (i) encode sequentiality by properly activating trios and (ii) propagate bound values.
By separating propagators along those two roles, we can we can dispense with polyadic communication in the breakdown function.

We define
monadic breakdown
,
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathsf{B}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-}\big)}
, and
monadic decomposition
,
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathsf{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-})}
, which use two
kinds
of propagators: (i) propagators for only activating trios of form
c_{k}
(where
k>0
is an index) and (ii) for propagating bound values of form
c_{x}
(where
x
is some variable).
We depict the mechanism of the monadic breakdown in
Figure

8
.
The main idea is to establish a direct link between trio that binds the variable
x
and trios that make use of
x
on propagator channel
c_{x}
.
Thus, propagators on
c_{k}
only serve to activate next trios: they do so by
receiving
an abstraction that contains the next trio.

Formally, we define a
monadic decomposition
,
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathsf{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P})}
, that simplifies
Definition

3.9
as follows:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathsf{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P})}=(\nu\,\widetilde{c})\,\big(\overline{c_{k}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathsf{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\sigma}\big)}\big)

where
k>0
,
\widetilde{c}=(c_{k},\dots,c_{k+{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\lbag{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\rbag}-1})
,
and the initializing substitution
\sigma=\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathsf{index}(\widetilde{u})$}\!/\mbox{\small$\widetilde{u}$}\}
is the same as in
Definition

3.9
.

The monadic break down function
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathsf{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-}\big)}
, given in
Figure

9
, simplifies the one in
Table

1
by using only one parameter, namely
k
.
In
Figure

9
we use
\sigma
to denote the subsequent substitution
\mathsf{next}(u_{i})
, the same as in
Table

1
,
and use
\widetilde{m}
to denote the breakdown
(u_{i},\ldots,u_{i+|{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C})}|-1})
of the name
u_{i}
.

\displaystyle V_{x}

\displaystyle=\lambda y.\,y?(z).c_{x}!\langle x\rangle.(\nu\,s)\,(z\,{s}\;|\;\overline{s}!\langle z\rangle)

\displaystyle W^{\leadsto}_{x}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\overline{c_{x}}!\big\langle x\big\rangle&\text{if}\ \leadsto=\multimap\\
(\nu\,s)\,(V_{x}\,{s}\;|\;\overline{s}!\langle V_{x}\rangle)&\text{if}\ \leadsto=\rightarrow\end{cases}\vskip 5.69054pt

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathsf{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}u_{i}?(x:{C}\leadsto\diamond).Q}\big)}

\displaystyle=(\nu\,c_{x})\,\big(c_{k}?().u_{i}?(x).(\overline{c_{k+1}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;W^{\leadsto}_{x})\big)\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathsf{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k+1}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q\sigma}\big)}\big)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathsf{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}u_{i}!\langle x\rangle.Q}\big)}

\displaystyle=c_{k}?().c_{x}?(x).u_{i}!\langle x\rangle.\overline{c_{k+1}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathsf{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k+1}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q\sigma}\big)}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathsf{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}u_{i}!\langle V\rangle.Q}\big)}

\displaystyle=c_{k}?().u_{i}!\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathsf{V}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V\sigma}\big)}\rangle.\overline{c_{k+1}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathsf{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k+1}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q\sigma}\big)}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathsf{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x\,{u_{i}}}\big)}

\displaystyle=c_{k}?().c_{x}?(x).x\,{\widetilde{m}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathsf{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V\,{u_{i}}}\big)}

\displaystyle=c_{k}?().{{{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathsf{V}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V}\big)}\,{\widetilde{m}}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathsf{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\nu\,s)\,{P^{\prime}}}\big)}

\displaystyle=(\nu\,\widetilde{s})\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathsf{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}\sigma}\big)}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathsf{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q\;|\;R}\big)}

\displaystyle=c_{k}?().\overline{c_{k+1}}!\langle\rangle.\overline{c_{k+{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\lbag{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}\rbag}+1}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathsf{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k+1}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}\big)}\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathsf{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k+{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\lbag{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}\rbag}+1}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}R}\big)}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathsf{V}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}y}\big)}

\displaystyle=y

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathsf{V}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lambda y:{C}^{\leadsto}.\,P}\big)}

\displaystyle=\lambda(y_{1},\ldots,y_{|{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C})}|}):{{{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C})}}^{\leadsto}}.\,(\nu\,c_{1},\ldots,c_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\lbag{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\rbag}})\,\big(\overline{c_{1}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathsf{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$y_{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\}}\big)}\big)

Figure 9
:
Monadic breakdown of processes and values

The breakdown function
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathsf{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-}\big)}
uses
propagators
c_{k}
(
k>0
) for encoding sequentiality and
dedicated propagators
c_{x}
for each variable
x
.
As propagators
c_{k}
now only serve to encode sequentiality,
only dummy values are being communicated along these channels (see
Remark

3.2
).

Let us describe the breakdown of a process with an input prefix, as it illustrates the key points common to all the other cases.
The breakdown
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathsf{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}u_{i}?(x).Q}\big)}
consists of a trio in parallel
with the breakdown of the continuation
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathsf{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k+1}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q\sigma}\big)}

with name
c_{x}
restricted. The trio is first activated
on
c_{k}
. This is followed by
the prefix that mimics original input action on indexed name
u_{i}
.
Upon receiving value
x
, two things will happen in parallel.
First, the next trio will be activated on name
c_{k+1}
. Second,
the value
x
received on
u_{i}
is propagated further by the dedicated process
W^{\leadsto}_{x}
.

The specific mechanism of propagation depends on whether a received value is linear (
\leadsto=\multimap
) or shared (
\leadsto=\rightarrow
).
In the former case, we simply propagate a value along the
linear
name
c_{x}
once.
In the later case, we cannot propagate the value only once, because a shared variable can be used in multiple trios.
Thus,
W^{\rightarrow}_{x}
implements a recursive mechanism that repeatedly sends a value on the
shared
name
c_{x}
.
The recursion is encoded in the same way as in
Example

3.4
: action
c_{x}!\langle x\rangle
is enclosed in value
V
that gets appropriately duplicated upon a synchronization.

The breakdown function for values,
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathsf{V}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-}\big)}
, is
accordingly changed to invoke
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathsf{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-}\big)}

for breaking down a function body.

For simplicity, we defined the decomposition of the output process using a subprocess with four prefixes.
Alternatively, we could have used a decomposition that relies on two trios, by introducing abstraction passing as in the previous section.

Let us illustrate the monadic breakdown by the means of an example:

Example 5.1

(Monadic Decomposition)
.

P=(\nu\,u)\,(Q\;|\;R)
as
in

\displaystyle Q

\displaystyle=u?(x).\overbrace{u?(y).(\nu\,s)\,\big(x\,{\overline{s}}\;|\;s!\langle y\rangle\big)}^{Q^{\prime}}

\displaystyle R

\displaystyle=\overline{u}!\langle V\rangle.\overline{u}!\langle\textsf{true}\rangle.\mathbf{0}

\displaystyle V

\displaystyle=\lambda z.\,z?(w).\mathbf{0}

Similarly,

\displaystyle D^{2}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow(\nu\,c_{4},\ldots,c_{7},c_{10})\,(\nu\,c_{x})\,\big(c_{x}!\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathsf{V}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V}\big)}\rangle\;|\;(\nu\,c_{y})\,\big(\overline{c_{4}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;W_{y}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\textsf{true}$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\}\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathsf{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}4}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\nu\,s)\,\big(x\,{\overline{s}}\;|\;s!\langle y\rangle\big)}\big)}\big)\big)

\displaystyle\qquad\;|\;\overline{c_{10}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{10}?().\mathbf{0}

The subprocess
W_{y}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\textsf{true}$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\}
is dedicated to providing the value
true
on a shared name

.
Specifically, it reduces as follow
Its reductions are as follows:

\displaystyle W_{y}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\textsf{true}$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\}\longrightarrow^{2}c_{y}!\langle\textsf{true}\rangle.W_{y}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\textsf{true}$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\}

In this example, the shared value received on

in the general case,

With this information, we have the following reductions of the decomposed process:

\displaystyle D^{3}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow^{2}(\nu\,c_{7})\,(\nu\,c_{x})\,\big(c_{x}!\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathsf{V}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V}\big)}\rangle\;|\;(\nu\,c_{y})\,\big(c_{y}!\langle\textsf{true}\rangle.W_{y}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\textsf{true}$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\}\;|\;(\nu\,s_{1})\,c_{x}?(x).x\,{\overline{s}_{1}}

\displaystyle\qquad\;|\;c_{y}?(y).s_{1}!\langle y\rangle.\overline{c_{7}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{7}?().\mathbf{0}\big)\big)=D^{4}

to name

. Similarly, a value for

These values are retrieved in two reductions steps as follows:

\displaystyle D^{4}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow^{2}(\nu\,c_{7})\,(\nu\,s_{1})\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathsf{V}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V}\big)}\,{\overline{s}_{1}}\;|\;s_{1}!\langle\textsf{true}\rangle.\overline{c_{7}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{7}?().\mathbf{0}\;|\;(\nu\,c_{y})\,W_{y}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\textsf{true}$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\}=D^{5}

We remark that

reduces to

equivalent to the inactive process.

Next, the application of the value is followed by the
synchronization on propagator
