\displaystyle D^{5}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow(\nu\,c_{7})\,(\nu\,s_{1})\,(\nu\,c^{V}_{1},c^{V}_{2})\,c^{V}_{1}!\langle\rangle\;|\;(\nu\,c_{w})\,c{{}^{V}_{1}}?().\overline{s}_{1}?(w).(\overline{c{{}^{V}_{2}}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;W_{w})\;|\;c{{}^{V}_{2}}?()\mathbf{0}

\displaystyle\qquad\;|\;s_{1}!\langle\textsf{true}\rangle.\overline{c_{7}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{7}?().\mathbf{0}\;|\;(\nu\,c_{y})\,W_{y}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\textsf{true}$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow(\nu\,c_{7})\,(\nu\,s_{1})\,(\nu\,c^{V}_{2})\,(\nu\,c_{w})\,\overline{s}_{1}?(w).(\overline{c{{}^{V}_{2}}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;W_{w})\;|\;c{{}^{V}_{2}}?()\mathbf{0}

\displaystyle\qquad\;|\;s_{1}!\langle\textsf{true}\rangle.\overline{c_{7}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{7}?().\mathbf{0}\;|\;(\nu\,c_{y})\,W_{y}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\textsf{true}$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\}=D^{6}

Here, we can see that
D^{6}
can simulate
P^{\prime}
, and its internal communication on the channel
s
.

6
Extension with Labeled Choice

In this section we discuss how to extend our approach to include sessions with
selection
and
branching
—the constructs used to express deterministic choices.
Forgoing formal proofs, we illustrate by examples how to harness the expressive power of abstraction-passing to decompose these constructs at the process level.
First, we demonstrate how to break down selection and branching constructs in absence of recursion in
Section

6.1
.
Then, in
Section

6.2
we explore the interplay of recursion and labeled choice, as it requires special attention.
Finally, in
Section

6.3
we sketch how the operational correspondence proof can be adapted to account for branching and selection.

Let us briefly recall the labeled choice constructs in
\mathsf{HO}
, following
[
18
]
.
On the level of processes, selection and branching are modeled using labeled choice:

\displaystyle P,Q

\displaystyle\;::=\;\ldots\mathrel{\big|}u\triangleleft l.P\mathrel{\big|}u\triangleright\{l_{i}:P_{i}\}_{i\in I}

The process
u\triangleleft l.P
selects the label
l
on channel
u
and then proceeds as
P
.
The process
u\triangleright\{l_{i}:P_{i}\}_{i\in I}
receives a label on the channel
u
and proceeds with the continuation branch
P_{i}
based on the received label.
Selection and branching constructs can synchronize with each other, as represented in the operational semantics by the following reduction rule:

\displaystyle u\triangleleft l_{j}.Q\;|\;\overline{u}\triangleright\{l_{i}:P_{i}\}_{i\in I}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow Q\;|\;P_{j}\qquad\penalty\ \penalty\ (j\in I)\penalty\ \penalty\ {\scriptsize{[\textrm{\small Sel}]}}

At the level of types, selection and branching are represented with the following types:

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle\;::=\;\ldots\mathrel{\big|}\oplus\{l_{i}:S_{i}\}_{i\in I}\mathrel{\big|}\&\{l_{i}:S_{i}\}_{i\in I}

The
selection type

\oplus\{l_{i}:S_{i}\}_{i\in I}
and the
branching type

\&\{l_{i}:S_{i}\}_{i\in I}
are used to type, respectively,
the selection and branching process constructs.
Note the implicit sequencing in the sessions involving selection and branching: the exchange of a label
l_{i}
precedes the execution of one of the stipulated protocol
S_{i}
.
The typing rules for type-checking branching and selection processes are given in
Figure

10
.

\frac{\Gamma;\Lambda;\Delta,u:S_{j}\vdash P\triangleright\diamond\quad j\in I}{\Gamma;\Lambda;\Delta,u:\oplus\{l_{i}:S_{i}\}_{i\in I}\vdash u\triangleleft l_{j}.P\triangleright\diamond}\quad\textsc{(Sel)}\qquad\frac{\forall i\in I\quad\Gamma;\Lambda;\Delta,u:S_{i}\vdash P_{i}\triangleright\diamond}{\Gamma;\Lambda;\Delta,u:\&\{l_{i}:S_{i}\}_{i\in I}\vdash u\triangleright\{l_{i}:P_{i}\}_{i\in I}\triangleright\diamond}\quad\textsc{(Bra)}

Figure 10
:
Typing rules for selection and branching.

Given these process constructs and types, what are the minimal versions of the session types with labeled choice?
We do not consider branching and selection as atomic actions as their purpose is to make a choice of a stipulated protocol.
In other words, it is not meaningful to type a channel with branching type in which all protocols are
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}
.
Thus, we extend the syntax of minimal session types (
Definition

3.1
) with
branching and selection constructs, as follows:

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle\;::=\;\ldots\mathrel{\big|}\oplus\{l_{i}:M_{i}\}_{i\in I}\mathrel{\big|}\&\{l_{i}:M_{i}\}_{i\in I}

That is, MSTs also include branching and selection
types with MSTs nested in branches.

Next we explain our strategy for extending the breakdown function to
account for selection and branching.

6.1
Breaking Down Selection and Branching

Notice that in a branching process
u\triangleright\{l_{i}:P_{i}\}_{i\in I}
each subprocess

P_{i}
can have a different session with a different degree.
Abstraction-passing allows to uniformly handle these kinds of processes.
We extend the breakdown function in
Definition

3.3
to selection and branching as follows:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\&\{l_{i}:S_{i}\}_{i\in I}})}

\displaystyle=\&\{l_{i}:!\langle{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S_{i}})}\!\multimap\!\diamond\rangle\}_{i\in I}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\oplus\{l_{i}:S_{i}\}_{i\in I}})}

\displaystyle=\oplus\{l_{i}:?({\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\overline{S_{i}}})}\!\multimap\!\diamond)\}_{i\in I}

This decomposition follows the intuition that branching and selection correspond to the input and output of labels, respectively.
For example, in the case of branching, once a particular branch
l_{i}
has been selected, we would like to input names on which to provide sessions from the branch
{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S_{i}})}
.
In our higher-order setting, we do not input or output names directly.
Instead, we send out an abstraction of the continuation process, which binds those names.
It is then the job of the (complementary) selecting process to activate that abstraction with the names we want to select.

To make this more concrete, let us consider the decomposition of branching and selection at the level of processes through the following extended example.

Example 6.1
.

Consider a mathematical server
Q
that offers clients two operations: addition and negation of integers.
The server uses name
u
to implement the following session type:

\displaystyle S=\&\{\textsf{add}:\underbrace{?(\textsf{int}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}?(\textsf{int}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}!\langle\textsf{int}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}}_{S_{\textsf{add}}}\penalty\ ,\ \textsf{neg}:\underbrace{?(\textsf{int}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}!\langle\textsf{int}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}}_{S_{\textsf{neg}}}\}

The branches have session types with different lengths: one receives two integers and sends
over their sum, the other has a single input of an integer followed by an
output of its negation.
Let us consider a possible implementation for the server
Q
and for a client
R
that selects the first branch to add integers 16 and 26:

\displaystyle Q

\displaystyle\triangleq u\triangleright\{\textsf{add}:{Q_{\textsf{add}}},\ \textsf{neg}:{Q_{\textsf{neg}}}\}

\displaystyle R\triangleq\overline{u}\triangleleft\textsf{add}.\overline{u}!\langle\mathbf{16}\rangle.\overline{u}!\langle\mathbf{26}\rangle.\overline{u}?(r)

\displaystyle Q_{\textsf{add}}

\displaystyle\triangleq u?(a).u?(b).u!\langle a+b\rangle

\displaystyle Q_{\textsf{neg}}

\displaystyle\triangleq u?(a).u!\langle-a\rangle

The composed process
P\triangleq(\nu\,u)\,(Q\;|\;R)
can reduce as follows:

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle\penalty\ \longrightarrow\penalty\ (\nu\,u)\,(u?(a).u?(b).u!\langle a+b\rangle\;|\;\overline{u}!\langle\mathbf{16}\rangle.\overline{u}!\langle\mathbf{26}\rangle.\overline{u}?(r))

\displaystyle\penalty\ \longrightarrow^{2}\penalty\ (\nu\,u)\,(u!\langle\mathbf{16+26}\rangle\;|\;\overline{u}?(r))=P^{\prime}

Let us discuss the decomposition of
P
. First, the decomposition of
S
is the minimal session type
M
, defined as follows:

\displaystyle M={\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S})}=\&\{

\displaystyle\textsf{add}:!\langle\big(?(\textsf{int}),?(\textsf{int}),!\langle\textsf{int}\rangle\big)\!\multimap\!\diamond\rangle,

\displaystyle\textsf{neg}:!\langle\big(?(\textsf{int}),!\langle\textsf{int}\rangle\big)\!\multimap\!\diamond\rangle\}

Following
Definition

3.9
, we decompose
P
as follows:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P})}=(\nu\,c_{1}\ldots c_{7})\,\big(\overline{c_{1}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;(\nu\,u_{1})\,(c_{1}?().\overline{c_{2}}!\langle\rangle.\overline{c_{3}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q\sigma_{2}}\big)}\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}3}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}R\sigma_{2}}\big)})\big)

where
\sigma_{2}=\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$u_{1}\overline{u_{1}}$}\!/\mbox{\small$u\overline{u}$}\}
.
The breakdown of the server process
Q
, which implements the branching, is as follows:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q\sigma_{2}}\big)}=c_{2}?().u_{1}\triangleright\{\textsf{add}:

\displaystyle\penalty\ u_{1}!\big\langle\underbrace{\lambda(y_{1},y_{2},y_{3}).\,(\nu\,c^{V}_{1}\ldots c^{V}_{4})\,\overline{c{{}^{V}_{1}}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{\textsf{add}}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$y_{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$u$}\}}\big)}\sigma_{V}}_{V}\big\rangle,

\displaystyle\textsf{neg}:

\displaystyle\penalty\ u_{1}!\big\langle\underbrace{\lambda(y_{1},y_{2}).\,(\nu\,c^{W}_{1}\ldots c^{W}_{3})\,\overline{c{{}^{W}_{1}}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{\textsf{neg}}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$y_{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$u$}\}}\big)}\sigma_{W}}_{W}\big\rangle\}

where:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{\textsf{add}}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$y_{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$u$}\}}\big)}

\displaystyle=c_{1}?().y_{1}?(a).\overline{c_{2}}!\langle a\rangle\;|\;c_{2}?(a).y_{2}?(b).\overline{c_{3}}!\langle a,b\rangle\;|\;c_{3}?(a,b).y_{3}!\langle a+b\rangle.\overline{c_{4}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{4}?()

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{\textsf{neg}}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$y_{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$u$}\}}\big)}

\displaystyle=c_{1}?().y_{1}?(a).\overline{c_{2}}!\langle a\rangle\;|\;c_{2}?(a).y_{2}!\langle-a\rangle.\overline{c_{3}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{3}?()

with
\sigma_{V}=\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$c^{V}_{1},\ldots,c^{V}_{4}$}\!/\mbox{\small$c_{1},\ldots,c_{4}$}\}

and
\sigma_{W}=\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$c^{W}_{1},c^{W}_{2},c^{W}_{3}$}\!/\mbox{\small$c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}$}\}
.
In process
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q\sigma_{2}}\big)}
, name
u_{1}
implements the minimal session type
M
.
Following the common trio structure, the first prefix awaits activation on

c_{2}
. The next prefix mimics the
branching action of
Q
on
u_{1}
.
Then, each branch consists of the output of an abstraction along
u_{1}
.
This output does not have a counterpart in
Q
; it is meant to synchronize with process
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}3}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}R\sigma_{2}}\big)}
, the breakdown of the corresponding selection process (see below).

The abstractions sent along
u_{1}
encapsulate the breakdown of subprocesses in
the two branches (
Q_{\textsf{add}}
and
Q_{\textsf{neg}}
). An abstraction
in the branch has the same structure as the breakdown of a value

\lambda y:C^{\rightarrow}.\,P
in
Table

1
: it is a composition of a
control trio and the breakdown of a subprocess; the generated propagators are
restricted.
In the first branch the server needs three actions to perform the
session, and in the second branch the server needs to perform two actions.
Because of that the first abstraction binds three names
y_{1},y_{2},y_{3}
, and the
second abstraction binds two names
y_{1},y_{2}
.

In the bodies of the abstractions we break down
Q_{\textsf{add}}
and
Q_{\textsf{neg}}
, but not before adjusting the names on which the broken down processes provide the sessions.
For this, we substitute
u
with
y_{1}
in both processes, ensuring that the broken down names are bound by the abstractions.
By binding decomposed names in abstractions we account for different
session types of the original name in branches, while preserving typability:
this way the decomposition of different branches can use (i) the same names
but typed with different minimal types and (ii) a different number of names, as it is the
case in this example.

The decomposition of the client process
R
, which implements the selection, is as follows:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}3}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}R\sigma_{2}}\big)}=\penalty\

\displaystyle(\nu\,u_{2},u_{3},u_{4})\,c_{3}?().{\overline{u_{1}}\triangleleft\textsf{add}.{\overline{u_{1}}?(z).\overline{c_{4}}!\langle\rangle.z\,{(u_{2},u_{3},u_{4})}}}\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}4}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\overline{u}_{2}!\langle\mathbf{16}\rangle.\overline{u}_{2}!\langle\mathbf{26}\rangle.\overline{u}_{2}?(r)}\big)}

where:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}4}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\overline{u}_{2}!\langle\mathbf{16}\rangle.\overline{u}_{2}!\langle\mathbf{26}\rangle.\overline{u}_{2}?(r)}\big)}=c_{4}?().\overline{u}_{2}!\langle\mathbf{16}\rangle.\overline{c_{5}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{5}?().\overline{u}_{3}!\langle\mathbf{26}\rangle.\overline{c_{6}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{6}?().\overline{u}_{4}?(r).\overline{c_{7}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{7}?()

After receiving the context on
c_{3}
(empty in this case), the selection action on
u_{1}
is mimicked; then, an abstraction (an encapsulation of the selected branch) is received and applied to
(u_{2},u_{3},u_{4})
, which are locally bound.
The intention is to use these names to connect the received abstraction and the continuation of a selection process: the subprocess encapsulated within the abstraction will use

(u_{2},u_{3},u_{4})
, while the dual names
(\overline{u}_{2},\overline{u}_{3},\overline{u}_{4})
are present in the breakdown of the continuation.

For simplicity, we defined
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}3}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}R\sigma_{2}}\big)}
using a subprocess with four prefixes.
Alternatively, we could have used a decomposition that relies on two trios, by introducing abstraction passing as in
Section

5
.

We will now examine the reductions of the decomposed process
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P})}
.
First,
c_{1}
,
c_{2}
, and
c_{3}
will synchronize.
We have
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P})}\longrightarrow^{4}D_{1}
, where

\displaystyle D_{1}

\displaystyle=(\nu\,c_{4}\ldots c_{7})\,(\nu\,u_{1})\,\big(u_{1}\triangleright\{\textsf{add}:\penalty\ u_{1}!\big\langle V\big\rangle,\penalty\ \textsf{neg}:\penalty\ u_{1}!\big\langle W\big\rangle\}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\;|\;(\nu\,u_{2},u_{3},u_{4})\,{(\lambda(y_{1},y_{2},y_{3}).\,\overline{u}_{1}\triangleleft\textsf{add}.{\overline{u}_{1}?(z).\overline{c_{4}}!\langle\rangle.z\,{(y_{1},y_{2},y_{3})}})\,{{(u_{2},u_{3},u_{4})}}}\;|\;

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}4}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\overline{u}_{2}!\langle\mathbf{16}\rangle.\overline{u}_{2}!\langle\mathbf{26}\rangle.\overline{u}_{2}?(r)}\big)}\big)

In
D_{1}
,
(u_{2},u_{3},u_{4})
will be applied to the abstraction; after that,
the process chooses the label
add
on
u_{1}
.
Process
D_{1}
will reduce further as

D_{1}\longrightarrow^{2}D_{2}\longrightarrow^{2}D_{3}
, where:

\displaystyle D_{2}

\displaystyle=(\nu\,c_{4}\ldots c_{7})\,(\nu\,u_{1})\,\big(\penalty\ u_{1}!\big\langle V\big\rangle\;|\;(\nu\,u_{2},u_{3},u_{4})\,({{{{\overline{u}_{1}?(z).\overline{c_{4}}!\langle\rangle.z\,{(u_{2},u_{3},u_{4})}}}}}\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}4}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\overline{u}!\langle\mathbf{26}\rangle.\overline{u}?(r)}\big)})\big)

\displaystyle D_{3}

\displaystyle=(\nu\,c_{4}\ldots c_{7})\,(\nu\,u_{1},u_{2},u_{3},u_{4})\,\big({{{{\overline{c_{4}}!\langle\rangle.V\,{(u_{2},u_{3},u_{4})}}}}}\;|\;

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad c_{4}?().\overline{u}_{2}!\langle\mathbf{16}\rangle.\overline{c_{5}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{5}?().\overline{u}_{3}!\langle\mathbf{26}\rangle.\overline{c_{6}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{6}?().\overline{u}_{4}?(r).\overline{c_{7}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{7}?()\big)

Then
D_{3}
reduces as
D_{3}\longrightarrow D_{4}\longrightarrow D_{5}
, where:

\displaystyle D_{4}

\displaystyle=(\nu\,c_{5}\ldots c_{7})\,(\nu\,u_{2},u_{3},u_{4})\,\big((\nu\,c^{V}_{1}\ldots c^{V}_{4})\,(\overline{c{{}^{V}_{1}}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c{{}^{V}_{1}}?().u_{2}?(a).\overline{c{{}^{V}_{2}}}!\langle a\rangle\;|\;

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad c{{}^{V}_{2}}?(a).u_{3}?(b).\overline{c{{}^{V}_{3}}}!\langle a,b\rangle\;|\;

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad c{{}^{V}_{3}}?(a,b).u_{4}!\langle a+b\rangle.\overline{c{{}^{V}_{4}}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c{{}^{V}_{4}}?())\;|\;

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\overline{u}_{2}!\langle\mathbf{16}\rangle.\overline{c_{5}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{5}?().\overline{u}_{3}!\langle\mathbf{26}\rangle.\overline{c_{6}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{6}?().\overline{u}_{4}?(r).\overline{c_{7}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{7}?()\big)

\displaystyle D_{5}

\displaystyle=(\nu\,c_{5}\ldots c_{7})\,(\nu\,u_{2},u_{3},u_{4})\,\big((\nu\,c^{V}_{2}\ldots c^{V}_{4})\,(u_{2}?(a).\overline{c{{}^{V}_{2}}}!\langle a\rangle\;|\;c{{}^{V}_{2}}?(a).u_{3}?(b).\overline{c{{}^{V}_{3}}}!\langle a,b\rangle\;|\;

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad c{{}^{V}_{3}}?(a,b).u_{4}!\langle a+b\rangle.\overline{c{{}^{V}_{4}}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c{{}^{V}_{4}}?())\;|\;

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\overline{u}_{2}!\langle\mathbf{16}\rangle.\overline{c_{5}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{5}?().\overline{u}_{3}!\langle\mathbf{26}\rangle.\overline{c_{6}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{6}?().\overline{u}_{4}?(r).\overline{c_{7}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{7}?()\big)

Now, process
D_{5}
can mimic the original
transmission of the integer
16
on channel
u_{2}
as follows:

\displaystyle D_{5}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow(\nu\,c_{5}\ldots c_{7})\,(\nu\,u_{2},u_{3},u_{4})\,\big((\nu\,c^{V}_{2}\ldots c^{V}_{4})\,(\overline{c{{}^{V}_{2}}}!\langle\mathbf{16}\rangle\;|\;c{{}^{V}_{2}}?(a).u_{3}?(b).\overline{c{{}^{V}_{3}}}!\langle a,b\rangle\;|\;

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad c{{}^{V}_{3}}?(a,b).u_{4}!\langle a+b\rangle.\overline{c{{}^{V}_{4}}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c{{}^{V}_{4}}?())\;|\;

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\overline{c_{5}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{5}?().\overline{u}_{3}!\langle\mathbf{26}\rangle.\overline{c_{6}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{6}?().\overline{u}_{4}?(r).\overline{c_{7}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{7}?()\big)=D_{6}

Finally, process
D_{6}
reduces to
D_{7}
in three steps, as follows:

\displaystyle D_{6}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow^{3}(\nu\,c_{5}\ldots c_{7})\,(\nu\,u_{4})\,\big((\nu\,c^{V}_{4})\,(u_{4}!\langle\mathbf{16}+\mathbf{26}\rangle.\overline{c{{}^{V}_{4}}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c{{}^{V}_{4}}?())\;|\;\overline{u}_{4}?(r).\overline{c_{7}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{7}?()\big)=D_{7}

Clearly, process
D_{7}
correctly simulates the synchronizations of the process
P^{\prime}
.

\lhd

6.2
The Interplay of Selection/Branching and Recursion

Now, we discuss by example how recursive session types involving branching/selection
are broken down. For simplicity, we consider recursive types without nested recursion and in which the recursive step is followed immediately by branching or selection, without any intermediate actions, i.e. types of the following form:

\mu\mathsf{t}.\&\{l_{i}:S_{i}\}_{i\in I}\qquad\mu\mathsf{t}.\oplus\{l_{i}:S_{i}\}_{i\in I}

where none of
S_{i}
contain branching/selection or recursion.

In this case, the decomposition of branching recursive types should be defined differently than for tail-recursive types: a type such as
\mu\mathsf{t}.\&\{l_{i}:S_{i}\}_{i\in I}
does not
necessarily describe a channel with an infinite behavior, because some of the branches
S_{i}
can result in termination.
In such case, decomposing all actions in the type
\&\{l_{i}:S_{i}\}_{i\in I}
as their own recursive types using the
{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{R}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-})}
function would be incorrect.

Instead, we decompose the body of the recursive type with
{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-})}
itself:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mu\mathsf{t}.\&\{l_{i}:S_{i}\}_{i\in I}})}

\displaystyle=\mu\mathsf{t}.\&\{l_{i}:!\langle{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S_{i}})}\!\multimap\!\diamond\rangle\}_{i\in I}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mu\mathsf{t}.\oplus\{l_{i}:S_{i}\}_{i\in I}})}

\displaystyle=\mu\mathsf{t}.\oplus\{l_{i}:?({\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\overline{S_{i}}})}\!\multimap\!\diamond)\}_{i\in I}

If some branch
S_{i}
contains the recursion variable
\mathsf{t}
, then it will appear in
{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S_{i}})}
, because
{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{t}})}=\mathsf{t}
.
That is, recursion variables will appear as part of the abstraction
{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\overline{S_{i}}})}\!\multimap\!\diamond
.
That means that the decomposition of a tail-recursive type form can produce a minimal
non
-tail-recursive types.

Now, we illustrate this decomposition on the level of processes.

Example 6.2
.

We consider a process
P
with a name
r
that is typed as follows:

S=\mu\mathsf{t}.\&\{l_{1}:?(\mathsf{str}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}!\langle\mathsf{int}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathsf{t},\ l_{2}:\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}\}.

For simplicity, we give
P
in
\mathsf{HO}\pi
(which includes
\mathsf{HO}
with recursion as sub-calculus):

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle=R\;|\;Q

\displaystyle R

\displaystyle=\mu X.r\triangleright\{l_{1}:r?(t).r!\langle\mathtt{len}(t)\rangle.X,\ l_{2}:\mathbf{0}\}

\displaystyle Q

\displaystyle=\overline{r}\triangleleft l_{1}.\overline{r}!\langle{{\text{``Hello''}}}\rangle.\overline{r}?(a_{1}).\overline{r}\triangleleft l_{1}.\overline{r}!\langle{\text{``World''}}\rangle.\overline{r}?(a_{2}).\overline{r}\triangleleft l_{2}.\mathbf{0}

That is,
P
contains a server
R
which either accepts a new request to calculate a length of a string, or to terminate.
Dually,
P
contains a client
Q
, which uses the server twice before terminating.

We can give an equivalent process in
\mathsf{HO}
by encoding the recursion (as done in
[
18
]
):

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.36,0.54,0.66}\llbracket}P{\color[rgb]{0.36,0.54,0.66}\rrbracket}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.36,0.54,0.66}\llbracket}R{\color[rgb]{0.36,0.54,0.66}\rrbracket}\;|\;Q

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.36,0.54,0.66}\llbracket}R{\color[rgb]{0.36,0.54,0.66}\rrbracket}

\displaystyle=(\nu\,s)\,(V\,{(r,s)}\;|\;\bar{s}!\langle V\rangle)

\displaystyle V

\displaystyle=\lambda(x_{r},x_{s}).\,x_{s}?(y).x_{r}\triangleright\{l_{1}:x_{r}?(t).x_{r}!\langle\mathtt{len}(t)\rangle.(\nu\,s)\,{(y\,{(x_{r},s)}\;|\;\overline{s}!\langle y\rangle)},\ l_{2}:\mathbf{0}\}

The decomposition of
S
, denoted
M^{*}
, is the following minimal session type:

\displaystyle M^{*}={\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S})}=\mu\mathsf{t}.\&\{l_{1}:!\langle(?(\mathsf{str}),\ !\langle\mathsf{int}\rangle,\ \mathsf{t})\!\multimap\!\diamond\rangle,\ l_{2}:\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}\}

As in the previous example (
Example

6.1
), the continuation of a selected branch will be packed in an abstraction and sent over.
This abstraction binds names on which the session actions should be performed.
In addition, if a branch contains a recursive call, then the last argument of the abstraction will be a name on which the next instance of the recursion will be mimicked.
We illustrate this mechanism by giving the decomposition of
{\color[rgb]{0.36,0.54,0.66}\llbracket}P{\color[rgb]{0.36,0.54,0.66}\rrbracket}
and inspecting its reductions.

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.36,0.54,0.66}\llbracket}P{\color[rgb]{0.36,0.54,0.66}\rrbracket}})}

\displaystyle=(\nu\,c_{1},\ldots,c_{12})\,\overline{c_{1}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{1}?().\overline{c_{2}}!\langle\rangle.\overline{c_{5}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.36,0.54,0.66}\llbracket}R{\color[rgb]{0.36,0.54,0.66}\rrbracket}}\big)}\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}5}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}\big)}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.36,0.54,0.66}\llbracket}R{\color[rgb]{0.36,0.54,0.66}\rrbracket}}\big)}

\displaystyle=(\nu\,s_{1})\,(c_{2}?().\overline{c_{3}}!\langle\rangle.\overline{c_{4}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{3}?().{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V}\big)}\,{(r_{1},s_{1})}\;|\;c_{4}?().\overline{s}_{1}!\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V}\big)}\rangle)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V}\big)}

\displaystyle=\lambda(x_{r_{1}},x_{s_{1}}).\,(\nu\,c^{V}_{1}c^{V}_{2})\,\overline{c{{}^{V}_{1}}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c{{}^{V}_{1}}?().x_{s_{1}}?(y).\overline{c{{}^{V}_{2}}}!\langle y\rangle\;|\;c{{}^{V}_{2}}?(y).x_{r_{1}}\triangleright\{l_{1}:x_{r_{1}}!\langle W\rangle,\ l_{2}:\mathbf{0}\}

\displaystyle W

\displaystyle=\lambda(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3}).\,(\nu\,c^{W}_{1}\ldots c^{W}_{5})\,\overline{c{{}^{W}_{1}}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c{{}^{W}_{1}}?().z_{1}?(t).\overline{c{{}^{W}_{2}}}!\langle t\rangle\;|\;c{{}^{W}_{2}}?(t).z_{2}!\langle\mathtt{len}(t)\rangle.\overline{c{{}^{W}_{3}}}!\langle\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad\;|\;(\nu\,s_{1})\,(c{{}^{W}_{3}}?().\overline{c{{}^{W}_{4}}}!\langle\rangle.\overline{c{{}^{W}_{5}}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c{{}^{W}_{4}}?().y\,{(z_{3},s_{1})}\;|\;c{{}^{W}_{5}}?().\overline{s}_{1}!\langle y\rangle)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}5}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}\big)}

\displaystyle=(\nu\,r_{2}:?(\mathsf{str}),\ r_{3}:!\langle\mathsf{int}\rangle,\ r_{4}:{M^{*}})\,

\displaystyle\qquad c_{5}?().\overline{r}_{1}\triangleleft l_{1}.\overline{c_{6}}!\langle\rangle.\overline{r}_{1}?(y).y\,{(r_{2},r_{3},r_{4})}\;|\;

\displaystyle\qquad c_{6}?().\overline{r}_{2}!\langle{\text{``Hello''}}\rangle.\overline{c_{7}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{7}?().\overline{r}_{3}?(t).\overline{c_{11}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;

\displaystyle\qquad\quad(\nu\,r_{5}:?(\mathsf{str}),\ r_{6}:!\langle\mathsf{int}\rangle,\ r_{7}:{M^{*}})\,

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\quad c_{8}?().\overline{r}_{4}\triangleleft l_{1}.\overline{c_{12}}!\langle\rangle.\overline{r}_{4}?(y).y\,{(r_{5},r_{6},r_{7})}\;|\;c_{9}?().\overline{r}_{5}!\langle\text{``World''}\rangle.\overline{c_{10}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\quad c_{10}?().\overline{r}_{6}?(t).\overline{c_{11}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{11}?().\overline{r}_{7}\triangleleft l_{2}.\overline{c_{12}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{12}?()

In the process
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}5}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}\big)}
, the restricted names
(r_{2},r_{3},r_{4})
are the decomposition of the name
r
for the branch
l_{1}
.
To calculate their types, we unfold
S
:

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle=\mu\mathsf{t}.\&\{l_{1}:?(\mathsf{str}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}!\langle\mathsf{int}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathsf{t},\ l_{2}:\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}\}\equiv\&\{l_{1}:?(\mathsf{str}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}!\langle\mathsf{int}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathsf{t},\ l_{2}:\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}\}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$S$}\!/\mbox{\small$\mathsf{t}$}\}

\displaystyle=\&\{l_{1}:?(\mathsf{str}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}!\langle\mathsf{int}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}S,\ l_{2}:\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}\},

and we look at the decomposition of the type corresponding to the branch
l_{1}
:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}?(\mathsf{str}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}!\langle\mathsf{int}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}S})}=(?(\mathsf{str}),\ !\langle\mathsf{int}\rangle,\ M^{*})

Now we inspect a few reductions of
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P})}
.
First, we have synchronizations on
c_{1},\ldots,c_{4}
.
This is followed by the application of the exchanged value

{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V}\big)}
to names
r_{1},s_{1}
:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.36,0.54,0.66}\llbracket}P{\color[rgb]{0.36,0.54,0.66}\rrbracket}})}\longrightarrow^{*}

\displaystyle(\nu\,c^{V}_{1}c^{V}_{2})\,\overline{c{{}^{V}_{1}}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c{{}^{V}_{1}}?().{s_{1}}?(y).\overline{c{{}^{V}_{2}}}!\langle y\rangle\;|\;c{{}^{V}_{2}}?(y).{r_{1}}\triangleright\{l_{1}:x_{r_{1}}!\langle W\rangle,\ l_{2}:\mathbf{0}\}

\displaystyle\;|\;\overline{s}_{1}!\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V}\big)}\rangle\;|\;\overline{c_{5}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}5}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}\big)}=D_{1}

Then, after synchronizations on
c^{V}_{1},s_{1}
, and
c^{V}_{2}
in
D_{1}

we have the following:

\displaystyle D_{1}\longrightarrow^{*}

\displaystyle(\nu\,c_{6},\ldots,c_{12})\,{r_{1}}\triangleright\{l_{1}:{r_{1}}!\langle W\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small${\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V}\big)}$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\}\rangle,\ l_{2}:\mathbf{0}\}\;|\;

\displaystyle(\nu\,r_{2}:?(\mathsf{str}),\ r_{3}:!\langle\mathsf{int}\rangle,\ r_{4}:{M^{*}})\,

\displaystyle\qquad\overline{r}_{1}\triangleleft l_{1}.\overline{c_{6}}!\langle\rangle.\overline{r}_{1}?(y).y\,{(r_{2},r_{3},r_{4})}\;|\;

\displaystyle\qquad c_{6}?().\overline{r}_{2}!\langle\text{``Hello''}\rangle.\overline{c_{7}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{7}?().\overline{r}_{3}?(t).\overline{c_{11}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;

\displaystyle\qquad\quad(\nu\,r_{5}:?(\mathsf{str}),\ r_{6}:!\langle\mathsf{int}\rangle,\ r_{7}:{M^{*}})\,

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\quad c_{8}?().\overline{r}_{4}\triangleleft l_{1}.\overline{c_{12}}!\langle\rangle.\overline{r}_{4}?(y).y\,{(r_{5},r_{6},r_{7})}\;|\;c_{9}?().\overline{r}_{5}!\langle\text{``World''}\rangle.\overline{c_{10}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\quad c_{10}?().\overline{r}_{6}?(t).\overline{c_{11}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{11}?().\overline{r}_{7}\triangleleft l_{2}.\overline{c_{12}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{12}?()=D_{2}

D_{2}
can mimic a silent select action on
r_{1}
; this is followed
by a reception of value
W\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small${\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V}\big)}$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\}
on name
\overline{r}_{1}
,
which is then applied to names
(r_{2},r_{3},r_{4})
. The resulting
process is as follows:

\displaystyle D_{2}\longrightarrow^{*}

\displaystyle(\nu\,c_{8},\ldots,c_{12})\,(\nu\,r_{2}:?(\mathsf{str}),\ r_{3}:!\langle\mathsf{int}\rangle,\ r_{4}:{M^{*}})\,

\displaystyle\qquad(\nu\,c^{W}_{1}\ldots c^{W}_{5})\,\overline{c{{}^{W}_{1}}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c{{}^{W}_{1}}?().r_{2}?(t).\overline{c{{}^{W}_{2}}}!\langle t\rangle\;|\;c{{}^{W}_{2}}?(t).r_{3}!\langle\mathtt{len}(t)\rangle.\overline{c{{}^{W}_{3}}}!\langle\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad\;|\;(\nu\,s_{1})\,(c{{}^{W}_{3}}?().\overline{c{{}^{W}_{4}}}!\langle\rangle.\overline{c{{}^{W}_{5}}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c{{}^{W}_{4}}?().{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V}\big)}\,{(r_{4},\overline{s}_{1})}\;|\;c{{}^{W}_{5}}?().s_{1}!\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V}\big)}\rangle)\;|\;

\displaystyle\qquad\overline{r}_{2}!\langle\text{``Hello''}\rangle.\overline{c_{7}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{7}?().\overline{r}_{3}?(t).\overline{c_{11}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;

\displaystyle\qquad\quad(\nu\,r_{5}:?(\mathsf{str}),\ r_{6}:!\langle\mathsf{int}\rangle,\ r_{7}:{M^{*}})\,

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\quad c_{8}?().\overline{r}_{4}\triangleleft l_{1}.\overline{c_{12}}!\langle\rangle.\overline{r}_{4}?(y).y\,{(r_{5},r_{6},r_{7})}\;|\;c_{9}?().\overline{r}_{5}!\langle\text{``World''}\rangle.\overline{c_{10}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\quad c_{10}?().\overline{r}_{6}?(t).\overline{c_{11}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{11}?().\overline{r}_{7}\triangleleft l_{2}.\overline{c_{12}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{12}?()=D_{3}

The next interesting process emerges once silent actions on
r
are mimicked by

r_{2}
and
r_{3}
:

\displaystyle D_{3}\longrightarrow^{*}

\displaystyle(\nu\,c_{8},\ldots,c_{12})\,(\nu\,r_{4}:{M^{*}})\,(\nu\,s_{1})\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V}\big)}\,{(r_{4},s_{1})}\;|\;\overline{s}_{1}!\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V}\big)}\rangle)\;|\;

\displaystyle\qquad(\nu\,r_{5}:?(\mathsf{str}),\ r_{6}:!\langle\mathsf{int}\rangle,\ r_{7}:{M^{*}})\,

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\quad c_{8}?().\overline{r}_{4}\triangleleft l_{1}.\overline{c_{12}}!\langle\rangle.\overline{r}_{4}?(y).y\,{(r_{5},r_{6},r_{7})}\;|\;c_{9}?().\overline{r}_{5}!\langle\text{``World''}\rangle.\overline{c_{10}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\quad c_{10}?().\overline{r}_{6}?(t).\overline{c_{11}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{11}?().\overline{r}_{7}\triangleleft l_{2}.\overline{c_{12}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{12}?()=D_{4}

In
D_{4}
, name
r_{4}
with type
M^{*}
, is applied to the abstraction
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V}\big)}
, which encapsulates a “new instance” of the recursive branch process. After application, we obtain the following process:

\displaystyle D_{4}\longrightarrow^{*}

\displaystyle(\nu\,c_{8},\ldots,c_{12})\,(\nu\,r_{4}:{M^{*}})\,(\nu\,s_{1})\,(\nu\,c^{V}_{1}c^{V}_{2})\,\overline{c{{}^{V}_{1}}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c{{}^{V}_{1}}?().s_{1}?(y).\overline{c{{}^{V}_{2}}}!\langle y\rangle\;|\;

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad c{{}^{V}_{2}}?(y).r_{4}\triangleright\{l_{1}:r_{4}!\langle V_{1}\rangle,\ l_{2}:\mathbf{0}\}\;|\;s_{1}!\langle{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V}\big)}\rangle)\;|\;

\displaystyle\qquad(\nu\,r_{5}:?(\mathsf{str}),\ r_{6}:!\langle\mathsf{int}\rangle,\ r_{7}:{M^{*}})\,

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\quad c_{8}?().\overline{r}_{4}\triangleleft l_{1}.\overline{c_{12}}!\langle\rangle.\overline{r}_{4}?(y).y\,{(r_{5},r_{6},r_{7})}\;|\;c_{9}?().\overline{r}_{5}!\langle\text{``World''}\rangle.\overline{c_{10}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\quad c_{10}?().\overline{r}_{6}?(t).\overline{c_{11}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{11}?().\overline{r}_{7}\triangleleft l_{2}.\overline{c_{12}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{12}?()=D_{5}

Thus, we can see that after
few administrative reductions (on
c^{V}_{1}
,
s_{1}
, and
c^{V}_{2}
)
the process is able to mimic the a next selection on
r

on name
r_{4}
.
As the process again selects
l_{1}
, we can see that the
next selection will occur on name
r_{7}
, again
typed with
M^{*}
.

\lhd

We would like to finish this subsection with the following remark.
So far we have only considered recursive types which did not contain any actions between recursion and branching/selection.
However, types with prefixed branching

\mu\mathsf{t}.\alpha_{1}.\ldots.\alpha_{n}.\&\{l_{i}:S_{i}\}_{i\in I},

where
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}
are some session prefixes, can also be accommodated in
the same framework, as these types can be written equivalently without prefixed branching:

\alpha_{1}.\ldots.\alpha_{n}.\mu\mathsf{t}.\&\{l_{i}:S_{i}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\alpha_{1}....\alpha_{n}.\mathsf{t}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\mathsf{t}$}\}\}_{i\in I}.

6.3
Adapting Operational Correspondence

We briefly remark on how to adapt the operational correspondence result from

Section

4
.
For the operational correspondence result, and the related lemmas, we must enforce additional constraints on the processes that we break down.
These concerns arise from the following fact.
When a type
\&\{l_{i}:S_{i}\}_{i\in I}
is broken down as

{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\&\{l_{i}:S_{i}\}_{i\in I}})}=\&\{l_{i}:!\langle{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S_{i}})}\!\multimap\!\diamond\rangle\}_{i\in I},

an additional action gets introduced on the level of MST processes.
After performing the branching, an abstraction needs to be sent out.
This additional action will be matched by a corresponding abstraction-input action on the side of selection, if present.
However, this abstraction-sending action does not correspond to any action of the source process.

Therefore, to show the operational correspondence between the source term and its decomposition, we need to restrict our attention to processes in which branching and selection types are both present in (matching) pairs.
Specifically, we assume the following conditions on the source process
P
:

•

P
is a well-typed, that is
\Gamma;\Delta;\Lambda\vdash P\triangleright\diamond
with
\mathsf{balanced}(\Delta)
;

•

for any name
u
,
u\in\mathtt{fn}(P)
with
u:S
such that
S
involves
selection or branching constructs if and only if

\overline{u}\in\mathtt{fn}(P)
.

Intuitively, these two conditions ensure that
every branching action in
P
has its complement (and vice-versa).
Note that for closed typeable processes both the balancedness condition and the second condition on names are vacuously true.

With this condition in place, we need to extend the relation
\mathbin{\mathcal{S}}
in order to account for silent actions that are introduced by the breakdown of selection and branching constructs.
That is, when matching the original silent action involving selection/branching, the corresponding broken down process need to perform several silent actions, in order to be able to mimic the process continuation.

7
Related Work

Here we discuss the positioning of our fragment of minimal session types with respect to (i) other type systems for the
\pi
-calculus; (ii) prior comparisons between session types and other type systems; and (iii) Parrow’s seminal work on trio decompositions of untyped processes.

Other type systems for the
\pi
-calculus

The syntax of minimal session types (
Definition

3.1
) contain constructs already known in the literature: while our types

!\langle U\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}
and
?(U){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}
correspond, respectively, to the linear types
!^{1}[U]
and
?^{1}[U]
in the linearly-typed
\pi
-calculus by Kobayashi et al.
[
17
]
(where ‘
1
’ indicates a linear multiplicity), our recursive (non-linear) types

\mu\mathsf{t}.!\langle U\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathsf{t}
and
\mu\mathsf{t}.?(U){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathsf{t}
are reminiscent of the unlimited types in
[
17
]
(i.e., types with multiplicity
\omega
) and correspond to the recursive sortings in the simply-typed
\pi
-calculus of Pierce and Sangiorgi
[
22
]
. We find it insightful that our identified fragment of session types for
\mathsf{HO}
contains known forms of types, with a decomposition function that satisfies static and dynamic correctness properties; this means that minimal session types stand between simple and linear types and standard session types.

Comparisons between session types and other type systems

Our approach is broadly related to works that relate session types with other type systems
for the
\pi
-calculus (cf.
[
16
,
5
,
6
,
7
,
9
]
).
As such, these works establish formal relationships between two
different type systems
; instead, here we relate the session type system for
\mathsf{HO}
with its
own fragment
based on minimal session types.

Most related to our developments are formal connections between session types and linear types developed by Kobayashi
[
16
]
and Dardha et al.
[
5
,
6
]
.
Kobayashi
[
16
]
shows how to encode a finite session
\pi
-calculus into
a
\pi
-calculus with linear types with usages (but without sequencing);
Dardha et al.
[
5
,
6
]
establish the properties of Kobayashi’s encoding.
This approach relies on two encodings, one for processes and one for types: the former uses a freshly generated linear name to mimic each session action (thereby codifying a session name using multiple linear channels); the latter codifies sequencing in sessions by nesting payload types, relying on linear types extended with variant types.

The difference between the Kobayashi-Dardha et al.’s encoding and our trio-based decomposition is that we “slice” the
n
actions occurring in a session
s:S
along indexed names
s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}
—
n
slices of
S
—with each indexed name characterized by a minimal session type.
Hence, while their encoding could be characterized as codifying sequencing in a “dynamic style”, via the freshly generated names, we follow a “static style” using names that are indexed according to the corresponding session type.

MSTs and Parrow’s trios

We draw inspiration from prior work by Parrow
[
21
]
, who showed that every process
in the untyped, summation-free
\pi
-calculus with replication is weakly bisimilar to its decomposition into trios (i.e.,
P\approx{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P})}
).
As already mentioned, our technical setting is different: our decomposition treats processes from a calculus without name-passing but with higher-order concurrency (abstraction-passing), supports recursive types, and can accommodate labeled choices.
Our goals are also different:
for us, trios are a relevant instrument for defining processes typable with minimal session types, but they are not an end in themselves.
Still, we retain the definitional style and terminology for trios introduced by Parrow
[
21
]
, which are elegant and clear.

Our main results connect the typability and the behavior of a process with its decomposition, as witnessed by the static and dynamic correctness theorems.
Static correctness was not considered by Parrow, as he worked in an untyped setting.
As for dynamic correctness, a similar result was established in
[
21
]
, linking the process and its decomposition through weak bisimilarity.
In our setting we had to use a different, typed notion of bisimilarity.
A challenge here is that known notions of typed bisimilarity for session-typed processes, such as those given by Kouzapas et al.
[
19
]
, only relate processes typed under the
same
typing environments.
To that extent, our notion of equivalence (MST bisimulation) is more flexible than prior related notions as it (i) relates processes typable under different environments (e.g.,
\Delta
and
{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta})}
) and (ii) admits that actions along a name
s
from
P
can be matched by
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P})}
using actions along indexed names
s_{k}
, for some
k
(and viceversa).

Other related works

Jacobs
[
15
]
developed a small programming calculus with a single fork-like construct and a linear type system, which can be used to encode session-typed communications.
His system can be seen as a distillation of Wadler’s GV
[
24
]
which is, in essence, a
\lambda
-calculus with session-based concurrency; in contrast,
\mathsf{HO}
can be seen as a
\pi
-calculus in which abstractions can be exchanged.
While similar in spirit, our work and the developments by Jacobs are technically distant; we observe that the operational correspondences in
[
15
]
are strictly simpler than our dynamic correspondence result (
Theorem

4.1
) although they are mechanized in the Rocq proof assistant.

Finally, we elaborate further on our choice of
\mathsf{HO}
as source language for minimal session types.

\mathsf{HO}
is one of the sub-calculi of
\mathsf{HO}\pi
, a higher-order process calculus with recursion and both name- and abstraction-passing.
The basic theory of
\mathsf{HO}\pi
was studied by Kouzapas et al.
[
18
,
19
]
as a hierarchy of session-typed calculi based on relative expressiveness.
Our results enable us to place
\mathsf{HO}
with minimal session types firmly within this hierarchy.
Still, the definition of minimal session types does not rely on having
\mathsf{HO}
as source language, as they can be defined on top of other process languages.
In fact, in separate work we have
defined minimal session types on top of the first-order sub-calculus of
\mathsf{HO}\pi

[
2
]
.
This development attests that minimal session types admit meaningful formulations independently from the kind of communicated objects
(abstractions or names).

8
Concluding Remarks

We have studied
minimal session types
, a fragment of session types, one of the most studied classes of behavioral types for message-passing programs.
This fragment makes a very limited use of sequencing at the level of types; as such, it stands between linear types and standard session types.
We relate standard and minimal session types through a
decomposition
of session-typed processes, adopting the higher-order process calculus
\mathsf{HO}
as target language.
Following Parrow
[
21
]
, we defined the decomposition of a process
P
, denoted
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P})}
, as a collection of
trios
(processes with three sequential actions) that trigger each other mimicking the sequencing in the original process.
We proved that typability of
P
using standard session types implies the typability of
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P})}
in the minimal fragment; we also established that
{P}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P})}
are behaviorally equivalent through an MST bisimulation.
Our results hold for all session types constructs, including labeled choices and recursive types.

From a foundational standpoint, our study of minimal session types is a conceptual contribution to the theory of behavioral types, in that we study the status of sequencing in theories of session types.
There are many session types variants, and their expressivity often comes at the price of an involved underlying theory.
Our work contributes in the opposite direction, as we identified a simple fragment of an existing session-typed framework
[
18
,
19
]
that is quite expressive. Understanding further the underlying theory of minimal session types (e.g., notions such as type-based compatibility) is an exciting direction for future work.

As mentioned above, one insight derived from our results is that sequencing in session types is convenient but not indispensable.
Convenience is an important factor in the design of type systems for message-passing programs, because types are abstract specifications of communication structures.
By identifying sequencing as a source of redundancy, our minimal formulation of session types does not contradict or invalidate the prior work on standard session types and their extensions; rather, it contributes to our understanding of the sources of convenience of those advanced type systems.

In formulating minimal session types we have committed to a specific notion of minimality, tied to sequencing constructs in types—arguably the most distinctive feature in session types.
There could be other notions of minimality, unrelated to sequencing but worth exploring nevertheless.
Consider, for instance,
context-free
session types
[
23
]
, which extend standard session types by allowing sequencing of the form
S;T
.
This form of sequential composition is quite powerful, and yet it could be seen as achieving a form of minimality different from the one we studied here: as illustrated in
[
23
, Section 5]
, context-free session types allow to describe the communication of tree-structured data while minimizing the need for channel creation and avoiding channel passing.

Our work can also be seen as a new twist on Parrow’s decomposition results in the
untyped
setting
[
21
]
.
While Parrow’s work indeed does not consider types, in fairness we must observe that when Parrow’s work appeared (1996) the study of types (and typed behavioral equivalences) for the
\pi
-calculus was rather incipient (for instance, the widely known formulation of binary session types, given in
[
12
]
, appeared in 1998).
That said, we would like to stress that our results are not merely an extension of Parrow’s work with session types, for types in our setting drastically narrow down the range of conceivable decompositions.
Additionally, in this work we exploit features not supported in
[
21
]
, most notably higher-order concurrency (cf.
Section

5
).

Finally, from a practical standpoint, we believe that our
approach paves a new avenue to the integration of session types in programming
languages whose type systems lack sequencing, such as Go. It is natural to
envision program analysis tools which, given a message-passing program that
should conform to protocols specified as session types, exploit our
decomposition as an intermediate step in the verification of communication
correctness. Remarkably, our decomposition lends itself naturally to an
implementation—in fact, we generated our examples automatically using
MISTY
,
an associated artifact written in Haskell
[
4
]
.

Acknowledgments

We are grateful to Erik Voogd, who as a BSc student was one of the authors in the conference version of this paper
[
3
]
.
We also thank the anonymous reviewers of previous versions of this paper for their comments and suggestions.

Remark A.1
.

We derive polyadic rules for typing
\mathsf{HO}\pi
as the expected extension of the typing rules for
\mathsf{HO}
:

PolyVar

\Gamma,\widetilde{x}:\widetilde{U}_{x};\widetilde{y}:\widetilde{U}_{y};\emptyset\vdash\widetilde{x}\widetilde{y}:\widetilde{U}_{x}\widetilde{U}_{y}

PolySess

\Gamma;\emptyset;\widetilde{u}:\widetilde{S}\vdash\widetilde{u}\triangleright\widetilde{S}

\Gamma;\Lambda_{1};\Delta,u:S\vdash P\triangleright\diamond

\Gamma;\Lambda_{2};\emptyset\vdash\widetilde{x}\triangleright\widetilde{U}

PolyRcv

\Gamma\setminus\widetilde{x};\Lambda_{1}\setminus\Lambda_{2};\Delta,u:?(\widetilde{U}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}S\vdash u?(\widetilde{x}).P\triangleright\diamond

u:S\in\Delta

\Gamma;\Lambda_{1};\Delta\vdash P

\Gamma;\Lambda_{2};\emptyset\vdash\widetilde{x}\triangleright\widetilde{U}

PolySend

\Gamma;\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2};(\Delta\setminus u:S),u:!\langle\widetilde{U}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}S\vdash u!\langle\widetilde{x}\rangle.P

\leadsto\in\{\multimap,\rightarrow\}

\Gamma;\Lambda;\Delta_{1}\vdash V\triangleright\widetilde{C}\leadsto\diamond

\Gamma;\emptyset;\Delta_{2}\vdash\widetilde{u}\triangleright\widetilde{C}

PolyApp

\Gamma;\Lambda;\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}\vdash V\,{\widetilde{u}}

\Gamma;\Lambda;\Delta_{1}\vdash P\triangleright\diamond

\Gamma;\emptyset;\Delta_{2}\vdash\widetilde{x}\triangleright\widetilde{C}

PolyAbs

\Gamma\setminus\widetilde{x};\Lambda;\Delta_{1}\setminus\Delta_{2}\vdash\lambda\widetilde{x}.\,P\triangleright\widetilde{C}\!\multimap\!\diamond

\Gamma,\widetilde{a}:\widetilde{\langle U\rangle};\Lambda;\Delta\vdash P

PolyRes

\Gamma;\Lambda;\Delta\vdash(\nu\,\widetilde{a})\,{P}

\Gamma;\Lambda;\Delta,\widetilde{s}:\widetilde{S}_{1},\overline{\widetilde{s}}:\widetilde{S}_{2}\vdash P

\widetilde{S}_{1}\ \ \mathsf{dual}\ \ \widetilde{S}_{2}

PolyResS

\Gamma;\Lambda;\Delta\vdash(\nu\,\widetilde{s})\,{P}

Lemma A.1

(Substitution Lemma
[
18
]
)
.

\Gamma;\Lambda;\Delta,x:S\vdash P\triangleright\diamond

and
u\notin\text{dom}(\Gamma,\Lambda,\Delta)
imply

\Gamma;\Lambda;\Delta,u:S\vdash P\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$u$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\}\triangleright\diamond
.

Lemma A.2

(Weakening, shared environment)
.

If
\Gamma;\Lambda;\Delta\vdash P\triangleright\diamond

then

\Gamma,x:C\!\rightarrow\!\diamond;\Lambda;\Delta\vdash P\triangleright\diamond
and

\Gamma,u:\langle U\rangle;\Lambda;\Delta\vdash P\triangleright\diamond
.

Lemma A.3

(Strengthening, shared environment)
.

The following hold:

•

If
\Gamma;\Lambda;\Delta\vdash P\triangleright\diamond
and

x\notin\mathtt{fv}(P)
then
\Gamma\setminus x;\Lambda;\Delta\vdash P\triangleright\diamond
.

•

If
\Gamma;\Lambda;\Delta\vdash P\triangleright\diamond
and

u\notin\mathtt{fn}(P)
then
\Gamma\setminus u;\Lambda;\Delta\vdash P\triangleright\diamond
.

C.1
Proof of
4.3

See
4.3

Proof.

By the induction on the structure of
P_{1}
. We consider two base cases:

•

Case
P_{1}=\mathbf{0}
.
Let
\widetilde{B}
such that
\widetilde{W}\boxtimes\widetilde{B}
.
Then, the elements of
\mathcal{C}^{\tilde{W}}_{\tilde{x}}\big(\mathbf{0}\big)
are

R_{1}=(\nu\,c_{k})\,\overline{c_{k}}!\langle\widetilde{B}\rangle\;|\;c_{k}?(\widetilde{x}).\mathbf{0}

and
R_{2}=\mathbf{0}
. Clearly,
R_{1}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\tau}}R_{2}
.

•

Case
P_{1}=V_{1}\,{(\widetilde{r},u)}
.
Let
n=|\widetilde{r}|
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{\tilde{W}}_{\tilde{x}}\big(P_{1}\big)=N_{1}\cup N_{4}\cup\big(\cup_{1\leq l\leq n}N^{l}_{2}\big)\cup\big(\cup_{1\leq l\leq n-1}N^{l}_{3}\big)

where

\displaystyle N_{1}

\displaystyle=\{\mathcal{R}_{\tilde{v},\tilde{r}}\;|\;\overline{c_{k}}!\langle\widetilde{B}\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{x}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{1}}\big)}:\widetilde{W}\boxtimes\widetilde{B}\}

\displaystyle N^{l}_{2}

\displaystyle=\{\mathcal{R}_{\tilde{v},r_{l},\ldots,r_{n}}\;|\;\overbracket{c^{r_{l}}!\big\langle\lambda\widetilde{z}_{l}.c^{r_{l+1}}!\langle\lambda\widetilde{z}_{l+1}.\cdots.c^{r_{n}}!\langle\lambda\widetilde{z}_{n}.}^{|\tilde{r}|-l+1}Q^{V_{2}}_{l}\rangle\,\rangle\big\rangle:V_{1}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}\boxtimes V_{2}\}

\displaystyle N^{l}_{3}

\displaystyle=\{\mathcal{R}_{\tilde{v},r_{l+1},\ldots,r_{n}}\;|\;\lambda\widetilde{z}_{l}.\overbracket{c^{r_{l+1}}!\langle\lambda\widetilde{z}_{l+1}.\cdots.c^{r_{n}}!\langle\lambda\widetilde{z}_{n}.}^{|\tilde{r}|-l}Q^{V_{2}}_{l}\rangle\,\big\rangle\ {\widetilde{r}_{l}}:V_{1}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}\boxtimes V_{2}\}

\displaystyle N_{4}

\displaystyle=\{\mathcal{R}_{\tilde{v}}\;|\;V_{2}\,{(\widetilde{r}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{r}_{n},\widetilde{m})}:V_{1}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}\boxtimes V_{2}\}

with

\displaystyle Q^{V_{2}}_{l}=V_{2}\,{(\widetilde{r}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{r}_{l-1},\widetilde{z}_{l},\ldots,\widetilde{z}_{n},\widetilde{m})}

We can see that for
R_{1}\in N_{1}
there exist
R^{0}_{2}\in N^{0}_{2}
such that

R_{1}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\tau}}R^{0}_{2}
. Further, we observe that for
R^{l}_{2}\in N^{l}_{2}

there is
R^{l}_{3}\in N^{l}_{3}
such that
R^{l}_{2}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\tau}}R^{l}_{3}
. Now, we can
see that for
R^{l}_{3}\in N^{l}_{3}
there is
R^{l+1}_{2}\in N^{l+1}_{2}
such that

R^{l}_{3}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\tau}}R^{l+1}_{2}
. Finally, we have for

R^{n}_{3}\in N^{n}_{3}=\{\mathcal{R}_{\tilde{v}}\;|\;\lambda\widetilde{z}_{n}.\,Q^{V_{2}}_{n}\,{\widetilde{r}_{n}}:V_{1}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}\boxtimes V_{2}\}

there is
R_{4}\in N_{4}
such that
R^{n}_{3}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\tau}}R_{4}
.

We consider two inductive cases as remaining cases are similar:

•

Case
P_{1}=u_{i}!\langle V_{1}\rangle.P_{2}
.
We distinguish two sub-case: (i)
\neg\mathsf{tr}(u_{i})

and (ii)
\mathsf{tr}(u_{i})
.
In both sub-cases, we distinguish two kinds of an object value
V_{1}
:
(a)
V_{1}\equiv x
, such that

\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$V_{x}$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\}\in\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}

and (b)

, that is

is a pure abstraction.

First, we consider sub-case (i).

\widetilde{y}=\mathtt{fv}(V_{1})
,
\widetilde{w}=\mathtt{fv}(P_{2})
,
\widetilde{W}_{1}
, and
\widetilde{W}_{2}
such that

.

\widetilde{v}=\mathtt{rn}(P_{1}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\})
and

.

\mathcal{C}^{-}_{-}\big(-\big)

),

\displaystyle=\{\mathcal{R}_{\tilde{v}}\;|\;u_{i}!\langle V_{2}\rangle.\overline{c_{k}}!\langle\widetilde{B}_{2}\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k_{2}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{w}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{2}\sigma}\big)}:V_{1}\sigma\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\widetilde{W}_{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\widetilde{y}$}\}\boxtimes V_{2},\ \widetilde{W}_{2}\boxtimes\widetilde{B}_{2}\}

, a variable or a pure abstraction,

we have

, such that

,
that by a
\tau
-move substitutes

in

.
Thus, by

x\sigma\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}_{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{y}$}\}=V_{x}
we have

V_{1}\sigma\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}_{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{y}$}\}\boxtimes V_{2}
.
In the sub-case (b), by definition of
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-}\big)}

we have that
by a
\tau
-move
\widetilde{B}_{1}\subseteq\widetilde{B}

substitute
\widetilde{y}
in
{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{y}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V_{1}\sigma}\big)}
so we have

R_{1}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\tau}}R_{2}
where

V_{2}={\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{y}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V_{1}\sigma}\big)}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{B}_{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{y}$}\}

Now, we may notice that by
Table

3
we have

\displaystyle V_{2}\in\mathcal{C}^{\tilde{W}_{1}}_{\tilde{y}}\big(V_{1}\sigma\big)

Hence, by
Definitions

4.11
and
4.13
we have

V_{1}\sigma\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}_{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{y}$}\}\boxtimes V_{2}
.
Further, we may notice that there is no

\tau
-transition involving non-essential prefixes in
R_{2}
.
This concludes this sub-case.

Now, we consider sub-case (ii).
Let
\widetilde{y}=\mathtt{fv}(V_{1})
,
\widetilde{w}=\mathtt{fv}(P_{2})
,
\widetilde{W}_{1}
, and
\widetilde{W}_{2}
such that

\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\widetilde{W}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\widetilde{x}$}\}=\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\widetilde{W}_{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\widetilde{y}$}\}\cdot\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\widetilde{W}_{2}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\widetilde{w}$}\}
.
Then, we have

\mathcal{C}^{\tilde{W}}_{\tilde{x}}\big(P_{1}\big)=N_{1}\cup N_{2}\cup N_{3}\cup N_{4}

where

\displaystyle N_{1}

\displaystyle=\{\mathcal{R}_{\tilde{v}}\;|\;\overline{c_{k}}!\langle\widetilde{B}\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{x}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{1}}\big)}:\widetilde{W}\boxtimes\widetilde{B}\}

\displaystyle N_{2}

\displaystyle=\{\mathcal{R}_{\tilde{v}}\;|\;c^{u}!\big\langle M^{\tilde{B}_{2}}_{V_{2}}\big\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{w}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{2}}\big)}:V_{1}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}_{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{y}$}\}\boxtimes V_{2},\ \widetilde{W}_{2}\boxtimes\widetilde{B}_{2}\}

\displaystyle N_{3}

\displaystyle=\{\mathcal{R}_{\tilde{v}\setminus u}\;|\;M^{\tilde{B}_{2}}_{V_{2}}\,{\widetilde{u}}\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{w}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}\big)}:V_{1}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}_{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{y}$}\}\boxtimes V_{2},\ \widetilde{W}_{2}\boxtimes\widetilde{B}_{2}\}

\displaystyle N_{4}

\displaystyle=\{\mathcal{R}_{\tilde{v}\setminus u}\;|\;{u_{{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S}\rangle}}!\big\langle V_{2}\big\rangle.}{}\overline{c_{k}}!\langle\widetilde{B}_{2}\rangle.c^{u}?(b).(b\,{\widetilde{u}})\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{w}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{2}}\big)}:V_{1}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}_{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{y}$}\}\boxtimes V_{2},\ \widetilde{W}_{2}\boxtimes\widetilde{B}_{2}\}

where

\displaystyle M^{\tilde{B}_{2}}_{V_{2}}=\lambda\widetilde{z}.\,z_{{\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S}\rangle}}!\big\langle V_{2}\big\rangle.{}\overline{c_{k}}!\langle\widetilde{B}_{2}\rangle\;|\;c^{u}?(b).(b\,{\widetilde{z}})

As in the previous sub-case, we can see that in both cases of
V_{1}
, a
variable or a pure abstraction, we have that for
R_{1}\in N_{1}
there is

R_{2}\in N_{2}
such that
R_{1}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\tau}}R_{2}
,
for appropriate choice
of
\widetilde{B}
,
\widetilde{B}_{2}
, and
k
.
Similarly, by the communication
on shared name
c^{u}
for
R_{2}\in N_{2}

there is
R_{3}\in N_{3}
such that
R_{2}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\tau}}R_{3}
.
Finally, for
R_{3}\in N_{3}
there is
R_{4}\in N_{4}
such that
R_{3}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\tau}}R_{4}
by the
application. This concludes the output case.

•

Case
P_{1}=Q_{1}\;|\;Q_{2}
.
Let
\widetilde{y}=\mathtt{fv}(Q_{1})
,
\widetilde{w}=\mathtt{fv}(Q_{2})
,
\widetilde{W}_{1}
, and
\widetilde{W}_{2}

such that

\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\widetilde{W}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\widetilde{x}$}\}=\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}_{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{y}$}\}\cdot\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}_{2}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{w}$}\}
.
Then, by definition of
\mathcal{C}^{-}_{-}\big(-\big)
(
Table

3
), we have that

\mathcal{C}^{\tilde{W}}_{\tilde{x}}\big(Q_{1}\;|\;Q_{2}\big)=N_{1}\cup N_{2}\cup N_{3}
where:

\displaystyle N_{1}

\displaystyle=\{\overline{c_{k}}!\langle\widetilde{B}\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{x}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{1}\;|\;Q_{2}}\big)}:\widetilde{W}\boxtimes\widetilde{B}\}

\displaystyle N_{2}

\displaystyle=\{\overline{c_{k}}!\langle\widetilde{B}_{1}\rangle.\overline{c_{k+l}}!\langle\widetilde{B}_{2}\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{y}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{1}}\big)}\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k+l}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{w}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{2}}\big)}:\widetilde{W}_{1}\boxtimes\widetilde{B}_{1},\ \widetilde{W}_{2}\boxtimes\widetilde{B}_{2}\}

\displaystyle N_{3}

\displaystyle=\{R_{1}\;|\;R_{2}:R_{1}\in\mathcal{C}^{\tilde{W}_{1}}_{\tilde{y}}\big(Q_{1}\big),\ R_{2}\in\mathcal{C}^{\tilde{W}_{2}}_{\tilde{w}}\big(Q_{2}\big)\}

with
l={\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\lbag{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{1}}\rbag}
.

We show that the thesis holds for processes in each of these three sets:

1.

Clearly, by picking
appropriate
\widetilde{B}
,
\widetilde{B}_{1}
, and
\widetilde{B}_{2}
,
for any
R^{1}_{1}\in N_{1}
there is
R^{1}_{2}
such that

R^{1}_{1}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\tau}}R^{1}_{2}
and
R^{1}_{2}\in N_{2}
.

2.

Now, we consider set
N_{3}
.
Let us pick
R^{3}=R_{1}\;|\;R_{2}\in N_{3}
, for some

R_{1}\in\mathcal{C}^{\tilde{W}_{1}}_{\tilde{y}}\big(Q_{1}\big)

and
R_{2}\in\mathcal{C}^{\tilde{W}_{2}}_{\tilde{w}}\big(Q_{2}\big)
.
By the definition of
\mathcal{C}^{-}_{-}\big(-\big)
(
Table

3
) we know
all propagator names are restricted element-wise in
R^{3}
, and so there
is no communication between
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
on propagator prefixes.
This ensures that any
\tau
-actions emanating from
R^{3}
arise from
R_{1}
or
R_{2}
separately, not from their interaction.
The thesis then follows by IH, for we know that if
R_{1}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\tau}}R^{\prime}_{1}
then
R^{\prime}_{1}\in\mathcal{C}^{\tilde{W}_{1}}_{\tilde{y}}\big(Q_{1}\big)
; similarly,
if
R_{2}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\tau}}R^{\prime}_{2}
then
R^{\prime}_{2}\in\mathcal{C}^{\tilde{W}_{2}}_{\tilde{w}}\big(Q_{2}\big)
.
Thus, by the definition
R^{\prime}_{1}\;|\;R^{\prime}_{2}\in N_{3}
.

3.

Finally, we show that for any
R_{2}\in N_{2}
if
R_{2}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\tau}}R^{\prime}_{2}

then
R^{\prime}_{2}\in\mathcal{C}^{\tilde{W}}_{\tilde{x}}\big(Q_{1}\;|\;Q_{2}\big)
. We know that
R_{2}=\overline{c_{k}}!\langle\widetilde{B}_{1}\rangle.\overline{c_{k+l}}!\langle\widetilde{B}_{2}\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{y}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{1}}\big)}\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k+l}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{w}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{2}}\big)}
, where
\widetilde{B}_{i}
are such that
\widetilde{W}_{i}\boxtimes\widetilde{B}_{i}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
. Clearly, by a synchronization on

c_{k}
, we have

R_{2}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\tau}}\overline{c_{k+l}}!\langle\widetilde{B}_{2}\rangle\;|\;B_{Q_{1}}\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k+l}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{w}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{2}}\big)}=R^{\prime}_{2}

where
B_{Q_{1}}
stands for the derivative of

{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{y}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{1}}\big)}
after the synchronization (and substitution of
\widetilde{B}_{1}
).

To show that
R^{\prime}_{2}
is already in
\mathcal{C}^{\tilde{W}}_{\tilde{x}}\big(Q_{1}\;|\;Q_{2}\big)
,
we consider an
R^{3}\in N_{3}
such that

\displaystyle R^{3}=\overline{c_{k}}!\langle\widetilde{B}_{1}\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{y}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{1}}\big)}\;|\;\overline{c_{k+l}}!\langle\widetilde{B}_{2}\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k+l}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{w}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{2}}\big)}

Note that there is a
\tau
-transition on
c_{k}
such that

R^{3}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\tau}}R^{\prime}_{2}
.
Because processes in
N_{3}
satisfy the thesis (cf. the previous
sub-sub-case), we have that

R^{\prime}_{2}\in N_{3}
.
Therefore,
R^{\prime}_{2}\in\mathcal{C}^{\tilde{W}}_{\tilde{x}}\big(Q_{1}\;|\;Q_{2}\big)
, as desired.
This concludes parallel composition case.

This concludes the proof of
4.3
.
∎

C.2
Proof of
4.4

For the proof we will use the following syntactic sugar.

Definition C.1

(Function
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{-}_{-}\big(-\big)
)
.

Let
P
be an
\mathsf{HO}
process,
\rho
be a value substitution, and
\sigma
be an indexed
name substitution. We define
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{\rho}_{\sigma}\big(P\big)
as follows:

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{\rho}_{\sigma}\big(P_{1}\big)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{C}^{\tilde{W}\sigma}_{\tilde{x}}\big(P_{1}\sigma\big)\qquad\quad\text{with }\rho=\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}

See
4.4

Proof.

By transition induction. Let
\rho_{1}=\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}
.
By inversion of
P_{1}\rho_{1}\mathbin{\mathcal{S}}Q_{1}

we know there is
\sigma_{1}\in\mathsf{index}(\mathtt{fn}(P\rho_{1}))

such that
Q_{1}\in\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{\rho_{1}}_{\sigma_{1}}\big(P_{1}\big)
.
Then, we need the following assertion on the index substitution.
If

P_{1}\rho_{1}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\ell}}P_{2}\rho_{2}

and
\mathtt{subj}(\ell)=n
such that
\neg\mathsf{tr}(n)
then
there exists

Q_{2}
such that
Q_{1}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{\breve{\ell}}}Q_{2}

with
\mathtt{subj}(\breve{\ell})=n_{i}
and

Q_{2}\in\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{\rho_{2}}_{\sigma_{2}}\big(P_{2}\big)

such that
\sigma_{2}\in\mathsf{index}(P_{2}\rho_{2})
and
\mathsf{next}(n_{i})\in\sigma_{2}
.

First, we consider three base cases: Rules
Snd
,
Rv
, and

App
. Then, we distinguish five inductive cases and analyze three cases
(as cases
\texttt{Par}_{R}
and
\texttt{Par}_{L}
, and
New
and

Res
are similar).
Thus, in total we consider six cases:

1.

Case
\langle\texttt{Snd}\rangle
.
Then
P_{1}=n!\langle V_{1}\rangle.P_{2}
. We first consider the case when
P_{1}
is not
a trigger collection, and then briefly discuss the case when it is a trigger
collection.

We distinguish two sub-cases: (i)
\neg\mathsf{tr}(n)

and (ii)
\mathsf{tr}(n)
.
In both sub-cases, we distinguish two kinds of an object value
V_{1}
:
(a)
V_{1}\equiv x
, such that

\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$V_{x}$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\}\in\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}

and (b)
V_{1}=\lambda y:C.\,P^{\prime}
, that is
V_{1}
is a pure abstraction.
Next, we consider two sub-cases:

\widetilde{W}_{1}

We have the following transition:

\langle\texttt{Snd}\rangle

Let

\sigma_{1}\in\mathsf{index}(\widetilde{u})
where

\widetilde{u}=\mathtt{fn}(P_{1}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\})
such that

.
Also, let
\sigma_{2}=\sigma_{1}\cdot\mathsf{next}(n_{i})
.
Further, let

\widetilde{v}=\mathtt{rn}(P_{1}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\})

When

\mathbin{\mathcal{S}}
(

\displaystyle=\big\{(\nu\,\widetilde{c}_{r})\,(\nu\,\widetilde{c}_{k})\,\mathcal{R}_{\tilde{v}}\;|\;\overline{c_{k}}!\langle\widetilde{B}\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{x}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{1}\sigma_{1}}\big)}:\widetilde{W}\boxtimes\widetilde{B}\big\}

\displaystyle N_{2}

\displaystyle=\big\{(\nu\,\widetilde{c}_{r})\,(\nu\,\widetilde{c}_{k+1})\,\mathcal{R}_{\tilde{v}}\;|\;n_{i}!\langle V_{2}\rangle.\overline{c_{k+1}}!\langle\widetilde{B}_{2}\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k+1}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{w}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{2}\sigma_{2}}\big)}:V_{1}\sigma\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\widetilde{W}_{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\widetilde{y}$}\}\boxtimes V_{2},\ \widetilde{W}_{2}\boxtimes\widetilde{B}_{2}\big\}

Q_{1}\in N_{1}
,

\widetilde{c}_{r}\cdot\widetilde{c}_{k+1}\cap\mathtt{fn}(n_{i}!\langle V_{2}\rangle)=\emptyset

\langle\texttt{New}\rangle

(167)

where

Then, we shall show the following:

\displaystyle({P_{2}\parallel t\hookleftarrow_{\mathtt{H}}V_{1}})\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}\penalty\ \mathbin{\mathcal{S}}\penalty\ (\nu\,\widetilde{c}_{r_{2}})\,(Q^{\prime}_{2}\parallel t_{1}\hookleftarrow_{\mathtt{H}}V_{2})

(168)

By assumption that

is well-typed,
we know

\displaystyle(\nu\,\widetilde{c}_{r_{2}})\,(Q^{\prime}_{2}\parallel t_{1}\hookleftarrow_{\mathtt{H}}V_{2})

\displaystyle\qquad\equiv(\nu\,\widetilde{c}_{r_{1}})\,(\nu\,\widetilde{c}_{r_{2}})\,{(\nu\,\widetilde{c}_{k+1})(\mathcal{R}_{\tilde{v}_{1}}\;|\;\overline{c_{k+1}}!\langle\widetilde{B}\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k+1}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{w}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{2}\sigma_{1}}\big)}\;|\;\mathcal{R}_{\tilde{v}_{2}}\parallel t_{1}\hookleftarrow_{\mathtt{H}}V_{2})}

\displaystyle\qquad\equiv(\nu\,\widetilde{c}_{r})\,{(\nu\,\widetilde{c}_{k+1})(\mathcal{R}_{\tilde{v}_{1}}\;|\;\overline{c_{k+1}}!\langle\widetilde{B}\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k+1}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{w}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{2}\sigma_{1}}\big)}\;|\;\mathcal{R}_{\tilde{v}_{2}}\parallel t_{1}\hookleftarrow_{\mathtt{H}}V_{2})}

\displaystyle\qquad=(\nu\,\widetilde{c}_{r})\,(\nu\,\widetilde{c}_{k+1})\,R

From the definition of
\mathbin{\mathcal{S}}
(
Definition

4.17
) we have that
n\not\in\mathtt{fn}(\widetilde{W}_{2})
and thus

\widetilde{W}_{2}\sigma_{1}=\widetilde{W}_{2}\sigma_{2}
.
Thus, by the definition of
\widetilde{B}_{2}
(
\widetilde{W}_{2}\sigma_{2}\boxtimes\widetilde{B}_{2}
)
we can see that

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\tilde{v}_{1}}\;|\;\overline{c_{k+1}}!\langle\widetilde{B}\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k+1}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{w}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{2}\sigma_{1}}\big)}\;|\;\mathcal{R}_{\tilde{v}_{2}}\in\mathcal{C}^{\tilde{W}_{2}\sigma_{2}}_{\tilde{w}}\big(P_{2}\sigma_{2}\big)

(\nu\,\widetilde{m}_{2})\,R\mathrel{\xrightarrow{(\nu\,\widetilde{m}_{2}\cdot\widetilde{n}_{2})\,u_{i}!\langle V_{2}\rangle}}Q_{2}

Therefore, the sub-goal (
202
) follows.
Now, by (
201
)
and by
Definition

4.17
we can infer the following:

(\nu\,\widetilde{n}_{2})\,(Q_{2}\parallel t\hookleftarrow_{\mathtt{H}}V_{2})\equiv(\nu\,\widetilde{c}^{\prime}_{r})\,(\nu\,\widetilde{n})\,(\mathcal{R}_{\tilde{v}}\;|\;R_{2}\;|\;\mathcal{R}_{\tilde{w}}\parallel t\hookleftarrow_{\mathtt{H}}V_{2})

where
\widetilde{v}=\mathtt{rn}(R_{2})
,
\widetilde{w}=\mathtt{rn}(V_{2})
,

\widetilde{c}^{\prime}_{r}=\mathsf{cr}(V_{2})
,
and

(\nu\,\widetilde{n})\,(\mathcal{R}_{\tilde{v}}\;|\;R_{2}\;|\;\mathcal{R}_{\tilde{w}}\parallel t\hookleftarrow_{\mathtt{H}}V_{2})\in\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{\rho_{1}}_{\sigma_{1}}\big((\nu\,\widetilde{n}_{1})\,(P_{2}\parallel t\hookleftarrow_{\mathtt{H}}V_{1})\big)

By this and the definition of
\mathcal{C}^{-}_{-}\big(-\big)
(
Table

3
)
we have

(\nu\,\widetilde{m})\,(\nu\,\tilde{c}^{m})\,(\nu\,\widetilde{n})\,(\mathcal{R}_{\tilde{v}}\;|\;R_{2}\;|\;\mathcal{R}_{\tilde{w}}\parallel t\hookleftarrow_{\mathtt{H}}V_{2})\in\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{\rho_{1}}_{\sigma_{1}}\big((\nu\,m)\,(\nu\,\widetilde{n}_{1})\,(P_{2}\parallel t\hookleftarrow_{\mathtt{H}}V_{1})\big)

Further, we may notice

\displaystyle(\nu\,\widetilde{m}_{2}\cdot\widetilde{n}_{2})\,(Q_{2}\parallel t\hookleftarrow_{\mathtt{H}}V_{2})\equiv(\nu\,\widetilde{c}_{r})\,(\nu\,\widetilde{m}_{2})\,(\nu\,\widetilde{n})\,(\mathcal{R}_{\tilde{v}}\;|\;R_{2}\;|\;\mathcal{R}_{\tilde{w}}\parallel t\hookleftarrow_{\mathtt{H}}V_{2})

Therefore, the sub-goal (
203
) follows.
This concludes case
\langle\texttt{New}\rangle
and the proof of
4.4
.

∎

C.3
Proof of
4.7

See
4.7

Proof (Sketch).

By transition induction.
First, we analyze the case of non-essential prefixes, which induce
\tau
-actions
that do not correspond to actions in
P_{1}
.
This concerns the sub-case (i) of Part 3.
This directly follows by
4.3
, that is by the fact that

\mathcal{C}^{\tilde{W}}_{\tilde{x}}\big(P_{1}\big)
is closed under transitions on
non-essential prefixes.

Now, assume
Q_{1}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\ell}}Q_{2}
when
\ell
is an essential prefix.
This is mainly the converse of the proof of
4.4
noting that
there are no essential actions in
\mathcal{C}^{\tilde{W}}_{\tilde{x}}\big(P_{1}\big)
not matched
in
P_{1}
. We consider only one case:

•

Case
\langle\texttt{Snd}\rangle
. In this case we know
P_{1}=n!\langle V_{1}\rangle.P_{2}
.
We distinguish two sub-cases: (i)
\neg\mathsf{tr}(u_{i})

and (ii)
\mathsf{tr}(u_{i})
.
In both sub-cases, we distinguish two kinds of an object value
V_{1}
:
(a)
V_{1}\equiv x
, such that

\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$V_{x}$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\}\in\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}

and (b)
V_{1}=\lambda y:C.\,P^{\prime}
, that is
V_{1}
is a pure abstraction.
We only consider sub-case (a).

Let
\widetilde{W}_{1}
,
\widetilde{W}_{2}
,
\widetilde{y}
, and
\widetilde{w}
such that

P_{1}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}=n!\langle V_{1}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}_{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{y}$}\}\rangle.P_{2}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}_{2}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{w}$}\}

Let

\sigma_{1}\in\mathsf{index}(\widetilde{u})
where

\widetilde{u}=\mathtt{fn}(P_{1}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\})
such that
\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$n_{i}$}\!/\mbox{\small$n$}\}\in\sigma_{1}
.
Also, let
\sigma_{2}=\sigma_{1}\cdot\mathsf{next}(n_{i})
.
When
P_{1}
is not a
trigger, by the definition of
\mathbin{\mathcal{S}}
(
Table

3
), for both
sub-cases,
we have

Q_{1}\in N_{1}
where:

\displaystyle N_{1}

\displaystyle=\big\{(\nu\,\widetilde{c}_{r})\,(\nu\,\widetilde{c}_{k+1})\,\mathcal{R}_{\tilde{v}}\;|\;n_{i}!\langle V_{2}\rangle.\overline{c_{k+1}}!\langle\widetilde{B}_{2}\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k+1}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{w}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{2}\sigma_{2}}\big)}:V_{1}\sigma\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\widetilde{W}_{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\widetilde{y}$}\}\boxtimes V_{2},\ \widetilde{W}_{2}\boxtimes\widetilde{B}_{2}\big\}

For
Q_{1}\in N_{1}
we have the following transition inference tree:

\langle\texttt{Snd}\rangle

Q_{1}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{n_{i}!\langle V_{1}\rangle}}Q_{2}

where

Q_{2}=(\nu\,\widetilde{c}_{r})\,(\nu\,\widetilde{c}_{k+1})\,\overline{c_{k+1}}!\langle\widetilde{B}_{2}\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}\mathcal{B}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{w}}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{2}\sigma_{2}}\big)}

We have

\langle\texttt{Snd}\rangle

P_{1}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{n!\langle V_{1}\{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{\small$\tilde{W}_{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{y}$}\}\rangle}}P_{2}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{W}_{2}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{w}$}\}

We should show that

{(\nu\,\widetilde{m_{1}})(P_{2}\parallel t\hookleftarrow_{\mathtt{H}}V_{1})}\ \mathbin{\mathcal{S}}\ {(\nu\,\widetilde{m_{2}})(Q_{2}\parallel t_{1}\hookleftarrow_{\mathtt{H}}V_{2})}

This immediately follows by the definition of
\mathbin{\mathcal{S}}

and
Table

3
.
This concludes
Snd
case.

As can be seen the proof of this part is essentially the inverse of
the proof of

4.4
. We just need to show that

\mathcal{C}^{\tilde{W}\sigma}_{\tilde{x}}\big(P_{1}\sigma\big)
does
not introduce extra actions on essential prefixes not present in
P_{1}
.
This is evident by the inspection of the definition of
\mathcal{C}^{-}_{-}\big(-\big)
.
Briefly, only in the case of the input and the output prefix does
\mathcal{J}^{-}_{-}\big(-\big)

introduce actions that mimic those prefixes. The remaining cases only
introduce actions on non-essential prefixes (
\tau
-actions on propagator names).
∎
